NBCE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Loss of gastric intrinsic factor prevents vitamin B12 absorption in the terminal ileum): Parietal cells secrete intrinsic factor, which binds vitamin B12 so the complex is absorbed in the terminal ileum. Autoimmune gastritis destroys these cells and causes pernicious anemia, with macrocytosis and posterior column neuropathy. Folate is absorbed in the proximal jejunum without intrinsic factor, and bile salt loss would affect fat soluble vitamins, not produce this picture.
  2. The correct answer is B (Whether she has had new temporal headache, scalp tenderness, jaw pain on chewing or visual loss): Polymyalgia rheumatica overlaps with giant cell arteritis, which can cause blindness if untreated. A woman over 50 with a markedly raised sedimentation rate should be asked about headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication and visual symptoms. Mechanical or osteoarthritic questions do not address that risk. Positive answers call for urgent medical referral, since corticosteroid treatment should not wait.
  3. The correct answer is C (Side bending of the head to the right with the face rotated to the left): The sternocleidomastoid tilts the head toward its own side and rotates the face to the opposite side. A short right muscle therefore produces a right tilt with the face turned left, the typical torticollis posture. Chin elevation with extension describes bilateral action, not a unilateral contracture.
  4. The correct answer is A (Oligodendrocytes, which produce and maintain central nervous system myelin, with relative sparing of axons early): Multiple sclerosis is an autoimmune demyelinating disease of the central nervous system in which oligodendrocytes are lost, producing plaques disseminated in time and space. Axons are relatively spared early, though they are damaged later. Schwann cells myelinate peripheral nerves and are affected in Guillain-Barre syndrome. Astrocyte injury characterizes neuromyelitis optica, and dopaminergic neuron loss defines Parkinson disease.
  5. The correct answer is D (Interferential stimulation delivered through electrodes placed across the low back and abdomen): Electrical stimulation across the trunk can be sensed by a pacemaker and inhibit or alter its output, so it is contraindicated in this patient. Superficial heat, cold and exercise do not emit current and do not interact with the device. Avoiding them would withdraw safe treatment without any benefit.
  6. The correct answer is A (N-acetylcysteine, which replenishes glutathione and limits liver injury from the toxic metabolite NAPQI): Acetaminophen is metabolized to NAPQI, which glutathione normally neutralizes, and an overdose depletes those stores and injures the liver. A level above the Rumack-Matthew treatment line, drawn from about 4 hours after ingestion, calls for N-acetylcysteine, which restores glutathione. The other antidotes belong to different poisonings.
  7. The correct answer is A (The maxillary division of the trigeminal nerve, cranial nerve V2): The foramen rotundum carries the maxillary division, V2, which gives sensation to the cheek, upper lip and upper teeth. V1 passes through the superior orbital fissure and V3 through the foramen ovale. The facial nerve exits at the stylomastoid foramen and is mainly motor, so it does not explain the numbness.
  8. The correct answer is A (Calcified carotid atheroma, indicating atherosclerosis that warrants carotid duplex ultrasound and vascular risk evaluation): A curvilinear calcification lateral to the C3 and C4 level, near the carotid bifurcation, in an older smoker is a calcified carotid plaque. It marks atherosclerosis and stroke risk, so carotid duplex ultrasound and risk factor management follow. Thyroid cartilage lies lower and more medial, lymph node calcifications are clumped, and the stylohyoid ligament is a thin straight line that runs toward the hyoid.
  9. The correct answer is B (Weight bearing reveals true cartilage loss, and the film should show medial narrowing with varus alignment): Standing films load the joint so cartilage thinning appears as true joint space narrowing, which supine films can hide. With varus alignment the medial compartment bears the load, so medial narrowing, osteophytes, and spiking of the tibial spines are expected. Lateral narrowing goes with valgus, and loading does not open the joint or alter magnification.
  10. The correct answer is C (Change the line of drive to posterior to anterior and slightly inferior.): A contact on the posterior surface of a prone patient can only push forward, so the line of drive must be posterior to anterior, here with a slight inferior component for the lumbar mamillary push. Anterior to posterior would pull away from the contact. The vertebral body cannot be touched from behind, and changing the stabilization hand or switching to the iliac spine does not repair the mismatch.
  11. The correct answer is D (The confluence of septal vessels on the anterior inferior nasal septum, called Little area): The anterior inferior septum holds Kiesselbach plexus, where branches of the anterior ethmoid, sphenopalatine, greater palatine and superior labial arteries anastomose close to the surface. Dry air and digital trauma commonly injure it, and bleeding responds to pressure. Posterior bleeds come from the Woodruff plexus or sphenopalatine branches and are heavier. Anterior ethmoid sites are superior and uncommon.
  12. The correct answer is D (Wolff law: bone adapts its density and trabecular pattern to the mechanical loads placed on it): Wolff law states that bone remodels in proportion to the loads it carries, increasing density and aligning trabeculae along stress lines in the rower and losing mass with disuse in the bed-bound patient. Davis law describes soft tissue adaptation, Hilton law describes joint innervation and the Hueter-Volkmann law concerns growth plates in children. Only Wolff law explains adult bone remodeling.
  13. The correct answer is A (Oxygen accepts electrons at complex IV, and proton flow through ATP synthase powers ATP formation): Electrons from NADH pass through complexes I, III and IV, and molecular oxygen is reduced to water at complex IV as the final acceptor. The pumping of protons across the inner membrane builds an electrochemical gradient. ATP synthase uses the protons flowing back down that gradient to phosphorylate ADP, which is chemiosmotic coupling. Cytochrome c and coenzyme Q are intermediate carriers, and substrate-level phosphorylation occurs in glycolysis and the citric acid cycle instead.
  14. The correct answer is D (Glossopharyngeal and vagus nerves on the right side (cranial nerves IX and X)): The gag reflex has a sensory limb carried by the glossopharyngeal nerve and a motor limb carried by the vagus nerve. A weak palate on one side makes the uvula deviate away from the damaged side, so deviation to the left places the lesion on the right. The trigeminal and facial nerves do not form this reflex arc. The hypoglossal and accessory nerves control the tongue and the trapezius and sternocleidomastoid, not the palate.
  15. The correct answer is B (Neurogenic thoracic outlet syndrome, managed with posture correction and scalene stretching): Arm heaviness and paresthesia reproduced by sustained elevation on the Roos test is a sensitive sign of neurogenic thoracic outlet syndrome, and a negative Adson test does not exclude it. Care begins with additional provocative tests, and posture correction, scalene and pectoral stretching and activity modification. Carpal tunnel would not be provoked by shoulder elevation, and normal pulses make a vascular cause unlikely.
  16. The correct answer is A (Abdominal aortic aneurysm, confirmed and sized with abdominal ultrasound): A thin curvilinear calcified line outlining a round soft tissue shadow anterior to the lumbar vertebrae is the calcified wall of an abdominal aortic aneurysm, common in older hypertensive male smokers. Ultrasound is the standard noninvasive way to confirm and measure the diameter. Stones lie lateral over the renal and ureteral paths, and osteophytes arise from the vertebral margins and do not form a curved wall.
  17. The correct answer is C (Stasis of blood flow in the deep veins of the legs): Virchow triad comprises stasis, endothelial injury and hypercoagulability. Sitting for a prolonged time slows venous return, which is stasis. Her estrogen contraceptive represents the hypercoagulable component. No direct vein trauma is described, and thrombocytopenia is not part of the triad.
  18. The correct answer is A (Just above the upper border of the lower rib, away from the costal groove): The intercostal vein, artery and nerve run in the costal groove along the inferior border of each rib. Passing the needle just over the upper border of the rib below keeps it clear of that bundle. Entering under the rib above risks hemorrhage and neuralgia. Staying at the mid-space is not reliable because collateral branches also run there.
  19. The correct answer is A (The superior mesenteric artery, arising from the aorta behind the neck of the pancreas): The superior mesenteric artery arises at L1 behind the pancreatic neck and supplies the midgut from the duodenum to the proximal two thirds of the transverse colon. The celiac trunk supplies the foregut at T12, and the inferior mesenteric artery supplies the hindgut at L3. The renal arteries are paired and supply the kidneys, not bowel.
  20. The correct answer is D (Findings are suspicious for malignancy, so urgent medical referral is indicated before any cervical treatment): Hard, fixed, painless and enlarging nodes, especially in the supraclavicular area with unexplained weight loss, are suspicious for malignancy and call for urgent medical referral. Reactive and infectious nodes are usually soft, mobile and tender and follow an illness. A lipoma is soft and mobile, not fixed and hard. Treating the neck or waiting two weeks would delay evaluation of a possible cancer.
  21. The correct answer is D (Osteosarcoma, with sunburst periosteal reaction and a Codman triangle at the distal femur): Osteosarcoma in a teenager commonly arises at the distal femoral or proximal tibial metaphysis. It produces an aggressive sunburst pattern from spiculated new bone and a Codman triangle where the periosteum is lifted. Ewing sarcoma favors the diaphysis with onion skin layering. Osteomyelitis gives a more uniform periosteal layer, and giant cell tumor occurs at the epiphysis after the physis closes.
  22. The correct answer is A (Activate emergency services immediately for urgent decompression, keeping him upright and monitored until paramedics arrive): Falling blood pressure, distended neck veins, and tracheal deviation away from the silent hemithorax indicate tension pneumothorax, a clinical diagnosis. It is life threatening and needs immediate decompression, so emergency services are called at once. Imaging wastes time, and observation risks cardiac arrest. Binding the chest and lying him flat would worsen the compromise of ventilation and venous return.
  23. The correct answer is D (No documented discussion of risks, benefits, and alternatives, so the signed form is inadequate): Informed consent is a conversation in which the doctor explains the nature of the procedure, material risks, expected benefits, and alternatives, and the patient has a chance to ask questions. The form records this and does not replace it. A witness signature is not the defining element. Consent by attendance is not informed consent. Consent must come before treatment.
  24. The correct answer is B (Fibrous dysplasia, with proximal femoral varus bowing called the shepherd crook deformity): Fibrous dysplasia produces an expansile lesion with a ground glass matrix and a thin sclerotic rim, and in the proximal femur it commonly causes coxa vara with lateral bowing, the shepherd crook deformity. The skin patches suggest McCune-Albright features. Paget disease is rare at this age, a simple bone cyst is lucent without ground glass density, and enchondroma shows rings and arcs calcification.
  25. The correct answer is B (Most carcinomas arise in the upper outer quadrant, whose tissue extends into the axilla): About half of breast carcinomas arise in the upper outer quadrant, and breast tissue continues from that quadrant into the axilla as the axillary tail of Spence, so palpation must include the axilla. The lower inner quadrant is not the common site. Carcinoma rarely begins in the nipple complex, and axillary nodes receive metastasis rather than give origin to the primary tumor.
  26. The correct answer is A (Rotation of the C5 body to the right, toward the side of the lateral flexion): Below C2 the facets are angled about 45 degrees between the coronal and horizontal planes, so lateral flexion and rotation are forced to the same side. Right lateral flexion therefore carries the C5 body into right rotation. The opposite-side pattern is the neutral-posture lumbar rule, and flexion does not reverse cervical coupling. A pure lateral flexion without rotation is not possible given the oblique facets.
  27. The correct answer is D (The highest oxygen consumption rate reached at maximal exertion, which plateaus as workload keeps rising): VO2 max is the maximal rate of oxygen uptake and use during exhaustive dynamic exercise, and a plateau despite rising workload is the classic sign it has been reached. It is limited chiefly by cardiac output and muscle oxidative capacity. Arterial oxygen content is a concentration, not a rate. The lactate threshold occurs at a submaximal fraction of VO2 max, and resting consumption is about 3.5 mL/kg/min.
  28. The correct answer is C (IgM, a pentamer produced first in primary responses and the most efficient classical complement activator): IgM appears first in a primary response, and its pentameric structure with ten binding sites lets a single molecule bind C1q efficiently. IgG rises later and dominates secondary responses, with weaker complement fixation per molecule. IgA works mainly at mucosal surfaces and IgE mediates allergic and antiparasitic responses.
  29. The correct answer is B (Testicular torsion, a surgical emergency needing exploration within about six hours): Sudden severe scrotal pain with nausea, a high riding horizontally oriented testis and an absent cremasteric reflex indicates torsion of the spermatic cord. Salvage rates fall sharply after about six hours, so immediate urologic referral is needed. Epididymitis has a gradual onset, a preserved reflex and relief of pain on elevation. Appendiceal torsion is milder with a blue dot, and a hernia does not account for the high lie.
  30. The correct answer is B (A sequestered fragment that has separated from the parent disc and migrated upward): A sequestration is disc material that has lost continuity with the parent disc and lies free in the canal, often migrating up or down from the level of origin. An extrusion remains connected, with a neck narrower than the herniated portion. A protrusion has a base wider than the herniated part. A Schmorl node herniates into the vertebral endplate and does not lie behind the vertebral body.
  31. The correct answer is C (Roots, then trunks, divisions, cords and finally terminal branches): The brachial plexus runs from roots to trunks to divisions to cords to terminal branches, often remembered as Randy Travis Drinks Cold Beer. The five roots form three trunks, each trunk splits into anterior and posterior divisions, and these regroup into three cords named for their relation to the axillary artery. The cords give the terminal nerves such as musculocutaneous, median, ulnar, radial and axillary.
  32. The correct answer is C (Renal colic from a ureteral stone, so refer urgently for urinalysis and imaging without manipulation.): Sudden colicky flank pain that radiates to the groin with hematuria and restlessness is the typical picture of a ureteral stone. Mechanical back pain changes with position and does not produce blood in the urine. Pyelonephritis usually brings fever and chills. Because severe pain may hide obstruction or infection, the patient needs urgent medical evaluation, and spinal manipulation is not appropriate.
  33. The correct answer is C (Lumbar spinal stenosis, with canal narrowing from ligamentum flavum thickening and facet hypertrophy): Extension narrows the stenotic canal and flexion widens it, so sitting or leaning forward relieves neurogenic claudication, and downhill walking worsens it. Thickened ligamentum flavum and facet hypertrophy are common causes. Vascular claudication is relieved by standing still, comes with diminished pulses and is worse uphill. Herniation worsens with sitting.
  34. The correct answer is C (L1 and L2 segments, carried by the genitofemoral and femoral branches): The cremasteric reflex is mediated by the L1 and L2 segments, and stroking the upper inner thigh normally elevates the ipsilateral testis. The abdominal reflexes test T8 to T12, the plantar response tests mainly S1, and the bulbocavernosus reflex tests S2 to S4. Superficial reflexes are lost in upper motor neuron lesions, so the cremasteric reflex helps localize a lesion between T12 and L2.
  35. The correct answer is B (Presbyopia from lens stiffening and presbycusis, with high frequency hearing loss appearing first): Presbyopia is loss of near focusing from stiffening of the lens, and presbycusis is gradual bilateral sensorineural hearing loss that begins with high frequencies. Both are normal. Cataract, otosclerosis, macular degeneration, Meniere disease, glaucoma and acoustic neuroma are disorders and are not expected in every older person.
  36. The correct answer is C (The vascular model, where positional change of the spine impairs arterial flow to neural structures): A theory that spinal position restricts arterial perfusion of the brainstem is the vascular model. Compression models deal with nerve root deformation, axonal aberration with axoplasmic flow, and somatovisceral models with autonomic reflexes. Only the vascular model rests on narrowing of the vertebral artery.
  37. The correct answer is D (Elevated methylmalonic acid, since B12 cofactors methylmalonyl-CoA mutase and folate deficiency leaves it normal): Methylmalonic acid is raised only when B12 is lacking, because folate is not involved in conversion of methylmalonyl-CoA, so a high value in this patient with neuropathy confirms B12 deficiency despite a normal folate of 12 ng/mL. Hypersegmentation, macrocytosis and high homocysteine occur in both conditions. Prolonged metformin reduces ileal B12 absorption, and neurologic signs warrant prompt treatment.
  38. The correct answer is A (Intrapleural pressure rises to atmospheric, so the lung collapses and the chest wall recoils outward): At rest the lung recoils inward and the chest wall recoils outward, and the negative intrapleural pressure holds them together. Air entering the pleural space brings that pressure to atmospheric, removing the opposing balance. The lung then collapses and the chest wall springs outward, giving absent breath sounds and hyperresonance.
  39. The correct answer is A (Withhold cervical manipulation and sports, then refer for neurologic evaluation and advanced imaging): Transverse ligament laxity is common in Down syndrome, and an atlantodental interval above about 5 mm in a child indicates atlantoaxial instability. With neck pain and gait change she may have early cord compromise, so manipulation and contact or loading sports must stop and she needs urgent neurologic and imaging review. Traction, clearance or watchful waiting could cause or miss cord injury.
  40. The correct answer is B (Hawkins-Kennedy test, suggesting subacromial impingement of the supraspinatus tendon): Passive internal rotation with the arm and elbow flexed to 90 degrees drives the greater tuberosity and supraspinatus tendon under the coracoacromial arch, so pain makes the test positive for impingement. The apprehension test uses abduction and external rotation, and the Speed test uses resisted forward flexion with a supinated forearm. The drop arm test needs the patient to lower the abducted arm slowly.
  41. The correct answer is A (Supraspinatus tendon, which initiates abduction and holds the arm in an abducted position): A positive drop arm test means the patient cannot hold the abducted arm against gravity, which indicates a rotator cuff tear, most often of the supraspinatus. Weak external rotation suggests the tear may extend into infraspinatus. Subscapularis tears affect internal rotation and the lift-off test. Biceps tendon problems are tested with Speed and Yergason maneuvers, and acromioclavicular sprains cause tenderness at the joint.
  42. The correct answer is D (The mitral valve, also called the bicuspid valve, with two cusps): The mitral or bicuspid valve separates the left atrium from the left ventricle and is the valve most often damaged in rheumatic heart disease. The tricuspid valve is the right atrioventricular valve. The aortic and pulmonary valves are semilunar outflow valves, so they do not lie between atrium and ventricle.
  43. The correct answer is C (Secondary prevention, because it detects disease early in people who do not yet have symptoms): Screening finds disease in the asymptomatic stage so that it can be treated earlier, which defines secondary prevention. Primary prevention would be counseling on diet or exercise to stop the disease from starting. Tertiary prevention applies after disease is established and aims to limit disability. Primordial prevention works upstream on social and environmental conditions, not on individual measurement.
  44. The correct answer is D (Stretch iliopsoas and rectus femoris, then strengthen gluteals and abdominals with bridges and dead bugs): In lower crossed syndrome the tight hip flexors and erectors pull the pelvis into anterior tilt while the weak glutes and abdominals fail to counter it. Treatment lengthens the tight groups and strengthens the weak groups, so iliopsoas stretching plus bridges and dead bugs fits. Strengthening the erectors or hip flexors adds force to muscles that are already overactive.
  45. The correct answer is C (Glucose has an aldehyde at carbon 1, making it an aldose, while fructose is a ketose): Monosaccharides are classed by their carbonyl group. An aldose has an aldehyde at the end of the chain, as glucose does at carbon 1, and a ketose has a ketone inside the chain, as fructose does at carbon 2. Both are hexoses with six carbons and one carbonyl group. The answers that swap the labels, call the carbonyl a carboxyl, or change the carbon count are wrong.
  46. The correct answer is C (Phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency, managed by dietary restriction of phenylalanine plus tyrosine supplementation): Phenylketonuria results from phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency, so phenylalanine accumulates and tyrosine becomes essential, causing hypopigmentation and neurological injury. A musty odor comes from phenylketones. Early dietary phenylalanine restriction with tyrosine supplementation prevents damage. Alkaptonuria involves homogentisate oxidase, galactosemia causes jaundice and cataracts, and maple syrup urine disease has a distinctive sweet odor.
  47. The correct answer is B (Obtain lumbar radiographs now and arrange MRI or bone scan to search for skeletal metastasis): Age over 50, a history of cancer, night pain and unexplained weight loss are red flags for spinal metastasis, so imaging is indicated before any trial of care. Plain film is the first study, but it misses disease until 30 to 50 percent of the mineral is lost, so MRI or a bone scan should follow. Stress films test for instability, which is not the concern here, and delaying imaging risks missed cord compression.
  48. The correct answer is A (Dorsal root and cranial nerve sensory ganglia, where the genome persists after primary varicella infection): After primary varicella, varicella-zoster virus travels along sensory nerves and establishes latency in dorsal root and cranial nerve ganglia. Reactivation sends virus down the sensory nerve to the skin of one dermatome, producing painful vesicles of shingles. Motor neurons are not the site of latency. The skin heals without persistent virus, and memory B cells are the latency site for Epstein-Barr virus.
  49. The correct answer is B (10 gram monofilament pressed to bending at plantar sites, recording each as felt or not felt): The 10 gram Semmes-Weinstein monofilament is the accepted bedside screen for loss of protective sensation in diabetic neuropathy. It is pressed perpendicular to the skin until it bends, at several plantar sites such as the great toe and metatarsal heads, and each site is recorded felt or not felt. A cotton wisp tests light touch only, an ankle reflex does not test sensation, and a calf pinwheel level suits a root or cord lesion, not a stocking neuropathy.
  50. The correct answer is A (Femoral nerve, whose L2 to L4 fibers supply the quadriceps muscle and anterior thigh skin): The femoral nerve arises from L2 to L4 and passes along the psoas muscle, where retractor pressure can injure it. It supplies the quadriceps and anterior thigh skin, so damage weakens knee extension and abolishes the patellar reflex. The obturator nerve shares the same roots but supplies the adductors. The superior gluteal nerve drives hip abduction, and the sciatic nerve supplies the hamstrings and leg.
  51. The correct answer is A (Stroke volume increases, since greater end-diastolic stretch improves actin-myosin overlap and sarcomere calcium sensitivity): Raising venous return increases end-diastolic volume, which stretches sarcomeres toward their optimal length. This enhances calcium sensitivity and cross-bridge formation, so the heart ejects the extra volume and stroke volume increases. The mechanism is intrinsic and does not need nerves or hormones. Overstretch reducing output applies only to very high filling pressures, as in severe heart failure.
  52. The correct answer is D (Chondrosarcoma, with rings, arcs and popcorn calcifications that mark a cartilage matrix): Chondrosarcoma is the common malignant tumor of the pelvis in middle-aged and older adults, and it destroys cortex and extends into soft tissue. Rings, arcs and popcorn calcification are the signature of cartilage matrix. Osteosarcoma makes cloudlike osteoid, fibrous dysplasia shows ground glass density, and giant cell tumor has no matrix.
  53. The correct answer is D (Psoas major, which attaches to the lumbar vertebral bodies and discs and to the lesser trochanter): Psoas major arises from the lumbar vertebral bodies, intervertebral discs and transverse processes and inserts on the lesser trochanter, so it flexes the hip and loads the lumbar spine. Tightness produces pain on hip extension and tenderness lateral to the vertebral bodies. Quadratus lumborum lies between the twelfth rib and iliac crest and does not cross the hip. Rectus femoris flexes the hip but has no lumbar attachment.
Case N-B14

Patient: F, 33 y, 66 in, 158 lb

Vitals: Pulse 72, resp 16, BP 108/66 mmHg, temp 98.4 F

Chief complaint and history: Three months after vaginal delivery of her second child. Deep right buttock ache 6/10 when rising from a chair and rolling over in bed. She points with one finger just below the right PSIS. No pain below the knee, no numbness, no bladder or bowel change. Breastfeeding, no fever. Pelvic pain began in the third trimester.

Examination: Weight shift off the right leg in single leg stance; Trendelenburg negative. Lumbar flexion full, extension mildly uncomfortable at end range without leg symptoms. Tenderness over the right sacral sulcus and long dorsal sacroiliac ligament. Lumbar spinous processes nontender. Hip internal rotation 40 degrees bilaterally, painless.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Straight leg raise 80 degrees bilaterally without radicular pain. Femoral nerve stretch negative. Right side positive: distraction, compression, thigh thrust, Gaenslen. Sacral thrust negative. FABER reproduces buttock pain. Strength, reflexes and sensation L4 to S1 normal.

Laboratory and imaging: No imaging done. Hemoglobin 12.6 g/dL, ESR 14 mm/h. No history of inflammatory back pain, psoriasis or uveitis.

  1. The correct answers are B and D: The Fortin finger sign, pain located just below the PSIS, is a classic sacroiliac localizer, and pregnancy-related pelvic girdle pain with pain on transitions fits a joint loaded by ligament laxity. A negative Trendelenburg and normal hip rotation lower the likelihood of a hip source but do not point to the pelvis. Normal laboratory values and an absent cauda equina picture are safety findings, not localizers.
  2. The correct answers are B and E: The validated cluster supports a sacroiliac source when at least three of the five provocation tests reproduce familiar pain, and four are positive here. One negative test, sacral thrust, does not cancel the cluster. FABER stresses the hip as well as the sacroiliac joint, and a single test has weak accuracy. The negative neural tension tests and normal neurology argue against radiculopathy.
  3. The correct answers are D and E: Postpartum sacroiliac pain with laxity responds best to a combination of manual therapy and stabilization training, with a belt as a short-term aid to compress the pelvic girdle during upright activity. Bed rest deconditions the stabilizers and prolongs symptoms. Aggressive stretching of already lax tissue can aggravate instability. MRI is not indicated without neurological signs or red flags.
  4. The correct answer is C (Score of 4, so perform a rapid antigen test and treat for streptococcus if positive): One point each is given for fever, tonsillar exudate, tender anterior cervical nodes and absence of cough, which totals 4. The age modifier does not add or subtract points at age 19. A score of 4 raises the probability of group A streptococcus, but guidelines still advise confirming with a rapid antigen test or culture before antibiotics. Exudate and fever clearly count, so a score of 2 or 3 is miscounted.
  5. The correct answer is A (Use low-force methods such as instrument adjusting or soft tissue work, and avoid high-velocity rotational thrusts): A T-score of -3.1 is in the osteoporotic range, and high-velocity rotational loading raises the risk of vertebral compression fracture. Low-force instrument methods and soft tissue work can still help with pain. Added force would be unsafe, and referral for vertebroplasty is not indicated without a painful acute fracture, while fewer thrusts still leave the same hazard in each thrust.
  6. The correct answer is C (Supraspinatus tendon, which passes under the coracoacromial arch above the humeral head): Supraspinatus initiates abduction and its tendon runs under the coracoacromial arch, where it is squeezed and worn, so it is the most commonly torn rotator cuff tendon. Infraspinatus and teres minor externally rotate, and subscapularis internally rotates. Each of those can tear but far less often than supraspinatus.
  7. The correct answer is A (Collimate tightly to the lumbar region so a smaller volume of tissue is irradiated): Tight collimation limits the irradiated volume, which lowers both patient dose and scatter reaching the film, and it improves contrast. Opening the field does the opposite. Reducing kVp and raising mAs increases absorbed dose to the skin. Moving the patient away from the receptor adds magnification and blur and does not meaningfully reduce the dose to tissue in the beam.
  8. The correct answer is B (A covalent amide bond joining one carboxyl group to the amino group of the next residue): The peptide bond forms by condensation between the alpha carboxyl group of one amino acid and the alpha amino group of the next, releasing water and creating a covalent amide linkage. It has partial double bond character, so it is planar and rigid. Hydrogen bonds stabilize secondary structure, while disulfide bonds and salt bridges stabilize tertiary and quaternary structure.
  9. The correct answer is A (Acute respiratory alkalosis, with little renal compensation yet because bicarbonate remains near the normal range): A pH of 7.50 is alkalemic, and PaCO2 of 30 mmHg is low, so the primary problem is respiratory alkalosis from hyperventilation. Bicarbonate of 22 is only slightly below normal, which fits an acute process because the kidneys need days to compensate. Metabolic alkalosis would show a high bicarbonate. Respiratory acidosis would show a high PaCO2 and an acidic pH.
  10. The correct answer is B (Acromegaly, caused by excess growth hormone from a pituitary adenoma): A heel pad thicker than about 25 mm with an enlarged calcaneal spur, in an adult with enlarging hands and feet and coarse features, indicates acromegaly. The cause is excess growth hormone, usually from a pituitary adenoma, after epiphyseal closure. If the excess began before closure, the result would be gigantism with tall stature. Hyperparathyroidism and Cushing disease produce bone loss, not soft tissue thickening.
  11. The correct answer is A (Iliocostalis most lateral, longissimus intermediate, and spinalis closest to the spinous processes): The erector spinae columns from lateral to medial are iliocostalis, longissimus and spinalis, often remembered as I Love Spinach. Iliocostalis attaches to the ribs and angle, longissimus lies between and reaches the transverse processes and mastoid, and spinalis is closest to the spinous processes. Any other sequence misplaces at least one column.
  12. The correct answer is D (Middle meningeal artery, injured where it runs beneath the thin pterion region of the temporal bone): The lucid interval, temporal impact and lens-shaped collection that stops at the sutures describe an epidural hematoma. The high-pressure middle meningeal artery lies against the inner table at the pterion, so a fracture there tears it. Bridging veins produce crescent-shaped subdural bleeds across sutures. A sagittal sinus tear would follow a vertex injury, and a ruptured aneurysm causes subarachnoid blood.
  13. The correct answer is A (Korr facilitated segment, where a hyperexcitable cord level exaggerates output to minor afferent input): The decisive detail is the lowered threshold of segmental neurons that yields exaggerated responses, which defines Korr facilitation. Kerr dysafferentation stresses altered afferent input and its effect on central processing, not a hyperexcitable segment. Axonal and vascular models involve transport and perfusion.
  14. The correct answer is D (Subjective, because they are patient reported answers showing that red flag screening was done.): Important negatives such as denial of bowel or bladder changes come from the patient's history, so they belong in Subjective and show that red flag screening was done. Objective holds findings the doctor observes or measures. Assessment is the doctor's impression, and Plan is what happens next. Documenting negatives accurately and concisely earns Documentation credit.
  15. The correct answer is B (Thiamine deficiency, treated with intravenous thiamine given before any glucose administration): Confusion, ophthalmoplegia and ataxia form the triad of Wernicke encephalopathy from thiamine deficiency. Treatment is intravenous thiamine, given before any glucose because glucose can consume remaining thiamine and precipitate worse injury. B12 deficiency causes a dorsal column syndrome, niacin deficiency gives pellagra, and pyridoxine deficiency causes neuropathy and sideroblastic anemia.
  16. The correct answer is C (Suppression of competing bacterial flora by antibiotics, which allows resident Candida albicans to overgrow): Candida albicans is part of normal oral flora and is kept in check by competing bacteria. Broad spectrum antibiotics remove these competitors, allowing yeast to multiply and produce thrush. Other classic risk factors are diabetes, corticosteroids, and HIV, but each is excluded by her history. A neutrophil oxidative burst defect is the mechanism in chronic granulomatous disease and is not suggested, and new strain acquisition is not required.
  17. The correct answer is C (Abdominal muscles and the gluteal muscles, in a lower crossed syndrome): In lower crossed syndrome, tight hip flexors and lumbar extensors are crossed with weak abdominals and gluteals, which tips the pelvis forward and increases the lordosis. The upper crossed syndrome involves the neck and shoulders, and a posterior pelvic tilt would flatten the lordosis. The hip flexors and extensors in this patient are the tight muscles rather than the weak ones.
  18. The correct answer is C (Maintenance care, documented with a defined purpose, visit interval, and periodic reassessment): She has reached maximum improvement and wants visits to prevent recurrence, which is maintenance care. It should be documented with its purpose, a defined interval, and periodic reassessment. Supportive care is for a chronic condition that is not expected to change and still needs symptom control. Corrective care assumes more improvement is expected, and acute care does not fit stable, mild symptoms.
  19. The correct answer is D (Carotid triangle, bounded by omohyoid, digastric and sternocleidomastoid muscles): The carotid triangle is bounded by the superior belly of omohyoid, the posterior belly of digastric and the anterior border of sternocleidomastoid. It contains the common carotid bifurcation, the carotid sheath contents and the hypoglossal and vagus nerves. The submandibular triangle holds the gland of that name, the muscular triangle holds the strap muscles and thyroid, and the submental triangle lies in the midline above the hyoid.
  20. The correct answer is D (58 percent, from a stroke volume of 70 mL over an end-diastolic volume of 120 mL): Stroke volume equals end-diastolic volume minus end-systolic volume, so 120 mL minus 50 mL is 70 mL. Ejection fraction is stroke volume divided by end-diastolic volume, so 70 divided by 120 is 0.583, or about 58 percent. The 42 percent figure is the fraction left behind in the ventricle, not the fraction ejected. The 70 percent and 29 percent values mistake the unit or inflate the denominator.
  21. The correct answer is A (The T7 spinous process, lying near the same transverse plane as the inferior angle): With the arms at the sides the inferior angle of the scapula lies at about the T7 level, a standard surface landmark for the thoracic spine. The root of the scapular spine marks T3 instead. The xiphisternal junction is an anterior landmark at about T9. A line through the scapular angle falls well above the T12 level.
  22. The correct answer is B (Lesch-Nyhan syndrome from deficiency of the purine salvage enzyme hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase): The combination of self-mutilation, intellectual disability, spasticity, hyperuricemia and X-linked inheritance is classic for Lesch-Nyhan syndrome, a lack of HGPRT. Without salvage, purines are degraded to uric acid and PRPP accumulates, increasing de novo synthesis. Adenosine deaminase deficiency causes immunodeficiency and von Gierke disease causes hypoglycemia with hepatomegaly, and orotic aciduria causes anemia.
  23. The correct answer is B (Ask about pack years, persistent cough, unexplained weight loss and any coughing up of blood.): Night pain unrelated to movement in a long term smoker raises concern for a lung apex malignancy such as a Pancoast tumor. Pack years, cough, weight loss and hemoptysis are the targeted red flag questions. Mechanical, venous and digestive histories are valid in other cases but do not address this patient's risk. Asking the right questions, not many questions, earns Patient Evaluation credit.
  24. The correct answer is A (Avoid cervical rotation thrusts, use soft tissue or low-force care, and refer for vascular evaluation): Dizziness and nystagmus during the screen suggest reduced vertebrobasilar flow with that position, which is a warning sign rather than a diagnosis. Cervical thrust techniques of any type should be avoided, and gentle non-thrust care can address stiffness while she is referred for vascular evaluation. A reversed test or a different thrust leaves the same arterial risk.
  25. The correct answer is A (Sickle cell anemia, where endplate infarction halts central growth while peripheral growth continues): Central endplate depressions that produce H-shaped vertebral bodies result from sickle cell vaso-occlusion, which infarcts the central endplate while the peripheral endplate continues to grow. Chronic anemia with bone pain crises and autosplenectomy supports sickle cell disease. Thalassemia expands the marrow and Gaucher disease causes marrow packing, and neither produces the focal central step defect.
  26. The correct answer is B (Low oxygen tension promotes polymerization of deoxygenated hemoglobin S, distorting red cells and occluding small vessels): Sickle hemoglobin has valine in place of glutamic acid at position 6 of the beta chain, and when deoxygenated it polymerizes. Hypoxia, dehydration, and acidosis favor polymerization, producing rigid sickled cells that block small vessels and cause ischemic pain. Heinz bodies point to G6PD deficiency, and warm autoantibodies point to autoimmune hemolysis. Hemoglobin S releases oxygen readily, and its problem is the shape change.
  27. The correct answer is A (Levator ani and coccygeus, which together form the pelvic diaphragm supporting the viscera): The pelvic diaphragm is formed by levator ani and coccygeus, which support the pelvic viscera and resist increases in intra-abdominal pressure. Childbirth can stretch or injure them and contribute to prolapse. Obturator internus and piriformis line the pelvic walls and act on the hip. The bulbospongiosus and ischiocavernosus are superficial perineal muscles, and the sphincters guard the outlets rather than forming the floor.
  28. The correct answer is A (Extensor surfaces such as the elbows and knees, the scalp, lumbosacral area and nails): Silvery scaled plaques, nail pitting and the Auspitz sign (pinpoint bleeding on scale removal) point to psoriasis. Plaques favor extensor surfaces, the scalp, the lumbosacral area and the nails. Atopic dermatitis favors flexures in adults, seborrheic dermatitis causes greasy scale of the face and scalp, and zoster is dermatomal and vesicular. Psoriasis can also accompany psoriatic arthritis.
Case N-B01

Patient: M, 44 y, 71 in, 196 lb

Vitals: Pulse 74, resp 16, BP 128/82 mmHg, temp 98.2 F

Chief complaint and history: Electrician with 3 weeks of low back pain after lifting a cable spool. Pain now travels down the back of the left thigh and calf to the outer foot, worse with sitting and coughing. No bowel or bladder change, no fever, no weight loss. Ibuprofen gives partial relief. Prior episode of mild back stiffness 5 years ago.

Examination: Antalgic posture with slight right lateral shift. Lumbar flexion limited to 40 degrees by pain. Left L5-S1 paraspinal muscles tender and hypertonic. Hip range of motion full and painless bilaterally. Pedal pulses 2+ bilaterally. Abdomen soft and nontender.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Left straight leg raise reproduces leg pain at 40 degrees, right negative. Crossed straight leg raise negative. Patellar reflexes 2+ bilaterally, Achilles 1+ left and 2+ right. Left toe walking weak, heel walking normal. Great toe extension 5/5 bilaterally. Light touch reduced along the left lateral foot and fifth toe. Plantar responses flexor.

Laboratory and imaging: Lumbar radiographs: mild narrowing of the L4-5 disc space with small anterior osteophytes, lordosis reduced, no fracture or lytic lesion. Urinalysis normal. Fasting glucose 92 mg/dL.

  1. The correct answers are A and D: The diminished Achilles reflex, weak toe walking and lateral foot sensory loss map to the S1 root, and a posterolateral L5-S1 protrusion compresses the S1 root as it passes the disc. L5 radiculopathy would weaken great toe extension and heel walking and spare the Achilles reflex, the opposite of this chart. The L4-5 narrowing is an incidental degenerative finding. Piriformis syndrome and sacroiliac sprain do not produce a reflex deficit or a dermatomal sensory loss.
  2. The correct answers are D and E: The Achilles reflex is mediated mainly by S1, and the lateral foot and fifth toe are the S1 dermatome, so these two findings localize the root. A positive straight leg raise at 40 degrees supports root tension at L5 or S1 but cannot tell them apart. Pain below the knee and paraspinal tenderness are likewise shared by neighboring levels. Normal great toe extension and heel walking in this chart further argue against L5.
  3. The correct answers are B and D: A stable radiculopathy without cauda equina signs, progressive weakness or red flags is managed conservatively, with periodic neurologic rechecks. MRI is ordered when the deficit worsens or when there is no meaningful improvement after roughly 6 weeks, since results would not change early care. Prolonged bed rest is no longer advised and delays recovery. Surgical referral is for progressive or disabling deficit or failure of conservative care, not a stable reflex change.
  4. The correct answer is D (Grade 3, full range of motion against gravity without added resistance): Grade 3 means the muscle moves the joint through the full range against gravity but cannot overcome any added resistance. Grade 2 requires gravity to be eliminated, and grade 4 means the muscle tolerates moderate resistance. Grade 1 shows only a flicker of contraction with no movement. The patient moves against gravity and fails resistance, so grade 3 applies.
  5. The correct answer is D (Streptococcus pneumoniae, a gram positive diplococcus carried in the adult nasopharynx): Abrupt onset with shaking chills, rust colored sputum and lobar consolidation is the textbook community acquired lobar pneumonia, and Streptococcus pneumoniae is the usual cause. Mycoplasma causes a milder, gradual illness with patchy infiltrates. Klebsiella is linked with alcoholism and thick red sputum, and Staphylococcus aureus follows influenza. This patient has none of those settings.
  6. The correct answer is C (A plain film skeletal survey, because myeloma lesions are often cold on a bone scan): Technetium bone scans depend on osteoblastic activity, so purely lytic myeloma lesions can be cold or entirely missed even when the skeleton is full of disease. A plain film skeletal survey shows punched out lytic lesions directly and complements the scan. Repeating the scan or delaying images does not add osteoclastic lesion detection, one pelvis film samples too little of the skeleton, and gallium targets soft tissue and nodal disease rather than the marrow.
  7. The correct answer is B (Twice weekly visits tapering over weeks, combining mobilization or HVLA with active exercise): Chronic joint dysfunction involves fibrosis and soft tissue adaptation, so it responds to a graded plan that restores motion and builds capacity through exercise. Visits are spaced and tapered as function improves. Daily high-force care is unsafe and unnecessary, and passive modalities alone do not address the restricted motion.
  8. The correct answer is C (Findings consistent with a urinary tract infection, needing medical referral for culture and antibiotic treatment): Leukocyte esterase indicates white blood cells and nitrite indicates bacteria that convert nitrate, so together with dysuria and frequency they strongly suggest a urinary tract infection that requires medical treatment. A stone typically causes more blood and severe colicky pain. Glucose has its own separate pad, and dehydration concentrates urine without creating positive nitrite.
Case N-B11

Patient: F, 49 y, 64 in, 150 lb

Vitals: Pulse 78, resp 16, BP 122/76 mmHg, temp 99.0 F

Chief complaint and history: Middle school teacher with 6 months of stiffness and swelling in both hands and wrists, worst on waking and lasting about 2 hours. Grip is weak, with trouble opening jars. Fatigue. Both feet ache in the morning. No psoriasis, eye problems or back pain. Mother had thyroid disease. Takes acetaminophen. Hands also ache after long grading sessions.

Examination: Symmetric swelling and warmth of the 2nd and 3rd MCP joints and both wrists. PIP joints of the 2nd and 3rd fingers mildly boggy. DIP joints spared, with no Heberden nodes. Metatarsal squeeze painful bilaterally. Grip reduced bilaterally. No skin scaling or nail pitting.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Phalen and Tinel tests mildly positive at both wrists. Cervical compression negative. Reflexes 2+ in upper and lower limbs. Sensation intact. No elbow nodules. No knee effusion. Hip motion full.

Laboratory and imaging: Hand films: periarticular soft tissue swelling and mild osteopenia at the MCP joints, no erosions yet. Hemoglobin 11.4 g/dL, MCV 86 fL, WBC 7.2 x10^3/uL. Serologic tests and inflammatory markers have not been ordered.

  1. The correct answers are B and C: Symmetric swelling of the MCP and wrist joints with DIP sparing and morning stiffness beyond an hour for over 6 weeks fits rheumatoid arthritis. Osteoarthritis favors the DIP and first carpometacarpal joints with bony nodes and brief stiffness. Overuse aching does not cause warm swollen joints or foot involvement. Boggy PIP joints reflect synovitis, whereas Heberden nodes are hard bony DIP enlargements.
  2. The correct answers are A and E: Symmetric synovitis of MCP and wrist joints with feet involved, periarticular osteopenia and no erosions yet describes early rheumatoid arthritis, and normocytic anemia is the usual anemia of chronic inflammation. Osteoarthritis spares the MCP and wrist and shows bony nodes. Psoriatic arthritis is distal and often asymmetric with nail pitting. Hemochromatosis affects the same MCP joints but produces hook-like osteophytes without warm synovitis or soft tissue swelling.
  3. The correct answers are A and E: Anti-CCP antibody is highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis and often appears early, and rheumatoid factor is positive in most patients though less specific. ESR and C-reactive protein rise with active inflammation and track disease activity. The antinuclear antibody is nonspecific and HLA-B27 belongs to spondyloarthritis. Uric acid testing addresses gout, which does not give a symmetric small joint pattern.
  4. The correct answer is A (C6, from a herniation at the C5 to C6 level): The C6 root supplies the thumb and lateral forearm, helps flex the elbow and extend the wrist, and carries the brachioradialis reflex. The C5 root would weaken the deltoid and spare the thumb, C7 reduces the triceps reflex and numbs the middle finger, and C8 weakens grip. A C5 to C6 herniation compresses the C6 root.
  5. The correct answer is A (About 126 beats per minute, calculated as 70 percent of an estimated maximum of 180): Estimated maximal heart rate is 220 minus 40, which equals 180 beats per minute. Seventy percent of 180 is 0.70 times 180, which equals 126 beats per minute. Applying the percentage to 220, using 200 minus age, or subtracting age twice each produce a different number and do not follow the stated method.
  6. The correct answer is D (A burst fracture of the atlas ring, caused by axial compression forces through the skull): Axial loading drives the occipital condyles into the C1 ring, which breaks at the anterior and posterior arches, a Jefferson fracture. On the open mouth view the lateral masses of C1 overhang the C2 articular pillars bilaterally, and a combined overhang greater than 7 mm implies transverse ligament rupture. The other injuries arise from flexion, extension, or twisting loads.
  7. The correct answer is D (Within two to four weeks, with referral or co-management if function has not meaningfully improved): Uncomplicated acute low back pain without red flags usually improves within the first weeks, so a formal check at two to four weeks separates expected recovery from a poor response. If function and pain have not meaningfully improved by then, the plan is changed, co-managed or referred. Waiting two or three months wastes the window for a different pathway, and open-ended care without a decision point lacks accountability. Reflexive advanced imaging after one visit is not supported when no red flags exist.
  8. The correct answer is C (47,XXY from meiotic nondisjunction that leaves an extra X chromosome and causes primary testicular failure): Klinefelter syndrome, usually 47,XXY, causes small firm testes, azoospermia, gynecomastia, tall stature, and low testosterone, and the loss of negative feedback raises FSH and LH. Turner syndrome is 45,X in females. XYY males are tall but usually fertile. Complete androgen insensitivity produces a female phenotype in a 46,XY individual.
  9. The correct answer is D (Arrange emergency transfer for urgent MRI and surgical decompression evaluation today): Saddle anesthesia, urinary retention, reduced sphincter tone and bilateral weakness indicate cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency. Outcome depends on decompression within hours, so same-day emergency imaging with MRI and surgical evaluation is required. Radiographs cannot show the compressing lesion. Traction and a delayed urology visit both waste time and traction may worsen compression.
  10. The correct answer is C (A state of complete physical, mental, and social wellbeing, not only the absence of disease): The WHO defines health as complete physical, mental, and social wellbeing and not merely the absence of disease or infirmity, which is broader than a medical model. The other definitions narrow health to disease absence, pain-free function, or a nervous system claim that is not part of the WHO wording.
  11. The correct answer is A (Classic Hodgkin lymphoma, most often the nodular sclerosis subtype in young adults): Painless cervical nodes with B symptoms, pruritus and Reed-Sternberg cells on biopsy establish Hodgkin lymphoma. Nodular sclerosis is the most common subtype in young adults. Non-Hodgkin lymphomas lack Reed-Sternberg cells, mononucleosis nodes are tender and the biopsy shows reactive lymphocytes, and chronic lymphocytic leukemia affects older patients with high lymphocyte counts.
  12. The correct answer is C (Image left is the patient's right, so the organ is the liver): Axial CT and MRI slices are displayed as if the viewer stands at the patient's feet looking toward the head, so the patient's right appears on the viewer's left. A large solid organ on image left is therefore on the patient's right side, which fits the liver. Reading image left as patient left would mislabel it as the spleen. Slices are not viewed from the head end, and a fixed convention exists, so a scout image is unnecessary for this question.
  13. The correct answer is A (Ergosterol in the fungal membrane, which amphotericin B binds and azoles stop the cell making): Amphotericin B binds ergosterol and forms membrane pores, while azoles inhibit fungal cytochrome P450 14-alpha-demethylase and deplete ergosterol. Mammalian membranes contain cholesterol, which explains selective toxicity. Echinocandins inhibit beta-glucan synthesis in the cell wall. Griseofulvin disrupts microtubules in dermatophytes.
  14. The correct answer is B (Likely uncomplicated cystitis, so refer to a medical provider for urinalysis and antibiotic treatment): Dysuria, frequency and suprapubic discomfort without fever or flank tenderness point to an uncomplicated lower urinary tract infection, which a chiropractor does not treat and should refer for urinalysis and antibiotics. Pyelonephritis would bring fever, chills and costovertebral angle tenderness. Facet syndrome does not cause burning urination, and interstitial cystitis is a chronic diagnosis made only after infection is excluded.
  15. The correct answer is B (A raised GGT with the ALP places the source in the biliary tree, so image it): GGT is not found in bone, so a raised GGT with a raised ALP confirms hepatobiliary origin, whereas a normal GGT would point to bone. With bilirubin of 2.1 mg/dL and right upper quadrant symptoms, ultrasound for biliary obstruction or a mass is the logical next step. Normal calcium does not exclude bone disease but the paired GGT answers the question, and the intestinal isoform is a small, transient fraction.
  16. The correct answer is A (Transport of long-chain fatty acyl groups across the inner mitochondrial membrane for beta-oxidation): Long-chain fatty acids are activated in the cytosol and then need carnitine and the carnitine acyltransferases to cross the inner mitochondrial membrane. When carnitine is deficient, fatty acids accumulate in muscle as lipid droplets and cannot be oxidized, so fasting muscle weakness occurs. Because less acetyl CoA is formed in the liver, ketone production stays low, which is why this differs from other causes of fasting hypoglycemia. Activation of the fatty acid occurs before transport, and fatty acid synthesis or glycogen breakdown does not explain the biopsy.
  17. The correct answer is C (Renal cell carcinoma, producing an expansile lytic rib lesion with an adjacent soft tissue mass): Renal cell carcinoma and thyroid carcinoma are the two classic primaries that produce expansile, bubbly, lytic blow out metastases, and the painless gross hematuria in this patient points to a renal primary. The lesion often has a soft tissue mass and a thin outer cortical shell. Prostate metastases are typically sclerotic, and breast metastases are commonly mixed or lytic without marked expansion.
  18. The correct answer is B (Type II dens fracture, unstable with a high rate of nonunion): A type II fracture passes through the base of the dens at its junction with the C2 body, an area with limited blood supply. It is unstable and has a high rate of nonunion, so fixation or halo immobilization is often required. Type I is a tip avulsion, type III extends into the body with better healing, and a Jefferson fracture involves the atlas.
  19. The correct answer is B (Prostate cancer treated five years ago and an unintended 10 kg weight loss this year): A prior cancer, especially prostate, breast, lung, kidney or thyroid, plus unexplained weight loss and non-mechanical night pain makes metastatic spread to the spine the leading concern. Advanced imaging should come before any manipulation. A recent lifting strain would give pain that changes with movement and eases with rest. Short morning stiffness and a family history of disc degeneration describe common benign patterns and carry no malignancy risk of this size.
  20. The correct answer is B (Stay active as tolerated, resume normal tasks, and avoid prolonged bed rest): For acute nonspecific low back pain without red flags, evidence supports staying active and resuming usual tasks as pain allows. Prolonged bed rest leads to deconditioning, stiffness and slower recovery without better pain outcomes. Structured rest schedules and long restriction periods repeat the same problem.
  21. The correct answer is D (CT of the lumbar spine with thin axial slices and sagittal and coronal reformats): CT gives the best cortical bone detail, so it defines the fracture lines, vertebral body comminution, and the size of any retropulsed fragment in the canal. Reformats show the sagittal alignment that surgeons need for planning. MRI is better for the cord, discs, and ligaments, but it shows cortex poorly. A bone scan is nonspecific, and flexion-extension views risk further canal compromise in an acute unstable injury.
  22. The correct answer is A (Fatty streak composed of lipid-laden macrophage foam cells within the intima of the arterial wall): After endothelial injury, monocytes enter the intima, ingest oxidized lipoproteins, and become foam cells that form fatty streaks. These are flat, yellow, and can appear in children. Smooth muscle migration and fibrosis later create the fibrous plaque with a lipid core, and calcification, ulceration, and thrombosis mark complicated plaques. Monckeberg sclerosis is medial calcification without intimal plaque.
  23. The correct answer is D (Phase 4 diastolic depolarization, driven by the slow inward sodium current called the funny current): Sinoatrial nodal cells lack a stable resting potential: in phase 4 the funny current, a slow inward sodium current through HCN channels, drifts the membrane toward threshold, and calcium channels then drive the upstroke. Fast sodium channels drive the ventricular phase 0. The plateau and repolarization phases shape the ventricular response but do not set the pacemaker rate.
  24. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary embolism, with CT pulmonary angiography as the best test to confirm it): Estrogen therapy, prolonged immobility, a swollen calf, tachycardia and hypoxemia make pulmonary embolism highly likely, giving a high pretest probability. D-dimer is not appropriate when probability is high, so CT pulmonary angiography is the best test. Pneumothorax and pericarditis do not explain the calf and hypoxemia. Pneumonia would bring cough and fever.
  25. The correct answer is D (Niacin deficiency, since corn niacin is bound and the tryptophan precursor of NAD is scarce): The triad of dermatitis, diarrhea and dementia defines pellagra, which is niacin deficiency. Niacin in corn is chemically bound and not absorbed unless treated with alkali, and corn is also low in tryptophan, which the body can convert to niacin for NAD synthesis. NAD and NADP are essential electron carriers, so tissues with high turnover such as skin, gut and brain suffer. Riboflavin, pyridoxine and thiamine deficiencies produce different clinical pictures.
  26. The correct answer is D (Mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve, carrying motor fibers to the muscles of chewing): The mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve exits through foramen ovale and carries sensation from the lower face along with motor fibers to the muscles of mastication. Chin numbness and weak chewing fit a lesion there. The maxillary division uses foramen rotundum, the ophthalmic division uses the superior orbital fissure, and the facial nerve exits through the stylomastoid foramen.
  27. The correct answer is A (Reduced nuclear pressure shifts more compressive load to the annulus and facet joints): A hydrated nucleus acts like a fluid cushion that spreads load evenly. As water content falls, pressure falls and the annulus and facets bear more of the compressive load, which accelerates facet arthrosis and narrowing. Narrowed discs are thinner, not thicker, and the anterior longitudinal ligament does not carry axial compression.
  28. The correct answer is B (Naloxone, a competitive mu opioid receptor antagonist that displaces the agonist and restores ventilation): Pinpoint pupils, respiratory depression and needle marks form the opioid toxidrome, which is mediated at mu receptors. Naloxone competes with the opioid at those receptors, and its short half-life means repeat dosing may be needed. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepines, atropine treats cholinergic excess and physostigmine treats anticholinergic poisoning, so none fits this presentation.
  29. The correct answer is C (Positive on the right for local lumbar pain, with no radiating leg pain reproduced): A positive Kemp test is recorded on the side of the movement and the character of the pain matters. Local low back pain on that side suggests a facet, muscular or ligamentous source, while pain radiating into the leg points toward a disc or nerve root problem. Recording the opposite side or demanding leg pain for any positive result both misread the test.
  30. The correct answer is D (Carpal tunnel syndrome, managed with neutral wrist positioning, tool redesign and rest breaks): Night paresthesia in the median nerve distribution, relief with shaking and a positive Phalen test point to carpal tunnel syndrome. Controls aim to reduce wrist flexion, force and repetition through neutral posture, tool changes and rest breaks. De Quervain affects the first dorsal compartment and causes radial wrist pain, and cubital tunnel involves the ulnar fingers. Pronator teres syndrome spares night symptoms and a positive Phalen test.
  31. The correct answer is D (Ewing sarcoma of the femoral shaft, which can mimic osteomyelitis with fever and a raised ESR): A permeative diaphyseal lesion with lamellated onion skin periosteal reaction and a soft tissue mass in a child is Ewing sarcoma. Systemic fever, leukocytosis and a raised ESR make it a classic mimic of osteomyelitis, which is why a biopsy is needed. Osteosarcoma favors the metaphysis, and a stress fracture lacks a permeative destructive pattern and a soft tissue mass.
  32. The correct answer is D (Processor or darkroom fog from a light leak or safelight problem): An unexposed film that develops with density has been fogged by something other than the beam, such as a darkroom light leak, a safelight that is too bright or too close, or a chemistry problem. Fog from scatter, poor screen contact or beam filtration requires an exposure to occur and cannot affect a film never placed in the beam. Checking safelight distance and sealing leaks is the correct next step.
  33. The correct answer is B (Giardia lamblia, a flagellated protozoan that attaches to the mucosa of the duodenum and proximal jejunum): Giardia lamblia is acquired from water contaminated with cysts and attaches to the proximal small bowel, causing steatorrhea, bloating, and flatulence rather than bloody diarrhea. Metronidazole treats it. Entamoeba histolytica also responds to metronidazole but invades the colon and causes bloody stool. Cryptosporidium is acid-fast and causes watery diarrhea, and Vibrio cholerae produces massive rice water stool, not greasy malabsorptive stool.
  34. The correct answer is B (Coagulative necrosis in the kidney and liquefactive necrosis in the brain): Ischemic infarcts in most solid organs, including the kidney, produce coagulative necrosis in which the cell outlines persist for days. The brain is the exception because its high lipid content and abundant lysosomal enzymes digest tissue into a liquid, creating liquefactive necrosis. Caseous necrosis is typical of tuberculosis and fat necrosis follows pancreatitis or breast trauma. Fibrinoid necrosis occurs in immune vasculitis and malignant hypertension, and gangrene refers to a clinical pattern of limb or bowel death.
  35. The correct answer is D (Deficient antidiuretic hormone secretion, so collecting duct aquaporin-2 channels are not inserted into the membrane): Hypothalamic injury reduces antidiuretic hormone, so aquaporin-2 channels are not inserted into the collecting duct, water is lost, urine is dilute and serum sodium rises. The sharp response to desmopressin proves the kidney can respond, which makes the defect central. Nephrogenic disease would not respond, SIADH gives concentrated urine with low sodium, and primary polydipsia gives low sodium.
  36. The correct answer is B (Spinal accessory nerve, tested by resisted head rotation and resisted shoulder shrug): The spinal accessory nerve supplies the sternocleidomastoid and upper trapezius and runs superficially in the posterior triangle, where it is easily injured by biopsy. The sternocleidomastoid is tested by turning the head against resistance to the opposite side, and the trapezius by a shoulder shrug against resistance. The long thoracic nerve supplies serratus anterior, the hypoglossal nerve the tongue, and the dorsal scapular nerve the rhomboids.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Low estradiol with elevated FSH, since follicle depletion removes feedback and estradiol loss raises osteoclast activity): Menopause follows depletion of ovarian follicles, so estradiol and inhibin fall. The loss of negative feedback raises pituitary FSH and LH. Estradiol normally restrains osteoclasts, so its loss accelerates bone resorption and lowers density. A low FSH would suggest a hypothalamic or pituitary cause, not primary ovarian failure.
  2. The correct answer is C (The atlantoaxial joint between C1 and C2, which supplies roughly half of all cervical rotation): Full flexion tightens the capsules and orients the lower cervical facets so little rotation remains there, which isolates the pivot at C1 and C2. The atlantoaxial joint, with the dens as the pivot, supplies about 50 percent of total cervical rotation. The atlanto-occipital joint mainly allows nodding, and lower facets and uncovertebral joints are locked or contribute little in this position.
  3. The correct answer is A (Median nerve compression beneath the flexor retinaculum at the carpal tunnel): Wrist flexion for 60 seconds raises carpal tunnel pressure and reproduces tingling in the median nerve distribution, which fits carpal tunnel syndrome. Guyon canal entrapment affects the ulnar nerve and the ring and little fingers. Pronator syndrome affects the median nerve higher up and is provoked by forearm pronation against resistance, not wrist flexion. Radial nerve compression at the arcade of Frohse causes finger extensor weakness with little sensory loss.
  4. The correct answer is D (Applying it over the abdomen or low back of a pregnant patient): Therapeutic ultrasound is contraindicated over the abdomen or low back of a pregnant patient, and also over malignancy and open growth plates. Chronic tendinopathy and resolving muscle strains in adults are standard indications. Pulsed or continuous modes at suitable frequencies are chosen by tissue depth and healing stage, not avoided in these conditions.
  5. The correct answer is B (Median nerve beneath the transverse carpal ligament at the wrist, within the carpal tunnel): Nocturnal numbness of the lateral three and a half digits with thenar weakness describes carpal tunnel syndrome, from median nerve compression beneath the transverse carpal ligament. Ulnar compression spares those digits. The superficial radial nerve is sensory to the dorsal hand, and pronator compression adds forearm symptoms and spares the palm.
  6. The correct answer is B (Refer for same day abdominal ultrasound and medical evaluation, and avoid abdominal manipulation.): Right upper quadrant pain after fatty meals, referral to the right scapula and a positive Murphy sign point to gallbladder disease such as cholecystitis. The next step is same day imaging, typically ultrasound, with medical evaluation, since complications can be serious. Treating the thoracic spine misreads referred visceral pain, and a diet trial or lumbar films delay care.
  7. The correct answer is D (Pyogenic spondylodiscitis, with markedly elevated ESR and C-reactive protein and positive blood cultures): Fever, focal spinal tenderness and risk factors such as injection drug use, with narrowing of the L3-4 disc and endplate erosion, indicate pyogenic spondylodiscitis. Bacteria, most often Staphylococcus aureus, destroy the disc early, and the ESR and C-reactive protein are markedly elevated, with blood cultures commonly positive. Metastasis spares the disc, degeneration is afebrile, and ankylosing spondylitis does not have a febrile onset.
  8. The correct answer is C (Side posture or a pregnancy table with an abdominal cutout, avoiding prolonged lying on the back): After about 20 weeks the enlarged uterus can compress the inferior vena cava when the woman lies supine, lowering venous return and causing the lightheadedness she describes. Side posture or a pregnancy table with a cutout avoids both caval compression and abdominal pressure. A flat prone position loads the uterus, and a seated flexed position compresses the abdomen. Her symptom should not be waited out.
  9. The correct answer is B (A positive risk factor, because his father had coronary disease before age 55): Premature coronary artery disease in a first degree relative, defined as a male relative under age 55 or a female relative under age 65, is an independent risk factor. His father's infarction at 52 qualifies. The age cutoff of 65 applies to mothers and sisters, not fathers or brothers. A mother's diabetes is a separate risk consideration, and first degree relatives are the qualifying group.
  10. The correct answer is A (Middle mediastinum, the compartment bounded by the pericardial sac and its fibrous layer): The middle mediastinum contains the heart, the pericardium, the roots of the great vessels and the phrenic nerves running on the pericardium. The anterior compartment lies between the sternum and pericardium and holds fat, lymph nodes and the thymic remnant. The posterior compartment lies behind the pericardium with the esophagus and descending aorta. The superior compartment lies above the plane of the sternal angle.
  11. The correct answer is B (Lateral surface of the C5 spinous on the side of rotation, drive anterior and superior): A Gonstead cervical correction contacts the misaligned vertebra itself, here C5, at the spinous on the side where it is rotated. The line of drive is opposite the listing, so a posterior-inferior listing is corrected by driving anterior and superior. Driving along the listing pushes the segment further into misalignment, and contacting C6 or the pillar does not reposition the C5 spinous.
  12. The correct answer is C (The pars interarticularis, the bony bridge between the superior and inferior articular processes): On the oblique lumbar view the neck of the Scottie dog is the pars interarticularis, the segment of the lamina between the superior and inferior articular processes. A lucent defect there is spondylolysis and may lead to spondylolisthesis. The pedicle forms the eye, the transverse process the nose, and the lamina the body of the dog.
  13. The correct answer is A (Flexion distraction over the lap belt, with possible hollow viscus or mesenteric injury): A lap belt acts as a fulcrum across the abdomen, so the spine flexes and the posterior elements distract, producing a horizontal Chance fracture through the vertebral body and neural arch. Because the belt compresses the abdomen, bowel, mesenteric, and pancreatic injuries are common and need to be excluded. Burst, hyperextension, and lateral compression injuries come from different loading directions and different associated injuries.
  14. The correct answer is C (Right cerebellar hemisphere, with limb signs on the same side as the lesion): Intention tremor, dysdiadochokinesia and a wide-based gait are cerebellar signs. Cerebellar hemisphere lesions produce limb ataxia on the same side because the pathways cross twice. Sensory ataxia from dorsal column disease worsens when the eyes are closed. A motor cortex lesion causes weakness and spasticity on the opposite side, not coordination loss.
  15. The correct answer is D (Extracellular beta amyloid plaques together with intraneuronal neurofibrillary tangles composed of hyperphosphorylated tau protein): Alzheimer disease shows extracellular beta amyloid plaques and intraneuronal neurofibrillary tangles of hyperphosphorylated tau, with early involvement of the hippocampus and medial temporal lobe. This matches the slow four-year course with memory loss and disorientation. Lewy bodies belong to Parkinson disease and dementia with Lewy bodies, and Pick bodies belong to frontotemporal dementia, which spares memory early. Spongiform change marks Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease, which kills within months rather than years.
  16. The correct answer is A (Increased muscle proteolysis supplies amino acids, and increased hepatic gluconeogenesis raises blood glucose): Cortisol is catabolic in muscle, promoting proteolysis that releases amino acids, which the liver converts to glucose through gluconeogenesis. It also antagonizes insulin in peripheral tissue and raises blood glucose. Together these produce proximal myopathy, thin limbs and steroid-induced hyperglycemia. Cortisol decreases rather than increases muscle glycogen synthesis, and it increases rather than decreases gluconeogenesis.
  17. The correct answer is A (Pretracheal fascia binds the thyroid to the larynx and trachea, so the gland ascends with them): The thyroid is enclosed in pretracheal fascia that attaches it to the larynx and trachea, with the isthmus lying over tracheal rings 2 to 4. When the larynx rises during swallowing, the gland and any mass within it rise as well. A thyroglossal duct cyst moves with tongue protrusion rather than swallowing alone. The investing and prevertebral layers do not tether the gland to a moving structure in this way.
  18. The correct answer is C (The T4 to T5 disc level, the plane where the aortic arch begins and ends): The sternal angle lies at the T4 to T5 intervertebral disc level, which is also the level of the carina, the start and end of the aortic arch and the superior border of the pericardium. The jugular notch is at T2 to T3 and the xiphisternal joint at T9. The transpyloric plane at L1 is an abdominal landmark.
  19. The correct answer is B (Acute appendicitis, so arrange emergency surgical evaluation and avoid manipulation or laxatives.): Periumbilical pain migrating to the right lower quadrant with anorexia and low fever is the classic sequence of appendicitis, which risks rupture and needs emergency surgical evaluation. Gastroenteritis usually brings diarrhea and diffuse pain, and mesenteric adenitis is a diagnosis of exclusion. A ureteral stone causes colicky flank pain with hematuria. A delayed plan is unsafe here.
  20. The correct answer is C (Lateral cervical flexion and extension stress views, taken actively under supervision): Flexion and extension laterals show abnormal anterior or posterior translation of one vertebra on another that is invisible in neutral. Compare the translation to the normal range, and stop if the patient has acute pain or neurological signs. Obliques show the foramina, the swimmer's view shows the lower cervical and upper thoracic region, and a rotational open mouth view addresses atlantoaxial rotation, not sagittal instability.
  21. The correct answer is B (US Department of Health and Human Services, setting ten year objectives for prevention and equity): Healthy People is published by the US Department of Health and Human Services and updated each decade, with 2030 the current edition. It sets measurable objectives for prevention, disease reduction, and health equity. The WHO, CMS, and OSHA have their own roles but do not publish this framework.
  22. The correct answer is A (Struvite staghorn calculus, associated with urease-producing Proteus species): Recurrent infection with an alkaline urine suggests urea-splitting bacteria, usually Proteus, which raise urinary pH and precipitate magnesium ammonium phosphate as a struvite stone. It branches to fill the pelvis and calyces and is radiopaque. Escherichia coli does not split urea, uric acid stones are radiolucent in acidic urine, and cystine stones occur in a hereditary disorder.
  23. The correct answer is A (The presynaptic neuromuscular junction, where it cleaves SNARE proteins and blocks acetylcholine release): Botulinum toxin is a protease that cleaves SNARE proteins in the presynaptic terminal, so acetylcholine vesicles cannot fuse and release transmitter, causing flaccid descending paralysis with autonomic features. Competitive postsynaptic blockade describes curare. Acetylcholinesterase inhibition produces excess acetylcholine and cholinergic signs, and sodium channel block describes tetrodotoxin.
  24.
