The correct answer is B (Elevated absolute eosinophil count, reflecting a Th2 response directed against tissue-migrating multicellular worms): Multicellular helminths that migrate through tissue, such as hookworm and strongyloides, drive Th2 cytokines IL-4 and IL-5, which recruit eosinophils. Eosinophilia is therefore a classic clue to helminths and is usually absent in most protozoal infections that stay within gut lumen or cells. Neutrophilia points to bacteria, monocytosis to chronic intracellular infection, and atypical lymphocytes to viral illness such as Epstein-Barr virus.
  25. The correct answer is D (Refer for same-day blood work and MRI with urgent medical evaluation, and withhold all manipulation): Fever, intravenous drug use, and focal spinal percussion tenderness suggest spinal infection such as vertebral osteomyelitis or discitis. Because he is stable, the right tier is urgent referral for laboratory tests and MRI, which detects early infection. Plain films are normal early and manipulation is inappropriate. Watching and treating in the office delays diagnosis.
  26. The correct answer is A (Right atrium, the chamber that receives systemic venous blood and cardiac venous return): The superior and inferior venae cavae and the coronary sinus all drain into the right atrium, which receives deoxygenated blood from the body and the heart wall. The left atrium receives the pulmonary veins. The ventricles are pumping chambers that receive blood from the atria and do not receive the vena cavae directly.
  27. The correct answer is A (Hip flexion, supplied by the anterior rami of the upper lumbar nerves): The psoas major arises from the lumbar vertebrae and discs, inserts on the lesser trochanter, and is the main hip flexor. It receives direct branches from the anterior rami of L1 to L3, so spasm causes groin and low back pain and pain on resisted hip flexion. Gluteal muscles extend and abduct the hip, and rectus femoris crosses the knee.
  28. The correct answer is C (Achilles tendon rupture, shown by loss of plantar flexion when the calf is squeezed): In the Thompson test a squeeze of the calf normally pulls on an intact Achilles tendon and produces plantar flexion. A missing response after a sudden pop during push-off indicates a complete Achilles rupture. Plantaris rupture and medial gastrocnemius tears hurt but leave the tendon intact, so the squeeze still moves the foot. Posterior tibial tendon failure alters inversion and arch height, not the calf squeeze test.
  29. The correct answer is B (Mitral valve prolapse with mild regurgitation, since reduced preload makes leaflets prolapse earlier): Decreasing left ventricular volume on standing makes the mitral leaflets prolapse earlier, so the click and murmur move toward the first sound. That behavior is typical of mitral valve prolapse. The murmur of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy also grows louder on standing but does not start with a midsystolic click. Aortic stenosis has an early ejection click at the base, and tricuspid regurgitation changes with inspiration rather than posture.
  30. The correct answer is C (Pancoast tumor at the lung apex, compressing the sympathetic chain and causing Horner syndrome.): A lung apex tumor can invade the lower brachial plexus, causing shoulder and inner arm pain, and the sympathetic chain, causing ptosis, miosis and anhidrosis, known together as Horner syndrome. The smoking history raises the likelihood. Thoracic outlet syndrome rarely causes Horner syndrome, a rotator cuff tear would not change the pupil, and cluster headache requires headache.
  31. The correct answer is A (Skeletal metastasis to the thoracic spine, requiring urgent advanced imaging and oncology communication): A cancer history with progressive, nocturnal mid-back pain makes spinal metastasis the leading concern because breast cancer commonly spreads to bone, and recurrence can appear years after treatment. She needs urgent MRI and communication with her oncologist before any manual therapy. Aromatase inhibitors do weaken bone, but that does not explain progressive night pain better than metastasis, and facet or radiation strain are diagnoses of exclusion here.
Case N-B05

Patient: M, 58 y, 70 in, 205 lb

Vitals: Pulse 72, resp 16, BP 136/84 mmHg, temp 98.3 F

Chief complaint and history: House painter, right hand dominant, with 5 months of right shoulder pain that disturbs sleep when he lies on that side. He fell from a ladder onto the outstretched arm and has been unable to lift the arm overhead since. Pain is in the lateral upper arm. No neck pain. No numbness. Takes naproxen occasionally. Prior left rotator cuff tendinitis.

Examination: Right shoulder sits slightly elevated. Supraspinatus and infraspinatus fossae appear hollowed. Active abduction limited to 70 degrees with a shrug, passive abduction reaches 170 degrees. Passive external rotation 70 degrees, full. Mild tenderness at the acromioclavicular joint. Cervical range of motion full.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Drop arm test positive, the arm falls from 90 degrees. Empty can test weak and painful. External rotation lag sign positive at 20 degrees. Neer and Hawkins tests positive. Speed test negative. Apprehension test negative. Spurling test negative. Reflexes 2+ and symmetrical, sensation intact.

Laboratory and imaging: Shoulder radiographs, AP view: acromiohumeral interval 5 mm (normal 7 to 14 mm), humeral head high in the glenoid, small subacromial spur, mild acromioclavicular degenerative change, no fracture. Fasting glucose 101 mg/dL.

  1. The correct answers are C and E: Active motion that is much lower than passive motion points to loss of tendon function rather than a stiff capsule. A positive drop arm test and lag sign show tendon discontinuity, and the 5 mm acromiohumeral interval with a high humeral head shows the cuff no longer depresses the head. Adhesive capsulitis limits passive motion as well. Impingement alone does not cause a drop arm or a lag, and the normal reflexes and sensation argue against C5.
  2. The correct answers are B and E: The drop arm test fails when the supraspinatus cannot hold the arm at 90 degrees of abduction, and the empty can test loads the muscle in internal rotation with the thumb down. Speed and Yergason tests stress the long head of the biceps tendon. The apprehension test assesses anterior glenohumeral stability. The Speed test was negative in this chart.
  3. The correct answers are A and C: MRI or ultrasound shows tear thickness, size and tendon retraction, which determine whether repair is still possible. Surgical referral is justified because a full-thickness tear with weakness in a working adult is unlikely to heal with exercise alone, and retraction and fatty change worsen with delay. The neck examination is normal, so cervical imaging adds nothing. Resisted loading of a torn tendon and a bone scan do not help the decision.
  4. The correct answer is D (Decline to prescribe, explain the legal scope limit, and refer her to a licensed prescriber): Scope of practice is set by state law, and neither patient consent nor a physician's informal approval can enlarge it. The correct response is to refuse politely, explain the limit and refer to someone licensed to prescribe. Dispensing or writing a prescription would be unlicensed practice of medicine, and the low risk of one short course does not change that.
  5. The correct answer is A (The condition is unlikely, because a highly sensitive test rarely misses cases): SnNout means that a sensitive test with a negative result helps rule a condition out, because few true cases are missed. It does not give certainty, since predictive values also depend on prevalence. False negatives are governed by sensitivity, not specificity. Low specificity limits the use of a positive result, not a negative one.
  6. The correct answer is C (Osteoarthritis, with superolateral joint space loss, subchondral sclerosis and marginal osteophytes): Hip osteoarthritis typically narrows the joint space in the superolateral portion, with subchondral sclerosis, cysts, and osteophytes on the femoral head and acetabulum. Rheumatoid arthritis causes uniform concentric narrowing with osteopenia and axial migration. Avascular necrosis shows a subchondral crescent and eventual flattening. Paget disease thickens the cortex and coarsens the trabeculae without primary cartilage loss.
  7. The correct answer is C (Adjust the right side, since the short leg indicates a posterior-inferior ilium on that side): In Thompson analysis the leg that appears shorter prone marks the side of a posterior-inferior ilium, and that same side is adjusted. A listing on the opposite side or an anterior-superior listing would be tied to the long leg. A persistent difference does not by itself prove a structural short leg without further evaluation.
  8. The correct answer is B (Axial rotation, since the sagittal facet orientation blocks one vertebra from twisting on another): Lumbar facets lie close to the sagittal plane, which permits flexion and extension but makes the facet surfaces impact when a vertebra tries to rotate. As a result the lumbar spine has only a few degrees of axial rotation per segment. Flexion and extension are the lumbar spine's greatest motions, and lateral flexion is moderately free.
  9. The correct answer is B (Glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure falls, so filtration rate and plasma flow both decrease): Constricting the afferent arteriole raises resistance upstream of the glomerulus, which lowers blood flow into it and lowers glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure, the main force driving filtration. Both filtration rate and plasma flow therefore fall. Efferent constriction is the action that raises hydrostatic pressure and filtration while lowering plasma flow. Oncotic and capsule pressures are not the changed variables here.
  10. The correct answer is C (Radial nerve, in the spiral groove along the posterior shaft of the humerus): The radial nerve runs in the spiral groove of the posterior humerus, where a mid-shaft fracture can injure it. The result is wrist drop, weak finger extension and numbness on the dorsum of the hand. The ulnar and median nerves do not run in that groove, and the axillary nerve is injured at the surgical neck.
  11. The correct answer is A (Avoid manipulating the involved region and coordinate care with his oncologist using only gentle supportive measures): Spinal metastasis weakens bone and is a contraindication to manipulation of the involved region, whether by thrust or by table-assisted traction. Care should be coordinated with the treating oncologist, with supportive measures only. Calling the pain mechanical delays oncology communication and exposes the vertebrae to a pathologic fracture risk.
  12. The correct answer is B (Precontemplation; offer personalized risk information and ask permission to explore how his habits relate to pain): A patient who does not see a problem and has no intention to change is in precontemplation. The task then is raising awareness with relevant information and exploring his own view, not issuing an exercise program. Prescriptions, start dates and relapse planning belong to later stages and would meet resistance here.
  13. The correct answer is B (Gout, with needle-shaped monosodium urate crystals showing negative birefringence under compensated polarized light): Acute podagra after alcohol and red meat in a patient on a thiazide diuretic points to gout, since thiazides raise uric acid. Joint fluid shows needle-shaped monosodium urate crystals that are negatively birefringent, appearing yellow when parallel to the compensator axis. Pseudogout crystals are rhomboid and positively birefringent. Septic and reactive arthritis lack crystals, though aspiration also rules out infection.
  14. The correct answer is D (It has deoxyribose and thymine, and guanine pairs with cytosine through three hydrogen bonds): DNA contains the sugar deoxyribose and the pyrimidine thymine, while RNA contains ribose and uracil. Guanine pairs with cytosine through three hydrogen bonds, and adenine pairs with thymine through two. Because of the extra bond, regions rich in G-C are more stable and melt at higher temperatures. Each wrong option mixes up the sugar, the base or the bond count.
  15. The correct answer is A (It supports spondyloarthritis but is neither diagnostic nor required, because many healthy people carry the allele): HLA-B27 is present in roughly 8 percent of healthy white people and in most, but not all, patients with axial spondyloarthritis, so a positive result only raises the probability of disease. Diagnosis rests on inflammatory back pain history, sacroiliitis on imaging and clinical features. The allele does not change with inflammation, so repeating it adds nothing, and it neither excludes mechanical pain nor predicts fusion.
  16. The correct answer is A (Rickets, caused by vitamin D deficiency impairing mineralization at the growth plate): Cupped, frayed and splayed metaphyses with widened physes and an indistinct zone of provisional calcification are the radiographic hallmarks of rickets. Vitamin D deficiency prevents mineralization of the growth plate cartilage and osteoid, and risk factors include exclusive breastfeeding, dark skin and little sun. Scurvy produces a ring epiphysis and a white line, and vitamin D excess would produce dense bands rather than widened physes.
  17. The correct answer is B (Suspected bone tumor, so arrange urgent referral for imaging and oncologic evaluation without manipulation): Progressive night pain, swelling, warmth and a firm mass near the metaphysis of the distal femur in a child are red flags for a primary bone tumor such as osteosarcoma and need urgent imaging and referral. Growing pains are intermittent, symmetric and have no mass or swelling. Osgood-Schlatter involves the tibial tubercle, and patellofemoral pain is activity related without a palpable mass, so neither explains the findings and manipulation would be inappropriate.
  18. The correct answer is C (MHC class II molecules, presenting processed exogenous peptide to CD4 helper T cells): Macrophages are professional antigen-presenting cells that load peptides from phagocytosed material onto MHC class II. The CD4 co-receptor binds the class II beta-2 domain, so these complexes engage helper T cells. MHC class I, found on all nucleated cells, presents cytosolic peptides to CD8 cytotoxic T cells. B cells need helper T cell signals, not direct presentation by macrophages, for antibody class switching.
  19. The correct answer is B (Median nerve entrapment beneath the flexor retinaculum, known as carpal tunnel syndrome): Night paresthesia of the lateral three digits relieved by shaking, thenar weakness and a positive Tinel sign at the wrist identify carpal tunnel syndrome. The median nerve runs under the flexor retinaculum. A C6 radiculopathy would be provoked by neck movement. Pronator syndrome and Guyon canal compression involve different areas of the hand.
  20. The correct answer is D (The superior articular process of the same vertebra, projecting upward from the pedicle and pars region): On the oblique lumbar view the transverse process forms the nose, the pedicle forms the eye, the pars interarticularis forms the neck, the superior articular process forms the ear, and the inferior articular process forms the front leg. The ear is the upward projection, so it is the superior articular process. Each wrong choice names a real part of the dog but assigns it to the wrong feature.
  21. The correct answer is D (Quantity and type of melanin each melanocyte makes and passes to keratinocytes): Melanocytes in the basal layer make melanin and transfer it to neighboring keratinocytes. People of different skin tones have similar melanocyte numbers, and color depends on how much melanin is produced and on its type, eumelanin or pheomelanin. Corneum thickness and dermal blood flow modify appearance but do not set baseline pigmentation.
  22. The correct answer is C (Ribs 8 through 10, whose cartilages join the costal cartilage of the rib above): Ribs 1 to 7 are true (vertebrosternal) ribs, ribs 8 to 10 are false (vertebrochondral) ribs that join the cartilage above, and ribs 11 and 12 are floating (vertebral) ribs with free anterior ends. The false ribs therefore start at rib 8, not at 7. Ribs 11 and 12 are classed as floating, not false, in this scheme.
  23. The correct answer is C (Improved motor unit recruitment and synchronization, giving more neural drive before measurable fiber hypertrophy): Early strength gains in untrained lifters come mainly from neural adaptations, including better recruitment, rate coding and synchronization of motor units. Measurable fiber hypertrophy generally follows after several weeks of training. Hyperplasia is minimal in adult humans, and mitochondrial or capillary changes mainly improve endurance rather than maximal force.
  24. The correct answer is B (Refer to urology for MRI-guided biopsy, since a hard nodule is suspicious despite the normal PSA): A hard, irregular nodule on rectal examination is suspicious for prostate cancer, and a PSA below 4 ng/mL does not exclude it because a meaningful share of cancers occur at lower values. The finding warrants urologic referral for biopsy, with MRI to target tissue sampling. An alpha blocker treats voiding symptoms from hyperplasia, not a nodule, and chronic prostatitis would not present as a painless hard nodule.
  25. The correct answer is D (His father, because a first-degree male relative affected before age 55 meets the definition): Premature coronary disease in the family history means a first-degree male relative affected before age 55 or a first-degree female relative affected before age 65. His father at 52 qualifies. His mother at 67 is past the female cut-off, and the uncle and grandfather are second-degree relatives, so neither counts regardless of age.
  26. The correct answer is D (Contemplation, so explore ambivalence and weigh pros and cons through motivational interviewing): She recognizes the need to exercise but is not ready to commit, which defines contemplation. In this stage the most effective approach is to explore ambivalence and the pros and cons of changing, without pushing a plan. Precontemplation would involve denial of the problem, preparation involves small steps already planned, and action involves changes already under way.
  27. The correct answer is D (G6PD deficiency, because too little NADPH is made to keep glutathione reduced under oxidant stress): Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase is the first enzyme of the pentose phosphate pathway and the main source of red cell NADPH. NADPH keeps glutathione reduced, which neutralizes hydrogen peroxide. Fava beans impose oxidant stress, so hemoglobin precipitates as Heinz bodies and splenic macrophages remove the damaged cells as bite cells. Pyruvate kinase deficiency and spherocytosis lack the oxidant trigger, and methemoglobin reductase deficiency gives cyanosis.
  28. The correct answer is D (Central medullary chemoreceptors, detecting higher cerebrospinal fluid hydrogen ion concentration after CO2 crosses the barrier): CO2 diffuses across the blood brain barrier and combines with water to raise cerebrospinal fluid hydrogen ion concentration, which the central chemoreceptors sense. This is the dominant drive to breathe under normal conditions. Peripheral chemoreceptors respond mainly to low PaO2, and baroreceptors and stretch receptors do not detect CO2.
  29. The correct answer is A (Rheumatoid arthritis, with symmetric marginal erosions at the metacarpophalangeal joints): Symmetric involvement of the MCP joints and wrists, periarticular osteopenia and marginal erosions at the bare areas fit rheumatoid arthritis, supported by morning stiffness and a positive rheumatoid factor. Osteoarthritis favors the DIP and first carpometacarpal joints with osteophytes. Psoriatic arthritis is typically asymmetric and distal, and gout has overhanging edge erosions with preserved bone density.
  30. The correct answer is A (Two lobes, a lingula and a cardiac notch on its anterior border): The left lung has an oblique fissure only, two lobes, a lingula and a cardiac notch where the heart indents the anterior border. The right lung has three lobes, a horizontal and an oblique fissure and a middle lobe. The right lung is also larger and its main bronchus is wider and shorter, so those features belong on the right.
  31. The correct answer is C (Spondylolysis, a repetitive extension stress fracture of the pars interarticularis in a lower lumbar vertebra): Repetitive extension and landing in a young athlete stress the pars interarticularis, and pain with the one-legged hyperextension test is the classic provocation. A disc herniation tends to hurt with flexion and shows positive straight leg raise or root signs, which are absent here. Facet syndrome is difficult to separate by examination, and Scheuermann disease affects the thoracic spine.
  32. The correct answer is D (Oblique view, with uncovertebral and facet osteophytes encroaching on the intervertebral foramen): The intervertebral foramina in the cervical spine face obliquely, so they are best seen on oblique projections at about 45 degrees. Foraminal narrowing at C5-6 usually results from uncovertebral (Luschka) joint osteophytes anteriorly and facet joint hypertrophy posteriorly, often with disc height loss. The lateral view shows canal diameter and disc height, and the AP and open mouth views do not profile the foramina.
  33. The correct answer is B (Some trials suggest fewer recurrences with periodic care, but evidence is limited, so decide together): A few trials suggest periodic care may reduce recurrences or disability days, yet the evidence is limited and of variable quality. The honest counsel is a shared decision with periodic reassessment and clear stopping criteria. Claims of proven prevention or of visceral disease prevention go beyond the data, and a flat refusal ignores the available trials.
  34. The correct answer is D (Place a palm over the twelfth rib beside the spine and strike it with a fist): Costovertebral angle percussion is done over the twelfth rib and the angle beside the spine, where the kidney lies, using the ulnar side of a fist against a flat hand. Tenderness suggests pyelonephritis or another renal process. Spinous process percussion tests bone, not kidney. Iliac crest pressure and lateral abdominal percussion do not reach the renal angle.
  35. The correct answer is B (Cardiac tamponade from blood accumulating within the pericardial sac): Hypotension, distended neck veins and muffled heart sounds form Beck triad, and a systolic drop above 10 mmHg on inspiration is pulsus paradoxus, together diagnostic of cardiac tamponade after blunt chest trauma. Tension pneumothorax also gives neck vein distension but with absent breath sounds and tracheal deviation. Constrictive pericarditis evolves over months, and infarction would produce crackles from pulmonary congestion.
  36. The correct answer is C (Meiotic nondisjunction of chromosome 21, usually in the maternal oocyte, giving a free extra copy): About 95 percent of Down syndrome cases are free trisomy 21 from meiotic nondisjunction, mostly maternal, with risk rising with maternal age. Robertsonian translocation causes about 4 percent and is the type with a recurrence risk linked to a carrier parent. Mosaicism is about 1 percent, and paternal isochromosome is not the usual cause.
  37. The correct answer is B (4,200 mL/min, from 350 mL of fresh air reaching alveoli per breath times 12 breaths): Alveolar ventilation equals (tidal volume minus dead space) times respiratory rate. Here (500 mL minus 150 mL) is 350 mL per breath, and 350 mL times 12 breaths is 4,200 mL/min. The 6,000 mL/min value is total minute ventilation, which counts air that never reaches gas exchange surfaces. The 1,800 mL/min and 7,800 mL/min values come from using or adding the dead space instead of subtracting it.
  38. The correct answer is B (Squamous cell carcinoma of the epidermis with potential for regional metastasis): A scaly, ulcerated, enlarging nodule on a sun-exposed ear with a regional node suggests squamous cell carcinoma, which arises from actinic keratosis and can metastasize. Basal cell carcinoma is usually pearly and seldom spreads. Melanoma is typically pigmented, and seborrheic keratosis is benign and does not cause adenopathy.
  39. The correct answer is B (Trichinella spiralis, with larvae encysting in striated muscle after ingestion of infected meat): Periorbital edema, myositis with high creatine kinase, fever and marked eosinophilia after undercooked pork are classic for trichinellosis. Larvae released from ingested meat penetrate intestinal mucosa, travel through blood and encyst in striated muscle. The Taenia solium adult tapeworm causes only mild gut symptoms. Ascaris comes from soil eggs, and Toxoplasma is a protozoan that does not usually produce eosinophilia.
Case N-B07

Patient: M, 24 y, 70 in, 165 lb

Vitals: Pulse 68, resp 14, BP 118/72 mmHg, temp 98.2 F

Chief complaint and history: Graduate student with 8 months of low back and deep buttock pain, worst on waking, with stiffness lasting about 90 minutes. Pain eases with a jog or hot shower and returns after long lectures. Wakes at 4 AM with pain that alternates buttocks. No trauma. Ibuprofen helps. Mother has psoriasis. One red, painful left eye episode last winter.

Examination: Lumbar flexion limited. Modified Schober test increases only 3 cm from a 10 cm mark (normal over 5 cm). Chest expansion at the nipple line is 2 cm (normal 5 cm or more). Lumbar extension and lateral flexion reduced. Spinal percussion nontender. Gait normal. Mild loss of lumbar lordosis. Peripheral joints without swelling.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Straight leg raise negative bilaterally. FABER test produces right buttock pain. Sacroiliac compression and Gaenslen test mildly positive bilaterally. Femoral stretch negative. Patellar and Achilles reflexes 2+ and equal. Lower limb strength 5/5. Sensation intact. Heel palpation nontender.

Laboratory and imaging: No studies obtained yet. A CBC at a student clinic last year was reported normal. No prior spinal or pelvic films. Skin clear, cardiac auscultation normal, urinalysis negative.

  1. The correct answers are A and D: Inflammatory back pain begins insidiously before age 40, is worst after rest or at night, and produces stiffness longer than 30 minutes that eases with activity. This patient shows all of those features. Relief with rest and pain worsened by exertion describe mechanical pain, which is the opposite of the chart. A negative straight leg raise only argues against a lumbar root lesion and says nothing about inflammatory disease, and no radicular pattern appears in the chart.
  2. The correct answers are B and D: Young age, inflammatory pain, reduced chest expansion, reduced lumbar flexion, sacroiliac provocation and a probable prior anterior uveitis point to axial spondyloarthritis, typically ankylosing spondylitis. The sacroiliac joints are the earliest site, and restricted chest expansion shows costovertebral involvement. Disc herniation would show radicular signs and positive tension tests. Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis occurs in older patients and spares the sacroiliac joints, and facet pain does not improve with exercise.
  3. The correct answers are A and C: HLA-B27 is present in most patients with ankylosing spondylitis, though it is also found in many healthy people, so it supports rather than proves the diagnosis. Radiographs of the sacroiliac joints show the earliest definite change, with erosion, sclerosis and eventual fusion. Rheumatoid factor and anti-CCP belong to rheumatoid arthritis workups, and the antinuclear antibody is nonspecific. Stress films evaluate instability, which is not the question here.
  4. The correct answer is A (The medial branch of the dorsal ramus, which supplies two adjacent facet joints along the column): The facet joints are innervated by the medial branches of the dorsal rami, and each joint receives fibers from the branches at its own level and the level above. A medial branch block anesthetizes those fibers, so relief confirms a facet pain source. The sinuvertebral nerve serves the posterior disc and ligament, and the lateral branch and ventral ramus serve muscle, skin and the limb.
  5. The correct answer is A (Hydroxylation of proline and lysine in procollagen, which needs ascorbic acid to keep enzyme iron reduced): Prolyl and lysyl hydroxylases add hydroxyl groups to procollagen, and they need ascorbic acid to keep their iron in the reduced state. Without hydroxylation, collagen cannot form stable triple helices, so vessels, gums and wounds are fragile. This produces gingival bleeding, perifollicular hemorrhage, corkscrew hairs and poor healing, which is scurvy. Vitamin K deficiency alters clotting factors, and lysyl oxidase depends on copper rather than vitamin C.
  6. The correct answer is C (Black tarry stools, indicating melena from probable upper GI bleeding): Black tarry stools are melena, meaning digested blood from the upper gastrointestinal tract, and chronic high-dose NSAID use is a common cause through ulceration. Fatigue may reflect anemia from that bleeding. The back pain is the presenting complaint, and the dose is a risk factor rather than the red flag itself.
  7. The correct answer is B (Assist, by helping the patient set a quit date and supplying resources): Assist means helping the patient prepare to quit, which includes setting a quit date, offering medication information and providing counseling or quitline resources. Advise is the clear statement that quitting is important, and assess is checking willingness to try. Arrange is the follow up contact after the quit date, which was not described in the exchange.
  8. The correct answer is C (Left conductive hearing loss, with bone conduction exceeding air conduction and Weber lateralizing to that ear): A negative Rinne test, with bone louder than air, shows the middle ear or canal is not transmitting air-conducted sound. In conductive loss the Weber lateralizes to the impaired ear because it is masked from background noise. Sensorineural loss gives a positive Rinne with Weber lateralizing to the healthy ear. A normal Rinne has air conduction louder than bone.
  9. The correct answer is A (Rheumatoid arthritis, because erosions at the joint margins, periarticular osteopenia and uniform narrowing involve both hands): Rheumatoid arthritis begins in the small joints of the hands, usually symmetrically at the metacarpophalangeal and proximal interphalangeal joints. Marginal erosions occur at the bare areas, joint space narrows uniformly, and periarticular osteopenia is present. Osteoarthritis spares the knuckles, gout gives overhanging edges, and psoriatic arthritis targets the end joints with normal density.
  10. The correct answer is C (Gastric adenocarcinoma, spreading through the thoracic duct to the sentinel node known as Virchow node): A left supraclavicular node, Virchow node, signals abdominal malignancy, classically gastric, because the thoracic duct empties near the left venous angle. Early satiety and weight loss support gastric cancer. Oral and nasopharyngeal cancers drain to higher cervical chains, and papillary thyroid carcinoma spreads to central compartment nodes.
  11. The correct answer is C (A false rib, attaching indirectly through the costal cartilage of the rib above): Ribs 1 to 7 are true ribs with their own cartilages to the sternum, ribs 8 to 10 are false ribs that join the cartilage of the rib above, and ribs 11 and 12 are floating. The ninth rib is therefore a false rib. Atypical refers to ribs 1, 2, 10, 11 and 12, which differ in their vertebral articulation, not to the anterior attachment.
  12. The correct answer is D (Adjustment twice weekly for four weeks, home exercises, and repeat Oswestry at the eighth visit): The Plan states what will be done: the treatment, home care, visit frequency and duration, and when the patient will be re-examined with outcome measures. The pain rating and its aggravating factors belong in the Subjective section. The measured motion and straight leg raise belong in the Objective section. The working diagnosis belongs in the Assessment section.
  13. The correct answer is A (Ulnar nerve compression in the cubital tunnel at the medial elbow): Ring and small finger numbness, a positive Froment sign and a Tinel sign at the medial elbow after prolonged flexion indicate ulnar neuropathy at the cubital tunnel. The dorsal ulnar sensory branch leaves before Guyon canal, so involvement of the dorsal hand puts the lesion proximal to the wrist. A C8 root or lower plexus lesion would add other sensory and motor findings.
  14. The correct answer is B (Hemisection of the spinal cord, a Brown-Sequard lesion, on the right side): A cord hemisection interrupts the ipsilateral corticospinal tract and dorsal columns, giving same-side weakness and loss of vibration and proprioception. Spinothalamic fibers cross within a segment or two of entry, so pain and temperature loss appears on the opposite side. Anterior cord syndrome spares vibration and position sense, and complete transection abolishes everything bilaterally. Central cord syndrome mainly weakens the arms.
  15. The correct answer is D (ACE inhibitor induced cough from bradykinin accumulation in the airway): A dry, persistent cough that begins weeks after starting an ACE inhibitor is a class effect from accumulation of bradykinin and substance P in the airway, found in about 10 to 15 percent of patients. Reflux and postnasal drip are common causes of chronic cough, but she has no heartburn or nasal symptoms. Cough variant asthma would show episodic triggers. Stopping the ACE inhibitor and switching to an ARB resolves the cough.
  16. The correct answer is A (Structural scoliosis, since fixed vertebral rotation produces a rib prominence that persists in forward bending): In structural scoliosis the vertebrae rotate and the curve is fixed, so the ribs on the convex side form a hump that persists when the patient bends forward and does not correct on side bending. A postural or functional curve straightens on forward bending or when the cause is removed. Scheuermann disease affects the sagittal plane and does not produce this pattern.
  17. The correct answer is B (Record right lower quadrant rebound at McBurney point as a peritoneal sign needing emergency referral.): McBurney point lies one third of the way from the right anterior superior iliac spine to the umbilicus, and pain on release, called rebound, signals peritoneal irritation. The note should name the side, quadrant and point, and the plan is emergency referral for suspected appendicitis. Murphy sign belongs to the right upper quadrant, and a strain or left sided diverticulitis ignores the stated location and rebound.
  18. The correct answer is C (Arrange emergency transfer for CT angiography of the chest and keep him still): Sudden tearing interscapular pain, severe hypertension, a blood pressure difference between arms, and a widened mediastinum point to aortic dissection, which is a life-threatening emergency. CT angiography confirms it, and the patient needs emergency transfer without manipulation. A normal bone appearance does not exclude a vascular cause. Disc imaging and delayed repeat films waste time that the patient does not have.
  19. The correct answer is C (Measure carboxyhemoglobin by co-oximetry and give 100 percent oxygen, using hyperbaric therapy in severe cases): Carbon monoxide poisoning from a faulty furnace gives headache, nausea and confusion in several people at once. Pulse oximetry reads falsely normal because it cannot tell carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin, and a cherry red color is uncommon. Co-oximetry confirms it, and 100 percent oxygen shortens the half-life of carboxyhemoglobin. The other tests treat different poisons.
  20. The correct answer is A (A luteinizing hormone surge, triggered by sustained high estradiol giving positive feedback to the pituitary): Late in the follicular phase the dominant follicle secretes high estradiol for about two days, which switches pituitary feedback from negative to positive. This causes a large LH surge that triggers follicular rupture roughly 30 to 36 hours later. A corpus luteum does not exist until after ovulation, so a luteal progesterone surge cannot be the trigger. Urine ovulation tests detect this LH surge.
  21. The correct answer is A (At least 150 minutes of moderate aerobic activity weekly, plus muscle strengthening on two days): Federal guidelines advise adults to accumulate at least 150 minutes of moderate intensity aerobic activity per week, or 75 minutes of vigorous activity, plus muscle strengthening on two or more days. The 75 minute figure applies only to vigorous work, so it cannot be used for moderate activity. Sixty vigorous minutes falls short, and 300 minutes is the level for added benefit, not the minimum.
  22. The correct answer is D (Multiple myeloma, because the lesions are purely osteolytic with little osteoblastic activity to take up tracer): Multiple punched out lytic skull lesions without a sclerotic rim, with anemia, hypercalcemia and renal impairment, indicate multiple myeloma. Bone scanning depends on osteoblastic repair, and myeloma lesions have very little, so the scan is often falsely negative. This is why a skeletal survey is the preferred imaging test. Paget disease shows intense uptake, especially in the lytic phase, so it would not explain a negative scan.
  23. The correct answer is C (Cervical nerve root compression in a narrowed right intervertebral foramen): Spurling combines extension, ipsilateral rotation, lateral flexion and axial compression to narrow the intervertebral foramen. Radiating arm pain on that side indicates compression of a cervical nerve root. Vertebral artery insufficiency would produce dizziness or nystagmus, not radicular pain. Cord compression and thoracic outlet syndrome are assessed with other tests.
  24. The correct answer is B (Symptoms persisting beyond 10 days without any improvement meet the duration criterion for a bacterial cause): Viral rhinosinusitis usually improves by 7 to 10 days, so symptoms lasting more than 10 days without improvement, or a second worsening after initial improvement, suggest bacterial infection. Four weeks marks the boundary between acute and subacute disease, not between viral and bacterial causes. Colored discharge in the first days of an ordinary cold is common and does not indicate bacteria. One day of low-grade fever does not discriminate between the two.
  25. The correct answer is B (Bronchogenic carcinoma, whose risk is multiplied when asbestos exposure is combined with cigarette smoking): Bronchogenic carcinoma is the most common asbestos-associated cancer, and the risk with smoking is multiplicative rather than additive. Mesothelioma is strongly specific to asbestos but is less common than lung cancer and is not made more likely by smoking. Pleural plaques are benign fibrocalcific lesions and do not transform. Asbestos is not linked to hepatocellular carcinoma.
  26. The correct answer is B (Cervical artery dissection, so withhold manipulation and arrange emergency vascular imaging with hospital evaluation): Sudden severe unilateral neck pain, a new headache, and a partial Horner sign after minor trauma point to dissection of the carotid or vertebral artery with sympathetic involvement. Manipulation could extend the intimal tear, and stroke risk is highest in the first weeks. She needs emergency CT or MR angiography, not a neck adjustment.
  27. The correct answer is A (Holocrine secretion, in which whole secretory cells disintegrate to release sebum and cell debris): Sebaceous glands release sebum by holocrine secretion, in which the entire secretory cell disintegrates. They are associated with hair follicles and are absent from the palms and soles. Eccrine sweat glands use merocrine exocytosis from intact cells, and apocrine secretion, in classical teaching, loses only the apical cell portion. Paracrine describes local signaling, not the way a gland releases its product.
  28. The correct answer is D (About 10 percent, from incidence multiplied by the average duration of disease): At steady state, prevalence is approximately incidence times average duration. Here 20 per 1,000 per year is 0.02, and 0.02 times 5 years is 0.10, or 10 percent. Dividing by duration gives 0.4 percent, which would be a rate of recovery, not a pool of existing cases. Using incidence alone ignores how long cases accumulate, and reading 20 per 1,000 as 20 percent is a unit error.
  29. The correct answer is D (Primary prevention, because it reduces exposure to risk factors before any disorder has developed): Primary prevention acts on healthy people to prevent a condition from starting, and ergonomic education for a symptom-free worker fits that definition. Secondary prevention finds disease early, and tertiary prevention limits the consequences of established disease. Quaternary prevention concerns avoiding harm from unnecessary medical care, which is not the aim of this advice.
  30. The correct answer is A (Segmental malalignment from ligament injury with facet subluxation, so immobilize and obtain CT and MRI): Both the posterior vertebral body line (George line) and the spinolaminar line should be smooth, and a step over about 3.5 mm indicates anterolisthesis from posterior ligament disruption and facet dislocation or subluxation. After diving, such an injury is unstable and may threaten the cord. The neck stays immobilized, and CT shows the facets while MRI shows ligaments and the cord.
  31. The correct answer is C (Cortisol binds an intracellular receptor that alters transcription, while glucagon raises cAMP through a membrane receptor): Cortisol is a lipophilic steroid hormone that crosses the membrane, binds an intracellular receptor and changes transcription. Glucagon is a peptide hormone that binds a G protein coupled receptor, activates adenylate cyclase and raises cyclic AMP and protein kinase A activity. Peptide hormones cannot cross the membrane, and tyrosine kinase signaling is the mechanism of insulin and growth factors.
  32. The correct answer is D (Slightly anterior to the lateral malleolus, after passing slightly anterior to the center of the knee): In the lateral view the ideal plumb line passes through the ear canal, the acromion and the greater trochanter, slightly anterior to the center of the knee and slightly anterior to the lateral malleolus. A line falling behind the malleolus or through the heel suggests the body weight is shifted backward. Forward head posture and an anterior pelvic tilt move segments away from this line.
  33. The correct answer is D (Right straight leg raise positive at 40 degrees, reproducing buttock and posterior thigh pain): A good objective entry names the test, the side, the angle at which the response occurred, and what the patient felt, so another clinician can repeat and compare it. Writing only that the test was positive loses the angle and symptom location. A diagnosis such as L5 disc herniation belongs in the Assessment, not as the finding. Calling the limit hamstring tightness reinterprets a response in which the patient reproduced his familiar buttock and thigh pain.
  34. The correct answer is D (The L4 spinous process or the L4 to L5 interspace): The intercristal line, also called Tuffier line, crosses the midline at the L4 spinous process or the L4 to L5 interspace in most adults. Going up one or two spaces reaches L3 to L4 or L2 to L3 for the puncture. The subcostal plane sits near L3, and the lumbosacral junction lies below the intercristal line.
  35. The correct answer is D (Osteoporosis by the WHO definition, since the T score is at or below minus 2.5): WHO criteria define osteoporosis as a T score of minus 2.5 or lower compared with a young adult mean, so minus 2.7 qualifies. Osteopenia covers minus 1.0 to minus 2.5, and the Z score compares a patient to age matched peers and is not the diagnostic tool in a postmenopausal woman. A fragility fracture adds the term severe or established osteoporosis, but the diagnosis does not wait for one.
  36. The correct answer is C (Limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis, the CREST pattern, with a later risk of pulmonary arterial hypertension): Anti-centromere antibodies mark limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis, which produces calcinosis, Raynaud phenomenon, esophageal dysmotility, sclerodactyly and telangiectasia. Skin involvement stays distal, and the main late hazard is pulmonary arterial hypertension. Diffuse disease carries anti-topoisomerase I antibodies and early renal crisis. Mixed connective tissue disease requires anti-U1 RNP, and lupus is defined by anti-dsDNA.
  37. The correct answer is B (Resistance rises sixteenfold, because it varies inversely with the fourth power of radius): Poiseuille's law states that resistance is proportional to length and viscosity divided by the fourth power of radius. Halving the radius multiplies the denominator by one-half to the fourth power, which is one-sixteenth, so resistance rises sixteenfold. The twofold, fourfold, and eightfold answers use exponents of one, two, and three. This steep dependence is why small arteriolar diameter changes control blood pressure.
  38. The correct answer is B (3 MHz, because the energy is absorbed in the first 1 to 2 cm of tissue): Ultrasound frequency sets penetration depth: 3 MHz is absorbed mostly in the superficial 1 to 2 cm, while 1 MHz reaches about 3 to 5 cm. The Achilles midportion is superficial, so 3 MHz delivers the heat and mechanical effects where the tendon actually is. Using 1 MHz sends most of the energy past the target into deeper tissue, and attributing deep penetration to 3 MHz reverses the physics.
Case N-B15

Patient: F, 36 y, 64 in, 135 lb

Vitals: Pulse 68, resp 14, BP 118/74 mmHg, temp 98.3 F

Chief complaint and history: Bookkeeper. Two weeks of right neck pain spreading to a headache from the base of the skull to behind the right eye, 5/10, after a month-end deadline. Starts after long periods of neck flexion at a screen, eased by lying flat. No aura, nausea, photophobia or visual change. No prior migraines. Acetaminophen helps partly.

Examination: Cervical rotation right 40 degrees, left 70 degrees; right rotation reproduces the head pain. Suboccipital tenderness on the right. Cranial nerves II to XII intact. Upper limb reflexes 2+, strength 5/5. No scalp or temporal artery tenderness. Gait normal.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Flexion rotation test: right 22 degrees, left 40 degrees, familiar headache on the right. Segmental palpation of C1-C2 on the right reproduces the headache. Cervical distraction relieves neck pain. Spurling negative. Sustained end range rotation causes no dizziness or nystagmus.

Laboratory and imaging: No imaging indicated by history. ESR 10 mm/h. No fever, no jaw claudication, no sudden severe onset, no trauma.

  1. The correct answers are A and B: Cervicogenic headache is unilateral, starts in the neck or occiput, and is provoked or reproduced by neck movement or posture with reduced cervical motion. Absence of aura and photophobia argues against migraine. Stress at a deadline, analgesic response and a normal neurological exam occur across primary headaches and do not discriminate.
  2. The correct answers are D and E: The flexion rotation test isolates C1-C2 rotation, and a clear restriction on the painful side with headache reproduction supports an upper cervical source. Manual palpation of C1-C2 that reproduces the familiar headache adds specificity. Spurling targets lower cervical roots, distraction relief is nonspecific, and a negative vertebral artery screen only supports safety.
  3. The correct answers are C and E: Manual therapy to the upper cervical segments combined with deep neck flexor training and ergonomic correction addresses both the source and the cause, and a response is expected within a few weeks. Red flags such as thunderclap onset, fever or deficit need urgent referral. Daily forceful thrusts are excessive, unilateral pain alone does not justify MRI, and ignoring the workstation invites relapse.
  4. The correct answer is C (Paget disease of bone, where accelerated osteoblastic remodeling raises ALP while calcium and phosphate stay normal): A markedly high ALP with normal GGT, calcium, phosphate and PTH, plus a warm bowed tibia and enlarging skull, is classic for Paget disease, where turnover is high but the mineral balance is normal. A normal GGT excludes the liver as the source, hyperparathyroidism would raise calcium and PTH, and osteomalacia typically lowers calcium and phosphate. Radiographs would be expected to show thickened cortices and coarse trabeculae.
  5. The correct answer is C (Oxidation of hypoxanthine to xanthine and of xanthine to uric acid by xanthine oxidase): Allopurinol and its metabolite inhibit xanthine oxidase, which catalyzes both oxidation steps that end in uric acid. Blocking it lowers serum urate and allows the more soluble precursors hypoxanthine and xanthine to be excreted. HGPRT deficiency would increase urate production, not reduce it. URAT1 blockade is the uricosuric mechanism and would raise urinary uric acid.
  6. The correct answer is B (Perforated peptic ulcer with peritonitis, with upright chest film and emergency surgical evaluation): Sudden severe pain, a rigid abdomen with rebound and absent bowel sounds in a patient with ulcer history indicate perforation with chemical peritonitis. An upright chest film showing free subdiaphragmatic air supports it, and this is a surgical emergency. Pancreatitis may be tender but rarely board-like. Obstruction presents with colic, vomiting and distension, and diverticulitis would localize to the left lower quadrant.
  7. The correct answer is C (Nucleic acid amplification of a vaginal swab, with doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for one week): Mucopurulent cervicitis with a silent course in a young woman suggests Chlamydia trachomatis, the most commonly reported bacterial STD. Nucleic acid amplification on urine or a vaginal swab is the most sensitive test, and doxycycline for seven days is first line. Gram stain with ceftriaxone targets gonorrhea. Metronidazole treats trichomonas, and acyclovir treats herpes.
  8. The correct answer is D (Only slight agreement beyond chance, so the test has poor reproducibility between examiners): Kappa measures agreement beyond chance, and a value near 0.2 is conventionally labeled slight agreement. Raw percent agreement can look high even when kappa is low, because chance agreement is high. The value does not mean twenty percent extra concordance, and prevalence cannot turn a slight value into a strong one.
  9. The correct answer is D (C3, C4 and C5, which give rise to the nerve to the diaphragm): The phrenic nerve arises from the C3, C4 and C5 ventral rami, which is summarized as keeping the diaphragm alive. It supplies the diaphragm motor and sensory fibers, so a cord injury at or above this level causes respiratory paralysis. C5 to C7 contribute to brachial plexus nerves, C1 to C3 form upper cervical branches, and T7 to T11 supply intercostal muscles, so those levels would leave diaphragmatic breathing intact.
  10. The correct answer is B (Mammillary process of the segment, with rotation limited to tissue tension rather than end range): The mammillary process lies on the lumbar superior articular process, which gives a stable lever for a rotary thrust. Rotation is taken only to the point of tissue tension at the target segment, since twisting to end range loads the disc and adjacent joints without improving specificity. Extending the upper leg moves the tension away from the lumbar segment, and spinous or transverse contacts do not match the diversified technique described.
  11. The correct answer is A (Neuropathic arthropathy of the midfoot, from loss of protective sensation due to peripheral neuropathy): The six Ds of a neuropathic joint are distended joint, increased density, debris, dislocation, disorganization, and destruction, and the Lisfranc region is a classic site in diabetic neuropathy. Loss of pain and position sense allows repetitive microtrauma, producing a relatively painless but destroyed joint. Osteomyelitis and septic arthritis are usually painful with systemic signs, and gout favors the first metatarsophalangeal joint with punched-out erosions.
  12. The correct answer is C (Hypothyroidism; refer for thyroid function tests and medical management, while co-managing musculoskeletal care): Weight gain, cold intolerance, constipation, dry skin, bradycardia and a slow relaxation phase of the Achilles reflex form a classic pattern for hypothyroidism, which is confirmed with thyroid function tests and managed medically. Hyperthyroidism would bring weight loss, heat intolerance, tachycardia and brisk reflexes. Depression would not explain bradycardia and delayed reflex relaxation, and adrenal insufficiency usually presents with hypotension and electrolyte abnormalities.
  13. The correct answer is D (A 71-year-old man with a cardiac pacemaker implanted four years ago): Cardiac pacemakers can malfunction, heat or move in the magnetic field, so the scan is held until the exact device is identified and cleared. Older ferromagnetic cerebral aneurysm clips carry the same concern. Titanium joint replacements, nonferrous fracture plates and routine dental fillings are not ferromagnetic and are generally safe, though they may cause local image artifact.
  14. The correct answer is B (Dormant hypnozoites in liver hepatocytes that later reactivate, a stage that chloroquine alone does not clear): The female Anopheles mosquito injects sporozoites that travel to the liver; in Plasmodium vivax and ovale, some become hypnozoites that lie dormant in hepatocytes. Reactivation months later reseeds the blood and causes a relapse. Chloroquine kills only blood stages, so a drug such as primaquine is needed to clear hypnozoites. Splenic gametocytes, brain sequestration of falciparum, and dormant sporozoites in the bite wound do not account for vivax relapse.
  15. The correct answer is C (Histamine, which makes venular endothelial cells contract and widen their gaps, letting protein-rich fluid escape): Mast cell granules release histamine within minutes, and it acts on venules to open endothelial junctions, so plasma leaks and a wheal forms. This immediate transient response differs from later events. Interleukin-1 mediates fever, leukotriene B4 recruits neutrophils, and platelet-derived growth factor belongs to tissue repair.
  16. The correct answer is B (Small motor neurons supplying slow type I fibers fire first, larger ones added as force rises): Smaller motor neurons have higher input resistance and reach threshold with less synaptic drive, so they fire first. They supply slow, fatigue-resistant type I fibers that generate small forces. As demand rises, progressively larger neurons supplying type IIa and then IIx fibers are recruited. This orderly recruitment allows smooth gradation of force and sparing of fast fibers for strong efforts.
  17. The correct answer is C (Axial spondyloarthritis (ankylosing spondylitis) with sacroiliac and spinal involvement): Onset before age 40, insidious course, morning stiffness over 30 minutes, improvement with exercise and not rest, night pain and reduced chest expansion define inflammatory back pain from ankylosing spondylitis. Mechanical strain and spondylolysis worsen with activity and ease with rest. Disc herniation would add radicular signs. Reduced chest expansion under 2.5 cm is a classic supporting finding.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Transverse ligament laxity at the atlantoaxial joint, probably from inflammatory erosion): In adults the atlantodental interval should not exceed 3 mm, so 4 mm in flexion that narrows in extension points to transverse ligament laxity. Rheumatoid arthritis commonly erodes this ligament through synovitis around the dens. A fracture would show a cortical break, and frank rupture usually produces an interval above 5 mm. The 5 mm figure is the pediatric limit and does not apply to this woman.
  2. The correct answer is B (Ventilation continues while perfusion is blocked, so the ratio rises toward infinity as alveolar dead space): An embolus blocks blood flow to alveoli that are still ventilated, so the ventilation to perfusion ratio rises toward infinity, which is dead space. Hypoxemia results from redirected flow and mismatch elsewhere in the lung. A ratio approaching zero describes shunt, as with airway obstruction or consolidation. Proportional loss would leave the ratio normal, but an embolus blocks only perfusion.
  3. The correct answer is B (Teach abdominal bracing or transversus drawing-in in hook-lying with the spine held neutral): Stabilization training starts with learning to co-contract the deep trunk muscles while the spine stays neutral, using bracing or drawing-in in an unloaded position such as hook-lying. Once she can hold that contraction, bridging and quadruped limb lifts add load and challenge. Sit-ups and double leg raises load the spine through global muscles before control is learned, and sustained flexion holds are stretching, not stabilization.
  4. The correct answer is C (Marrow fat released into the joint, typically from an intra-articular tibial plateau fracture): On a cross-table lateral the beam is horizontal, so a fat-fluid level shows fat floating above blood in the suprapatellar pouch. It means a fracture has entered the joint and released marrow fat, and in the knee that is most often a tibial plateau or distal femoral fracture. Septic arthritis and pigmented villonodular synovitis do not float fat, and a ruptured cyst gives calf findings.
  5. The correct answer is B (Teach back, which checks that the doctor explained the plan clearly enough.): Teach back asks the patient to restate instructions in their own words, so the doctor can find and fix gaps in the explanation. It tests the doctor's communication, not the patient's memory. Active listening reflects feelings, motivational interviewing explores readiness to change, and closed ended questions invite short answers that cannot show understanding of a plan.
  6. The correct answer is D (Autoimmune T cell mediated destruction of pancreatic beta cells, producing absolute insulin deficiency and ketosis): Type 1 diabetes results from autoimmune destruction of beta cells in genetically susceptible individuals, so insulin is absent and ketoacidosis occurs. Islet autoantibodies and young age support this. Insulin resistance with amyloid describes type 2 disease. Glucagon excess worsens hyperglycemia but is not the primary defect, and receptor autoantibodies cause a rare resistance syndrome.
  7. The correct answer is C (Subacromial impingement involving the supraspinatus tendon and the subacromial bursa): Neer and Hawkins squeeze the supraspinatus tendon and bursa beneath the coracoacromial arch, so pain with both and normal strength fits subacromial impingement. A complete tear would show marked weakness and a drop arm sign. Acromioclavicular sprain is tested by tenderness and cross-body adduction. Instability would show apprehension and relocation findings rather than painful arc impingement signs.
  8. The correct answer is B (The left hip, the stance leg, because its gluteus medius cannot stabilize the pelvis): In single-leg stance the gluteus medius and minimus of the stance leg hold the pelvis level. When they are weak, the pelvis drops on the opposite, unsupported side, so the left hip is weak here. The dropped side is the normal one, and the gluteus maximus is an extensor supplied by the inferior gluteal nerve.
  9. The correct answer is B (Infective endocarditis, probably from Staphylococcus aureus, involving the tricuspid valve): Fever, nail splinter hemorrhages, Janeway lesions and a new regurgitant murmur in an injection drug user are classic for infective endocarditis. Right sided valve involvement is typical, and the tricuspid regurgitant murmur gets louder on inspiration. Rheumatic fever, marantic endocarditis and Libman-Sacks lesions produce sterile vegetations and lack this infectious context.
  10. The correct answer is C (At the L1 to L2 level, near the lower border of the first lumbar vertebra): The adult spinal cord ends as the conus medullaris at about L1 to L2 because the vertebral column grows faster than the cord. In a newborn the conus lies lower, near L3. Lumbar punctures are done at L3 to L4 or L4 to L5 to stay below the cord. The dural sac extends to about S2.
  11. The correct answer is A (Preeclampsia, new hypertension after 20 weeks with proteinuria or other signs of end organ involvement): Preeclampsia is hypertension of 140/90 mmHg or higher after 20 weeks with proteinuria or evidence of end organ injury such as severe headache. Chronic hypertension predates 20 weeks. Gestational hypertension has no proteinuria or organ involvement. Eclampsia requires a seizure, and she has had none, though her headache means she needs urgent obstetric referral to prevent it.
  12. The correct answer is B (Osteoid osteoma, with a nidus under 1.5 cm surrounded by dense reactive sclerosis): Night pain relieved promptly by aspirin or other NSAIDs is classic for osteoid osteoma, which shows a small lucent nidus with dense cortical sclerosis. A nidus under 1.5 cm separates it from osteoblastoma, which is larger and less sclerotic. A Brodie abscess has a serpentine tract and does not respond dramatically to aspirin.
  13. The correct answer is A (Multiple sclerosis, with demyelinating plaques in the dorsal cervical cord producing the Lhermitte sign): Optic neuritis followed later by a spinal cord symptom suggests demyelination separated in time and place, which defines multiple sclerosis. The electric shock on neck flexion is the Lhermitte sign from cervical cord plaques. Spondylotic myelopathy is unusual at her age, and B12 deficiency can give Lhermitte sign but not an earlier optic neuritis. A disc herniation causes a root pattern rather than cord and optic findings.
  14. The correct answer is D (Phosphofructokinase-1, which is activated by AMP and by fructose 2,6-bisphosphate): Phosphofructokinase-1 catalyzes fructose 6-phosphate to fructose 1,6-bisphosphate, the committed and main control point of glycolysis. AMP and fructose 2,6-bisphosphate activate it, while ATP and citrate inhibit it. Hexokinase is inhibited by its product, glucose 6-phosphate. Pyruvate kinase is inhibited by ATP and alanine, and G6PD belongs to the pentose phosphate pathway.
  15. The correct answer is B (The subclavian vein runs anterior to the anterior scalene, separate from the artery and plexus): The subclavian vein crosses over the first rib in front of the anterior scalene, while the subclavian artery and brachial plexus roots pass through the interscalene gap between the anterior and middle scalenes. This is why scalene tightness can compress the artery and plexus but spares the vein in the classic neurovascular compression pattern. The vertebral artery lies behind the anterior scalene, and the phrenic nerve runs down the front of that muscle, not through the gap.
  16. The correct answer is B (Autoimmune segmental demyelination of peripheral nerves with inflammatory infiltrate, triggered by molecular mimicry): Guillain-Barre syndrome follows infections such as Campylobacter jejuni when antibodies against microbial antigens cross-react with peripheral nerve myelin. The result is inflammatory segmental demyelination causing ascending symmetric weakness and loss of reflexes. Poliomyelitis destroys anterior horn cells and causes asymmetric flaccid weakness with fever. Myasthenia gravis weakness is fatigable with intact reflexes, and multiple sclerosis affects central myelin.
  17. The correct answer is D (Right SLR positive at 40 degrees, posterior thigh and calf pain; left negative at 80 degrees): Good objective documentation names the side, the angle at which symptoms appeared, where the pain was reproduced and the result on the opposite side. Pain traveling below the knee to the calf at 40 degrees suggests nerve root tension, not a hamstring stretch. Entries that give a vague label or omit the angle and side comparison cannot be repeated or checked by another clinician.
  18. The correct answer is A (Long thoracic nerve, which arises from C5 to C7 and supplies serratus anterior): The long thoracic nerve runs down the lateral chest wall on serratus anterior and is easily injured during axillary dissection. Serratus anterior holds the scapula against the thorax, so its paralysis causes winging that is worst when pushing forward against resistance. Spinal accessory injury weakens trapezius and drops the shoulder. Dorsal scapular and thoracodorsal injuries affect the rhomboids and latissimus without this classic winging.
  19. The correct answer is D (Rapid formation of a gas cavity in the synovial fluid as the joint surfaces separate): Cavitation occurs when joint surfaces are separated quickly, which lowers pressure in the synovial fluid and lets dissolved gas form a cavity or bubble. The pop is associated with that event and with a resulting increase in joint space. Tendon snapping, adhesion tearing, and spindle reflexes do not explain the sound.
  20. The correct answer is A (Her fetal limit is 0.5 mSv per month, so a 0.3 mSv month is acceptable): Radiation workers may receive 50 mSv per year effective dose, but once pregnancy is declared the fetus is limited to 0.5 mSv per month. Her 0.3 mSv month is below that, so she may continue with careful monitoring. The annual worker limit alone would not protect the fetus in the sensitive weeks 2 to 8. The limit is monthly, so it is not removed or measured per year.
  21. The correct answer is D (Smooth corticated margins at the upper outer pole, often matched in the other knee): A bipartite patella is an unfused secondary ossification center, found in the superolateral corner in most cases and bilateral in about half. Its edges are smooth and corticated because the cleft is a synchondrosis, not a break. An acute fracture has sharp, non-sclerotic edges, usually crosses the middle third and carries swelling and effusion.
  22. The correct answer is C (Obstructive disease with air trapping and hyperinflation from loss of elastic recoil): A low FEV1/FVC ratio indicates obstruction, and a high total lung capacity reflects air trapping and hyperinflation, as in emphysema where lost elastic recoil lets airways collapse on expiration. Restrictive disease lowers total lung capacity and keeps the ratio normal or high. Neuromuscular weakness would reduce lung volumes, and a ratio of 0.55 is not a normal aging finding.
  23. The correct answer is C (Deficiency of terminal complement components C5 through C9, impairing formation of the membrane attack complex): Neisseria depends on the membrane attack complex, built from C5 through C9, for killing, so deficiency of these terminal components leads to recurrent neisserial infection. Her normal vaccine response and immunoglobulin levels exclude agammaglobulinemia. C1q deficiency mainly causes lupus like disease, and C3 deficiency causes severe recurrent infection with many encapsulated bacteria from early childhood, not just Neisseria.
  24. The correct answer is C (Reduce neck pain to 3 out of 10 and rotation to 70 degrees within four weeks): A usable goal names a measure, a target value, and a deadline, so progress can be checked at each visit. The option with a pain rating, a rotation target, and four weeks meets all three. Being below the starting values with no deadline cannot be shown to fail. Improvement as much as possible has no measure. Three manipulations weekly describes treatment, not a result.
  25. The correct answer is A (Recurrent laryngeal nerve, which lies close to the inferior thyroid artery near the gland): The recurrent laryngeal nerve ascends in the tracheoesophageal groove and is closely related to the inferior thyroid artery, so it is at risk when that artery is ligated. Injury leaves the vocal fold paralyzed and gives hoarseness. The external superior laryngeal nerve injury causes loss of pitch, and the vagus trunk and hypoglossal nerve are not in the operative field.
  26. The correct answer is A (Thoracic and sacral kyphoses, which are present in the fetal C-shaped spine): The primary curves are the thoracic and sacral kyphoses, both concave anteriorly and present in the fetal C shape. The cervical lordosis appears when the infant raises the head at about 3 to 4 months, and the lumbar lordosis appears when the child sits and walks, so these two are secondary curves.
  27. The correct answer is B (Osgood-Schlatter disease, a traction apophysitis of the tibial tubercle): Osgood-Schlatter disease is a traction apophysitis where the patellar tendon pulls on the tibial tubercle during growth. Tenderness and swelling at the tuberosity decides the diagnosis. Sinding-Larsen-Johansson is tender at the lower patellar pole. Patellar tendinopathy and patellofemoral pain do not produce tuberosity swelling in this age group.
Case N-B10

Patient: M, 55 y, 70 in, 232 lb

Vitals: Pulse 88, resp 16, BP 148/92 mmHg, temp 100.1 F

Chief complaint and history: Accountant woke at 3 AM with severe throbbing pain in the right great toe and could not tolerate the bedsheet. He had four beers and a steak dinner the evening before. Hypertension treated with hydrochlorothiazide for 3 years. A milder left ankle episode last year resolved in a week. No trauma. No recent procedures or antibiotics.

Examination: Right first MTP joint red, swollen, hot and exquisitely tender, with passive motion limited by pain. Skin shiny without streaking or wound. Right ankle normal. Small firm nontender nodule on the left ear helix. Lumbar motion full. Calves soft.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Homans sign negative. Metatarsal squeeze negative except at the first MTP. Ankle drawer test negative. Dorsalis pedis pulse 2+. Monofilament sensation intact. Patellar and Achilles reflexes 2+. Plantar response flexor.

Laboratory and imaging: Serum uric acid 9.1 mg/dL (3.4 to 7.0), WBC 12.4 x10^3/uL, creatinine 1.3 mg/dL, fasting glucose 104 mg/dL, ESR 42 mm/hr. Right foot film: soft tissue swelling at the first MTP, joint space preserved, no erosion.

  1. The correct answers are A and C: Thiazides reduce renal urate excretion, and alcohol and purine-rich food raise urate, so these are classic precipitants. Abrupt night onset in the great toe with a previous brief self-limited attack is typical of gout. A low-grade fever and painful passive motion occur in septic arthritis and many inflammatory joints, so neither is specific. Hypertension is a coexisting condition and does not cause the arthritis.
  2. The correct answers are B and E: A first MTP attack at night with a prior self-limited episode, high uric acid and a likely tophus on the ear points to gout, with the thiazide adding to urate retention. Cellulitis would spread across skin with streaking and spare passive joint motion. Pseudogout favors older patients and the knee or wrist. Infection remains a consideration with fever and leukocytosis, which is why aspiration is the definitive step.
  3. The correct answers are A and B: Monosodium urate crystals are needle shaped and negatively birefringent, and finding them in synovial fluid is the definitive proof of gout. Culture of the same fluid excludes coexisting infection, which can occur with crystals. Serum uric acid is supportive but may be normal during an attack. Positively birefringent rhomboid crystals belong to pseudogout, and rheumatoid serology does not address this picture.
  4. The correct answer is C (Probable pancreatitis; send him for same day medical evaluation rather than spinal treatment): Epigastric tenderness, fever, nausea and back pain that follows meals and eases when leaning forward, in a heavy drinker, suggest pancreatitis, which needs same day medical evaluation. Spinal manipulation would not address a visceral source and could delay care. Peptic ulcer disease is also visceral but does not warrant a two week watch given the fever, and a strain would not vary with meals or tenderness in the epigastrium.
  5. The correct answer is A (Rapid extension then flexion as the torso accelerates beneath the head, spraining capsules and ligaments): In a rear-end collision the torso is pushed forward while the head lags, causing hyperextension followed by rebound flexion, which sprains facet capsules and ligaments without a fracture. Axial compression and lateral impact mechanisms do not match the direction of force, and creep is a gradual process, not an acute crash injury.
  6. The correct answer is C (Panic disorder, with recurrent unexpected attacks followed by persistent worry about more attacks): Panic disorder requires recurrent unexpected panic attacks, each reaching a peak within minutes, plus at least a month of concern about more attacks or a change in behavior. Generalized anxiety is steady worry without abrupt peaks. Ischemia would show exertional pain and ECG or enzyme changes, which are absent here. Pheochromocytoma is rare and would show sustained or paroxysmal hypertension and a tumor workup.
  7. The correct answer is D (Diabetic ketoacidosis, an anion gap acidosis from insulin deficiency with severe hyperglycemia): The panel shows a glucose of 520 mg/dL with bicarbonate of 9 mEq/L and an anion gap of 28, which is sodium minus chloride plus bicarbonate. Together with deep rapid respiration, this is diabetic ketoacidosis. Hyperosmolar state has little acidosis, and lactic acidosis does not cause such marked hyperglycemia. Alcoholic ketoacidosis requires alcohol use and usually a low or normal glucose.
  8. The correct answer is D (Reduced, since normal expansion is at least 2.5 cm, and this is consistent with ankylosing spondylitis): Normal chest expansion at the fourth intercostal space is at least 2.5 cm, and the costovertebral and costotransverse joints stiffen in ankylosing spondylitis, limiting excursion. With inflammatory back pain onset before age 40, morning stiffness and improvement with exercise, the picture supports spondyloarthritis. Restrictive lung disease also lowers expansion, but this history and age favor the rheumatic disorder.
  9. The correct answer is B (Parkinsonian gait, from dopamine deficiency in the basal ganglia): Short shuffling steps, reduced arm swing, a stooped posture and slow turning in several steps are typical of parkinsonian gait. Ataxic gait is wide based and lurching, with veering. Steppage gait features high knee lift to clear a dropped foot, and antalgic gait shortens the stance phase on the painful side.
  10. The correct answer is C (The pars interarticularis, the isthmus between the superior and inferior articular processes): On an oblique lumbar view the pars interarticularis forms the neck of the Scottie dog, so a lucent band there indicates spondylolysis. Repeated hyperextension in gymnasts stresses this isthmus and can cause stress fracture. A broken transverse process would involve the nose, and a pedicle lesion would change the eye. The superior articular process forms the ear.
  11. The correct answer is A (Postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors on the skeletal muscle motor endplate): Myasthenia gravis is caused by antibodies against postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors, reducing endplate potentials and causing fatigable weakness. Edrophonium inhibits acetylcholinesterase, raising acetylcholine in the cleft and briefly improving strength. Antibodies against presynaptic calcium channels cause Lambert-Eaton syndrome, and the other targets do not produce this pattern.
  12. The correct answer is B (The urogenital triangle, which contains the external genitalia and urethral opening): The line between the ischial tuberosities separates the anterior urogenital triangle from the posterior anal triangle. The urogenital triangle holds the external genitalia and urethral opening, while the anal triangle holds the anal canal and ischioanal fossae. The femoral and inguinal triangles belong to the thigh and abdominal wall.
  13. The correct answer is D (The pars interarticularis, lying between the superior and inferior articular processes): On the oblique lumbar view the transverse process forms the nose, the pedicle the eye, the superior articular process the ear, the inferior articular process the front leg, and the pars interarticularis the neck. A break across the neck is seen as a collar or band of lucency on the dog. That is why this view is used to detect a pars defect.
  14. The correct answer is A (Same-day urgent medical referral for corticosteroids, with ESR commonly above 50 mm/hr and elevated C-reactive protein): New temporal headache, scalp tenderness, and jaw claudication after age 50 indicate giant cell arteritis, and transient visual blurring warns of impending blindness. Treatment with high-dose corticosteroids should start without waiting for biopsy, so referral is urgent and same day. ESR is usually above 50 mm/hr and C-reactive protein is raised. A conservative trial or delayed referral risks permanent vision loss.
  15. The correct answer is A (Centralization suggests a favorable directional preference, so extension exercises continue with monitoring): When distal leg pain moves toward the spine during repeated movement testing, it is called centralization and predicts a good outcome with that movement direction. Extension is therefore continued, with the patient watching for continued retreat. Peripheralization is the opposite, with pain moving farther down the limb. Cauda equina syndrome presents with saddle numbness and bladder changes, which are absent here.
  16. The correct answer is B (Avoid thrust, use low-force or soft tissue care, and coordinate the INR with the prescriber): An INR above the therapeutic range makes spontaneous and traumatic bleeding more likely, so thrust techniques are relatively contraindicated until it is controlled. Gentle low-force or soft tissue care is acceptable, and the prescriber should be told about the INR. Deep friction can bruise tissue, and changing anticoagulant dosing belongs to the prescribing physician, not the chiropractor.
  17. The correct answer is C (Mitral valve, where fibrosis and commissural fusion create a narrowed fish-mouth orifice): Rheumatic heart disease follows group A streptococcal pharyngitis through molecular mimicry, and Aschoff bodies are its hallmark. Chronic scarring most often affects the mitral valve, giving commissural fusion and a fish-mouth stenosis with an opening snap. Aortic calcification is degenerative, tricuspid vegetations belong to injection drug use, and carcinoid involves right-sided valves.
  18. The correct answer is D (Cryotherapy for 10 to 15 minutes with a compression wrap and elevation above heart level): In the first 24 to 72 hours after a sprain the goal is to limit bleeding, edema and pain, so cold applied for 10 to 15 minutes with compression and elevation fits. Moist heat raises blood flow and worsens swelling at this stage.
