On the lateral film it lies well in front of the vertebral bodies, so it is in the abdominal cavity or a vessel and not in the retroperitoneal renal tract or in bone. Renal and ureteric stones project over the spine or the transverse processes, a bone island stays within the vertebral outline, and costal cartilage lies posteriorly near the ribs.
  23. The correct answer is C (Hip osteoarthritis, supported by deep groin pain and loss of internal rotation): Deep groin pain, loss of internal rotation and brief morning stiffness are typical of hip osteoarthritis, and FABER stresses the hip as well as the sacroiliac joint. Negative posterior sacroiliac provocation tests further point away from that joint. Bursitis usually causes pain with resisted flexion or snapping, and an L2 radiculopathy would show a dermatomal sensory or myotomal change.
  24. The correct answer is A (Stay with him, secure access to means where possible, and arrange immediate emergency psychiatric evaluation): A specific plan, access to a lethal means and stated intent mark high imminent risk. The doctor must not leave him alone, should reduce access to the means and must arrange emergency psychiatric evaluation, because the duty to protect overrides confidentiality. A contract, delayed referral or routine care leaves him unprotected tonight.
  25. The correct answer is C (Protect the joint, control swelling with ice and compression, and start gentle motion): In the acute phase of a mild sprain the goals are to protect the tissue, limit swelling and pain, and keep gentle pain-free motion going. Plyometrics and balance challenges belong to the later rehabilitative phase. Deep friction and forceful mobilization irritate inflamed tissue at two days. Rigid casting is excessive for a stable grade 1 sprain and delays motion.
  26. The correct answer is C (Psoriatic arthritis, because end joint erosions with fluffy periostitis occur with preserved bone density): Psoriatic arthritis favors the distal interphalangeal joints, produces fluffy periostitis and a pencil-in-cup deformity, and keeps normal bone density, which differs from the periarticular osteopenia of rheumatoid arthritis. Nail pitting and a sausage digit support it. Erosive osteoarthritis gives central erosions without nail change, and gout produces overhanging edges with tophi.
  27. The correct answer is C (Common fibular neuropathy at the fibular head, because inversion and hip abduction are spared): Pressure at the fibular head damages the common fibular nerve, which weakens dorsiflexion and eversion but spares tibialis posterior and gluteus medius. L5 root lesions weaken inversion and hip abduction as well. A sciatic injury would also impair plantar flexion because the tibial division is involved. Anterior compartment syndrome would present with severe pain and tense swelling, not a painless foot drop.
  28. The correct answer is D (Subluxation is a working hypothesis, with mechanisms under study and no established broad systemic effects): The modern view treats subluxation as a hypothesis whose mechanisms are being studied, with limited support for claims about systemic disease. Innate intelligence, a vital force, and a single cause of all disease are historical vitalistic ideas that the evidence-informed view does not endorse.
  29. The correct answer is C (Scheuermann disease, a structural juvenile kyphosis from vertebral endplate growth disturbance): Scheuermann disease is defined by anterior wedging of 5 degrees or more in at least three consecutive vertebrae with endplate irregularity and a rigid kyphosis in adolescents. Postural kyphosis is flexible and shows no wedging. Ankylosing spondylitis starts with sacroiliac disease and bamboo-like ossification, and congenital kyphosis involves a single malformed segment.
  30. The correct answer is C (Spina bifida resulting from incomplete closure of the neural tube): Folic acid taken before conception and through the first trimester lowers the risk of neural tube defects such as spina bifida and anencephaly. The neural tube closes by about day 28, often before pregnancy is recognized, so supplementation must begin early. Down syndrome comes from meiotic nondisjunction, which vitamins do not prevent. Tetralogy of Fallot and pyloric stenosis are not preventable with folate.
  31. The correct answer is B (The optic canal, which transmits the optic nerve together with the ophthalmic artery): The middle cranial fossa contains the sella turcica with the pituitary, the optic canal, the superior orbital fissure, and the foramina rotundum, ovale and spinosum. The cribriform plate lies in the anterior fossa. The foramen magnum and jugular foramen both belong to the posterior fossa.
  32. The correct answer is C (Dendritic cells, which migrate to lymph nodes and express abundant MHC class II and costimulatory molecules): Dendritic cells are the most potent antigen-presenting cells for naive T cells. After capturing antigen they mature, travel to the lymph node and display peptide on MHC class II with B7 costimulation. Macrophages present antigen but prime naive cells less efficiently, and B cells chiefly present to already primed helper T cells. Neutrophils have minimal antigen-presenting function.
  33. The correct answer is A (Typical angina, because all three defining features of the pain pattern are present): Typical angina requires substernal pressure, provocation by exertion or stress, and relief by rest or nitroglycerin, and all three are described here. Atypical angina meets only two features, and noncardiac pain meets one or none. The brief sharp pain with inspiration is a separate symptom and does not change the classification of the exertional pressure. Radiation to the jaw is common but not part of the definition.
  34. The correct answer is B (Sudden severe onset peaking within seconds, described as the worst headache of his life): A thunderclap headache that peaks within seconds and is the worst the patient has had is the key red flag for subarachnoid hemorrhage and calls for emergency evaluation and imaging. Tension type headaches build gradually and are dull and band like. Migraine with aura evolves over minutes to hours, and neck related headache tracks with posture and work. Only the abrupt maximal onset requires emergency transport.
  35. The correct answer is B (Suspicious for prostate carcinoma, requiring PSA testing and urology referral): A hard, nodular, asymmetric gland with loss of the median sulcus is suspicious for carcinoma, usually in the peripheral zone. Benign enlargement feels smooth, firm and symmetric, and prostatitis is exquisitely tender with systemic symptoms. These findings are not normal for age, so the patient needs PSA and urology referral.
  36. The correct answer is A (Lactate loses electrons and is oxidized, while NAD+ gains a hydride and is reduced): Oxidation is loss of electrons, and lactate loses a hydride equivalent as it becomes pyruvate, so lactate is oxidized. NAD+ accepts that hydride and is reduced to NADH, which carries the electrons to later pathways. The two halves of the reaction always pair one oxidation with one reduction. Options that call both species oxidized, or that swap the definitions, break this conservation of electrons.
  37. The correct answer is D (Avoid thrust at L4-L5, adjust adjacent levels cautiously, and start motor control stabilization exercises): Translation above about 3 to 4 mm in the lumbar spine signals segmental instability, so the problem is too much motion. Thrust at the unstable level is avoided, adjacent hypomobile levels can be treated gently, and motor control exercises improve segment control. Surgery is considered only after failed conservative care or neurological compromise.
  38. The correct answer is D (Right gluteus medius, supplied by the superior gluteal nerve, root levels L4 to S1): During single-leg stance the stance-side gluteus medius prevents the opposite pelvis from dropping, and weakness produces a Trendelenburg sign with a compensatory trunk lean over the stance hip. The muscle is supplied by the superior gluteal nerve from L4 to S1. The gluteus maximus extends the hip and uses the inferior gluteal nerve, while the iliopsoas flexes the hip.
  39. The correct answer is B (Osteoporotic vertebral compression fracture, best confirmed with a lateral thoracic radiograph): Long term glucocorticoid use causes osteoporosis, and a sudden thoracic pain with focal spinous tenderness after a minor strain like coughing fits a compression fracture. A lateral thoracic film shows the wedged vertebral body. Intercostal neuritis follows a rib rather than a spinous process, and facet pain is not sudden or focal in this way. Malignancy is possible in older patients, but the stable weight, absent cancer history and steroid exposure favor osteoporosis.
  40. The correct answer is B (Vertebral osteomyelitis with narrowing of the L3-L4 disc and erosion of both endplates): Fever, a raised ESR and diabetes with a narrowed disc and destruction of the adjacent endplates indicate infectious spondylodiscitis, because infection crosses the disc where tumor generally does not. Degenerative disease has osteophytes and a vacuum sign without destruction or fever, and ankylosing spondylitis forms bridging syndesmophytes. Metastasis typically spares the disc. MRI and blood culture follow.
  41. The correct answer is D (Thiamine deficiency, treated with parenteral thiamine before any glucose or dextrose is administered): The triad of confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia describes Wernicke encephalopathy from thiamine deficiency. His glucose is normal, so dextrose is not needed urgently, and glucose given before thiamine can consume remaining thiamine and worsen the injury. Parenteral thiamine comes first. Pellagra causes dermatitis, diarrhea, and dementia, and B12 deficiency causes dorsal column signs and macrocytic anemia.
  42. The correct answer is B (Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis, with thick flowing ossification and preserved disc heights): Flowing ossification along the anterior spine across at least four contiguous vertebrae, in an older man, with preserved disc heights and normal sacroiliac and facet joints, is DISH. It is often right-sided in the thoracic spine and linked to diabetes. Ankylosing spondylitis has thin syndesmophytes and sacroiliitis, spondylosis shows disc narrowing, and psoriatic arthritis would involve the sacroiliac joints.
  43. The correct answer is C (Extension of the spine, because the ligament is stretched when the column bends backward): The anterior longitudinal ligament covers the front of the vertebral bodies and discs, so it becomes taut when the column extends. It limits extension and helps prevent the discs from bulging forward. The posterior longitudinal ligament, ligamentum flavum and interspinous ligaments resist flexion. Rotation and lateral flexion are limited by the facet joints and the annulus.
  44. The correct answer is A (High serum calcium with low serum phosphate, due to bone resorption and renal phosphate loss): PTH stimulates osteoclastic bone resorption and renal activation of vitamin D, which raise serum calcium, while it inhibits proximal tubule phosphate reabsorption and lowers serum phosphate. Excess PTH therefore gives hypercalcemia with hypophosphatemia and calcium-containing stones. Phosphate rising together with calcium is not seen. Low calcium with high phosphate is the hypoparathyroid pattern, and reduced bone turnover contradicts PTH action.
  45. The correct answer is C (It occupies the active site, raising apparent Km with Vmax unchanged; high substrate overcomes it): Lines that meet on the y-axis share the same 1/Vmax, so Vmax is unchanged. The x-intercept equals -1/Km, so an intercept nearer zero means a larger apparent Km, which is the signature of competition for the active site. Raising substrate concentration outcompetes the inhibitor and restores the original maximal velocity. Allosteric inhibition would lower Vmax, and binding only the enzyme-substrate complex would give parallel lines.
  46. The correct answer is B (Urgent inflammatory markers and a complete blood count with contrast MRI of the thoracic spine): Night pain, fever, weight loss and recent infection raise concern for vertebral osteomyelitis, discitis or metastatic disease. ESR, CRP and a blood count help support suspicion, and contrast MRI is the most sensitive imaging for early spinal infection and marrow replacement. Plain films are often normal early and manipulation is not appropriate before serious disease is excluded. DEXA does not explain fever and sweats.
  47. The correct answer is C (Order urgent ESR and CRP and arrange same-day medical referral for corticosteroid consideration): New temporal headache after age 50 with jaw claudication, scalp tenderness and transient visual loss suggests giant cell arteritis, and untreated it can cause permanent blindness. The next step is urgent ESR and CRP with same-day referral for corticosteroids, without waiting for biopsy. Cervical care and conservative advice delay treatment, and a normal CT does not exclude arteritis.
  48. The correct answer is A (Risser grade 3, with relatively low progression risk because most skeletal growth has finished): Risser grade is assigned by how far the iliac apophysis has ossified along the crest: grade 1 is 25 percent, grade 2 is 50 percent, grade 3 is 75 percent, grade 4 is 100 percent, and grade 5 is fusion. At 75 percent excursion this is Risser 3, and with menarche 18 months earlier the growth spurt is largely finished. A curve of about 22 degrees at Risser 3 has a low risk of progression, much lower than the same curve at Risser 0 or 1.
  49. The correct answer is B (The thrust overcomes the tension, so the section drops a short distance and then stops): The terminal point drop mechanism is set so that the patient's weight is held just short of release. The quick thrust adds momentum that exceeds the tension, so the section drops a small distance and halts at its stop, which gives a low-force, high-speed impulse. If tension were set equal to body weight or lower, the section would drop on its own before the thrust.
  50. The correct answer is D (Modic type 1 change, with marrow edema and inflammation beside a degenerating disc): Modic type 1 shows low T1 and high T2 signal because of edema, fibrovascular tissue and inflammation next to a degenerated disc. Type 2 is bright on T1 and bright or intermediate on T2 from fat, and type 3 is dark on both from sclerosis. A hemangioma sits within the body and is bright on T1 with a polka dot pattern, so it does not fit an endplate band.
  51. The correct answer is B (The carina where the trachea divides, with the aortic arch beginning and ending): The sternal angle at the manubriosternal joint marks the plane of the T4 to T5 disc, where the trachea bifurcates at the carina and the aortic arch begins and ends. The esophageal hiatus is near T10, the caval opening near T8 and the celiac trunk near T12. Those are all lower and cannot be reached by this plane.
Case N-B04

Patient: M, 61 y, 69 in, 178 lb

Vitals: Pulse 70, resp 16, BP 142/86 mmHg, temp 98.1 F

Chief complaint and history: Retired machinist with 8 months of clumsy hands, trouble with shirt buttons and handwriting, and a stiff, wide-based walk. Neck is stiff but only mildly sore. Occasional electric sensation down the spine when bending the neck forward. Non-smoker. Treated hypertension and mild hyperlipidemia. No bowel or bladder complaints.

Examination: Cervical rotation reduced to about 40 degrees each way and extension limited. Mild atrophy of the first dorsal interosseous muscles bilaterally. Grip release test slow at 12 cycles in 10 seconds. Tandem gait unsteady. Mild positive Romberg. Shoulder range of motion full.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Biceps reflexes 1+ bilaterally, triceps 2+, patellar 3+, Achilles 3+. Hoffmann sign positive bilaterally. Right ankle clonus, 3 beats. Right plantar response extensor. Vibration sense reduced in the fingers, normal at the toes. Spurling test negative. Lhermitte sign positive.

Laboratory and imaging: Lateral cervical film: narrowing at C5-6 and C6-7 with anterior and posterior osteophytes, sagittal canal diameter 11 mm at C5. Vitamin B12 520 pg/mL. Fasting glucose 94 mg/dL. TSH 1.9 mIU/L. Complete blood count normal.

  1. The correct answers are A and D: Hyperreflexia, Hoffmann and Babinski signs, clonus and an unsteady gait indicate cord compression, and an 11 mm sagittal canal at C5 is below the 13 mm stenosis threshold and well under the normal of about 17 mm. Normal B12 and glucose argue against a metabolic neuropathy, which would also give reduced reflexes. Carpal tunnel syndrome does not cause leg hyperreflexia. Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis spares sensation and does not explain the canal narrowing.
  2. The correct answers are C and D: A Hoffmann sign, extensor plantar response and clonus reflect loss of corticospinal inhibition. The reduced biceps reflex reflects the compressed C5-6 segment itself and is a lower motor neuron finding at the level, giving the inverted radial reflex pattern. Finger vibration loss is sensory, and interosseous atrophy is a lower motor neuron change. Together they show a lesion with cord and root effects.
  3. The correct answers are B and C: Progressive myelopathy with a narrow canal needs MRI to show cord compression and signal change, and surgical evaluation because decompression is the treatment that stops progression. Thrust manipulation and traction can load a compressed cord and risk further injury. A collar with prolonged rest does not alter the course. Delay risks permanent hand and gait deficit.
  4. The correct answer is B (Depleted ATP leaves myosin heads bound to actin, since ATP binding is required for detachment): A myosin head releases actin only when a new ATP molecule binds it. After death ATP is exhausted and calcium leaks into the cytosol, so cross-bridges form and cannot detach. The muscle remains stiff until proteolysis breaks down the filaments, usually after one to three days. Acetylcholine release ceases after death, and troponin is not locked in place.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (From C3 through C7, on the superolateral margins of the vertebral bodies): Uncinate processes and the uncovertebral joints of Luschka are found on the typical cervical vertebrae from C3 to C7. The atlas has no body and the axis has a dens in its place, so neither carries them. They guide flexion and extension and limit lateral translation, and degenerative spurs there can narrow the intervertebral foramen.
  2. The correct answer is C (Nephritic syndrome from acute glomerulonephritis, likely poststreptococcal in origin): Red cell casts and dysmorphic red cells prove that blood is leaking through damaged glomeruli, and hypertension, edema and a rising creatinine complete the nephritic picture. The timing after pharyngitis points to poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis. Nephrotic syndrome shows protein losses above 3.5 g per day with lipid casts. Pyelonephritis has white cell casts, and stones cause isomorphic red cells.
  3. The correct answer is C (A popliteal (Baker) cyst from distention of the gastrocnemius-semimembranosus bursa communicating with the joint): A Baker cyst is a distended gastrocnemius-semimembranosus bursa that communicates with the knee joint, and it is common with osteoarthritis or meniscal tears that produce effusion. On MRI it appears as a fluid-signal structure between the medial gastrocnemius head and the semimembranosus tendon. An aneurysm would show a vessel with flow void and a connection to the artery. A parameniscal ganglion sits at the joint line, and a vein thrombosis would fill a vein rather than lie in the bursa.
  4. The correct answer is C (Radial head subluxation, reduced by supinating the forearm while the elbow is gently flexed): A pull on the extended pronated arm in a toddler slips the annular ligament over the radial head, producing nursemaid's elbow. With no swelling, deformity, or bony tenderness, the usual reduction is supination with flexion, or hyperpronation. Traction in extension would worsen it, and a supracondylar fracture would show swelling and bone tenderness.
  5. The correct answer is A (Taper to one or two visits weekly and add active exercise): Improvement of about 50 percent at two weeks means the acute-phase frequency can taper, and the plan should shift toward active care and self-management to prevent recurrence. Holding three visits weekly until pain is gone spends visits without added benefit. Discharging now ignores remaining deficits and the recurrence risk. Daily visits add burden with no support for better outcomes in a patient who is already responding.
  6. The correct answer is C (Superior sulcus bronchogenic carcinoma, with C8 to T1 ulnar pain and ipsilateral Horner syndrome): An apical lung mass invading the posterior first and second ribs and the lower brachial plexus is a Pancoast tumor, nearly always from lung carcinoma. The C8 and T1 involvement explains the ulnar border pain and intrinsic hand weakness, and invasion of the sympathetic chain produces ipsilateral Horner syndrome with ptosis, miosis and anhidrosis. Thoracic outlet syndrome and cuff pathology do not destroy ribs, and lymphoma tends to involve hilar and mediastinal nodes.
  7. The correct answer is A (IgE bound to mast cell receptors cross-links on antigen exposure and triggers immediate histamine degranulation): Immediate food allergy is a type I hypersensitivity in which IgE on mast cells and basophils cross-links with antigen, releasing histamine and other mediators within minutes. Atopy in the family and a positive skin test support it, and epinephrine is the first-line treatment. Complement-mediated cell destruction is type II, immune complex deposition is type III, and delayed T cell cytokine reactions are type IV.
  8. The correct answer is D (Approximately 5.0 liters per minute, the product of heart rate and stroke volume): Cardiac output equals heart rate times stroke volume, so 72 beats per minute times 70 mL gives 5,040 mL per minute, or about 5.0 liters per minute. A quotient of the two numbers has no physiological meaning here. Fifty liters per minute is ten times the real value and is impossible at rest. Resting output in a healthy adult is normally 4 to 8 liters per minute, so no halving is applied.
  9. The correct answer is A (To screen each body system for unvolunteered symptoms that may reveal related or unrelated disease): The review of systems is a structured, head-to-toe set of questions that screens every body system for symptoms the patient did not mention. It can uncover red flags or conditions that bear on management, such as weight loss or bowel changes in a back pain patient. It records symptoms, not objective measurements, and it is not meant to test credibility or confirm a diagnosis.
  10. The correct answer is B (Refer for cardiac evaluation before any treatment, and advise emergency care if symptoms occur at rest): Exertional chest tightness that resolves within minutes of rest in a smoker is typical of stable angina, and the next step is referral for cardiac workup before any treatment, with instructions to seek emergency care if symptoms start at rest or last longer. Manipulation assumes a spinal source without ruling out the heart. Thoracic films examine the wrong organ system, and advice with a later recheck leaves probable coronary disease unevaluated.
  11. The correct answer is C (Anterior cruciate ligament, the primary restraint to forward translation of the tibia): The Lachman test checks anterior tibial translation at about 20 degrees of flexion, and a soft endpoint with increased glide points to an anterior cruciate tear. The posterior cruciate resists posterior translation and is checked with the posterior drawer test. The medial collateral ligament is tested with valgus stress, not with anterior glide. A meniscal tear gives joint line pain and mechanical catching, though it can accompany an anterior cruciate injury.
  12. The correct answer is D (The pars interarticularis, with the collar indicating a pars defect or spondylolysis): On an oblique lumbar view the transverse process forms the nose, the pedicle the eye, the superior articular process the ear, the pars interarticularis the neck and the inferior articular process the front leg. A lucent collar at the neck represents a pars interarticularis defect, or spondylolysis, common at L5 in young athletes who repeatedly extend the spine. It can lead to spondylolisthesis.
Case N-B03

Patient: M, 38 y, 72 in, 210 lb

Vitals: Pulse 84, resp 18, BP 130/84 mmHg, temp 98.6 F

Chief complaint and history: Warehouse worker with 2 days of severe low back pain that began after lifting a pallet. Pain now goes into both legs, left worse, with weak feet. Since yesterday he has trouble starting urination and has dribbled. He also reports numb skin around the groin and inner thighs. Prior left knee arthroscopy. No fever or prior cancer.

Examination: Walks slowly with a wide stance and holds the lumbar spine flexed. Lumbar extension is refused. Bladder palpable above the pubic symphysis. No spinal deformity, no skin lesion. Femoral and pedal pulses 2+ bilaterally. Abdomen soft and nontender.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Straight leg raise positive bilaterally at 35 degrees. Patellar reflexes 2+, Achilles absent bilaterally. Plantar flexion 4/5 bilaterally, dorsiflexion 5/5. Pinprick reduced in the perianal and genital skin and the posterior thighs. Rectal tone reduced, voluntary squeeze weak. Babinski negative, no clonus.

Laboratory and imaging: Bladder scan shows 380 mL residual after voiding. Urine dipstick negative for nitrite and leukocytes. White cell count 7,400. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate 8 mm/h. Lumbar radiographs: mild disc narrowing at L4-5 and L5-S1, no fracture.

  1. The correct answers are C and E: Saddle sensory loss, retention with overflow, reduced rectal tone and absent Achilles reflexes show multiple lower sacral roots are compressed, and a large central disc herniation after lifting is the usual cause. Conus lesions tend to give mixed signs with a Babinski response and more preserved reflexes, which this chart lacks. A normal urine dipstick and white count argue against infection. Facet pain does not cause sphincter or reflex loss.
  2. The correct answers are A and E: The S2 to S4 roots supply the perianal skin and the external anal sphincter, so sensation there and rectal tone are the direct measures of their function. The slump test stresses neural tissue tension but does not grade sacral function. The femoral nerve stretch test examines the L2 to L4 roots. The Kemp test loads the facet joints and gives no neurologic data.
  3. The correct answers are C and D: Cauda equina syndrome with retention and sphincter weakness is a surgical emergency, because outcomes depend on decompression within a short window, so urgent MRI and surgical review are needed. Manipulation or any delay risks worsening neural compression. Plain films cannot show the herniated disc. Waiting a week or relying on medication and stretching wastes the window for recovery.
  4. The correct answer is A (The sternocleidomastoid and the trapezius, both supplied by the spinal accessory nerve): The spinal accessory nerve runs superficially in the posterior triangle and supplies the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius. Injury during node biopsy causes shoulder drooping, weak shrug and weak head turning to the opposite side. The dorsal scapular, thoracic and axillary nerves supply the other muscle pairs, which are not affected by this injury.
  5. The correct answer is A (Staphylococcus aureus, a catalase-positive, coagulase-positive gram-positive coccus arranged in clusters): Staphylococcus aureus is the most common cause of osteomyelitis in all age groups other than special settings such as sickle cell disease. It forms gram-positive clusters and is catalase positive and coagulase positive. Streptococcus pyogenes is catalase negative and forms chains, and Staphylococcus epidermidis is coagulase negative and typically involves prosthetic material. Escherichia coli is a minor cause.
  6. The correct answer is A (Smooth corticated margins with a short dens mark a chronic ossicle, risking cord compression from instability): Os odontoideum is a smooth, corticated ossicle separated from a short or absent dens, with a wide gap, and it leaves the atlas free to slide forward. That atlantoaxial instability can compress the cord, so flexion and extension studies and surgical opinion are needed. An acute type II dens fracture shows sharp uncorticated edges and prevertebral swelling.
  7. The correct answer is C (Epstein-Barr virus, a herpesvirus that infects B lymphocytes and spreads through saliva): Fever, exudative pharyngitis, posterior cervical adenopathy and a positive heterophile antibody test establish infectious mononucleosis from Epstein-Barr virus. Cytomegalovirus can mimic the syndrome but the heterophile test is negative. Adenovirus pharyngitis is usually accompanied by conjunctivitis, and acute HIV infection is diagnosed by antigen or RNA testing rather than a heterophile test.
  8. The correct answer is D (Nine months, when sitting alone, consonant babbling and a developing pincer grasp appear together): Sitting without support arrives at about 6 to 8 months, canonical babbling at about 6 to 9 months, and a neat pincer grasp at about 9 to 10 months, so nine months fits all three findings. A four month old would only be rolling and bringing hands together. A twelve month old would be pulling up and cruising, which this infant cannot do. A fifteen month old would be walking alone and using several words.
  9. The correct answer is A (Maximum velocity falls while the substrate concentration giving half-maximal velocity stays the same): A noncompetitive inhibitor binds an allosteric site on free enzyme and enzyme-substrate complex equally, so it removes functional enzyme without changing substrate affinity. Vmax falls and Km is unchanged, giving lines that share an x-intercept. A competitive inhibitor would raise Km with Vmax intact. An uncompetitive inhibitor would lower both Vmax and Km and give parallel lines.
  10. The correct answer is A (The lambdoid suture, which joins the parietal bones to the occipital bone): The lambdoid suture forms the posterior boundary of the parietal bones as they meet the occipital bone, and its shape resembles the Greek letter lambda. The coronal suture lies anteriorly between the frontal and parietal bones. The sagittal suture runs between the parietal bones in the midline. The squamous suture joins the temporal and parietal bones on the side of the skull.
  11. The correct answer is C (Reduced costovertebral mobility suggesting ankylosing spondylitis, since normal expansion is at least 2.5 cm): Normal chest expansion is at least 2.5 cm, and 1.5 cm in a young man with inflammatory back pain suggests costovertebral joint involvement from ankylosing spondylitis. The history of long morning stiffness improving with activity supports an inflammatory cause. Lung disease could restrict expansion but would not explain the back pain pattern. Mechanical strain and thoracic outlet syndrome do not fix the rib cage joints.
  12. The correct answer is B (A thin visceral pleural line with no lung markings beyond it, convex toward the chest wall): In pneumothorax, air outside the lung collapses it away from the chest wall, leaving a thin visceral pleural line, with absent lung markings peripheral to it. The line is convex toward the chest wall. A bulla is a space inside the lung with a thin wall that may be concave outward, and vessels or septa may still be seen. A fluid level points to a cavity, abscess or hydropneumothorax.
  13. The correct answer is B (Adenocarcinoma arising from epithelium, and osteosarcoma arising from mesenchymal connective tissue): Carcinomas arise from epithelium and sarcomas from mesenchymal tissue such as bone, cartilage, fat and muscle. The glandular colon tumor is an adenocarcinoma and the bone-forming tumor is an osteosarcoma. The suffix adenoma and osteoma denote benign tumors, and lymphoma and chondrosarcoma do not describe either report.
  14. The correct answer is C (Esophagus, which crosses at the level of T10 through the muscular hiatus of the right crus): The esophageal hiatus lies at about T10 in the muscular part of the right crus and transmits the esophagus and the anterior and posterior vagal trunks. The caval opening is at T8 in the central tendon. The aortic hiatus is at T12, behind the median arcuate ligament, and also transmits the thoracic duct and azygos vein. The splanchnic nerves pierce the crura instead.
  15. The correct answer is B (Upper motor neuron lesion, with spasticity, hyperreflexia, a Babinski sign and only mild disuse atrophy): A corticospinal tract lesion above the anterior horn releases spinal reflexes from inhibition, producing spasticity, hyperreflexia and an extensor plantar response. Atrophy is mild and comes from disuse. Lower motor neuron lesions cause flaccidity, hyporeflexia or areflexia, early atrophy and fasciculations. A junction disorder or myopathy would not give a Babinski sign or hyperreflexia.
  16. The correct answer is D (Positive Murphy sign, suggesting inflamed gallbladder disease such as acute cholecystitis needing medical evaluation): Inspiratory arrest during palpation under the right costal margin is the Murphy sign, which points to gallbladder inflammation and calls for medical evaluation rather than chiropractic treatment. McBurney point tenderness belongs to the right lower quadrant and appendicitis. Kehr sign is left shoulder pain from diaphragmatic irritation, and Courvoisier sign describes a painless enlarged gallbladder, not an acutely tender one.
  17. The correct answer is A (The 51 pound limit assumes ideal lifting; reach, twisting, and frequency lower it): The NIOSH recommended weight limit starts at 51 pounds (23 kg) under ideal conditions: load close to the body, no twisting, infrequent lifts and good hand coupling. Multipliers for horizontal distance, vertical height, asymmetry and frequency then reduce that value. This worker has a long reach, a twist and a high frequency, so his recommended limit is well below 45 pounds.
  18. The correct answer is D (Reduced carotid sinus firing lowers vagal tone and raises sympathetic outflow, increasing heart rate and vasoconstriction): Standing pools blood in the legs and lowers arterial pressure at the carotid sinus, so stretch receptor firing falls. The medulla responds by withdrawing vagal tone and increasing sympathetic outflow, which raises heart rate and constricts vessels. Increased firing would slow the heart, and the rapid rise in pulse points to a neural reflex rather than a hormonal effect.
  19. The correct answer is B (Cryotherapy, because cold exposure can trigger vasospasm and ischemia in the digital arteries): Raynaud disease involves episodic vasospasm of digital arteries that cold exposure triggers, so ice can cause blanching, pain, or tissue injury. Compression with a snug wrap, elevation, and gentle motion are standard acute sprain care and do not provoke vasospasm. Heat or protected cold alternatives are chosen instead.
  20. The correct answer is A (Simple bone cyst, with the fallen fragment sign inside a central well defined lucent lesion): A central lucent lesion in the proximal humeral metaphysis of a child that fractures is a simple bone cyst. The fragment that falls into the fluid-filled cavity is the pathognomonic sign. Aneurysmal cysts are eccentric with fluid levels, and fibrous dysplasia has a ground glass matrix. Eosinophilic granuloma is a punched-out lesion that lacks a fallen fragment.
  21. The correct answer is C (Retrograde flow through the valveless vertebral venous plexus linking pelvic veins with spinal veins): The vertebral venous plexus of Batson has no valves, so raised abdominal or pelvic pressure can reverse flow from the prostatic plexus into the vertebral veins. This allows pelvic tumors to seed the lumbar spine and sacrum without lung or liver involvement. Spread through the caval and pulmonary route would be expected to produce lung lesions first. Portal routes carry bowel tumors to the liver, not prostate cancer to the spine.
  22. The correct answer is A (Merkel cell, associated with sensory nerve endings in the basal epidermis): Merkel cells sit in the basal layer and form synapse-like contacts with sensory nerve endings, serving as slowly adapting touch receptors. Langerhans cells present antigens, and melanocytes make melanin. Meissner corpuscles also detect light touch but they are encapsulated receptors in the dermal papillae rather than epidermal cells.
  23. The correct answer is B (S1 root, the segmental level for the Achilles tendon reflex): The Achilles reflex is mediated by the S1 root, the patellar reflex by L4, and the triceps reflex by C7. Because the patellar and upper limb reflexes are normal, the diminished Achilles reflex points to S1. L5 has no reliable tendon reflex at the ankle, and the medial hamstring reflex is inconsistent.
  24. The correct answer is B ("My neck is on fire" for one week, written in her own words): The chief complaint is the reason for the visit recorded in the patient's own words, with duration, before any interpretation. Facet syndrome, radiculopathy and whiplash strain are diagnostic or causal conclusions that belong later in the assessment. Writing them as the chief complaint biases the rest of the history.
  25. The correct answer is D (Unexplained weight loss, night pain that position does not relieve, and prior cancer): Unexplained weight loss, night pain unrelieved by position, and a previous cancer are the classic history red flags for malignancy, and age over 50 adds to the concern. Short morning stiffness with improvement on walking fits degenerative change. Pain after lifting that eases with rest and paraspinal tenderness fit a muscle strain, and leg radiation with a positive straight leg raise and a reduced ankle reflex fit radiculopathy.
  26. The correct answer is C (Peripheral facial nerve in the facial canal of the temporal bone, as in Bell palsy): Loss of forehead wrinkling means the lower motor neuron is involved, because the forehead receives bilateral cortical input and is spared in central lesions. Reduced anterior taste and hyperacusis place the lesion in the facial canal, proximal to the chorda tympani and stapedius branches. The trigeminal nerve supplies mastication and facial sensation. A pontine lesion would also produce an abducens gaze palsy.
  27. The correct answer is C (C4, at the upper border of the thyroid cartilage in the neck): The common carotid artery divides at about C4, which corresponds to the upper border of the thyroid cartilage. C6 is the cricoid level, where the pharynx becomes the esophagus and the larynx becomes the trachea. The hyoid lies at C3, and the angle of the mandible is higher still, so neither marks the usual bifurcation.
  28. The correct answer is D (Ureteral calculus, with noncontrast helical CT of the abdomen and pelvis as the best imaging): Colicky flank pain radiating to the groin with microscopic hematuria in a restless patient is typical of ureteral stone. Noncontrast helical CT detects nearly all stones including uric acid stones and shows obstruction, so it is the preferred study. A plain film misses radiolucent and small stones. Pyelonephritis would produce fever and pyuria, and an aneurysm gives steady pain rather than waves.
  29. The correct answer is D (Rotation to the left, opposite the side of side bending, as a group): Fryette type I describes neutral spinal mechanics in which a group of vertebrae sidebends to one side and rotates to the opposite side. Right side bending therefore pairs with left rotation of the group. Same-side coupling belongs to type II, which occurs with the segment fully flexed or extended and involves a single segment. Neutral mechanics do not eliminate coupling.
  30. The correct answer is C (Pseudomonas aeruginosa, an oxidase-positive gram-negative rod producing pyocyanin and linked to puncture wounds through footwear): Pseudomonas aeruginosa classically causes osteomyelitis after puncture wounds through the sole of a shoe, because it thrives in the moist foam and rubber. Blue-green pigment from pyocyanin and a grape-like odor are characteristic, and the oxidase-positive gram-negative rod fits the profile. Staphylococcus aureus is more common overall but lacks the pigment. Clostridium and Pasteurella produce different clinical pictures.
  31. The correct answer is A (The phosphagen system, which uses stored creatine phosphate to regenerate ATP rapidly): Muscle holds only a few seconds of ATP, and creatine phosphate donates its phosphate to ADP through creatine kinase to cover roughly the first 10 seconds of maximal effort. Fast glycolysis takes over next and carries effort to about two minutes. Oxidative phosphorylation and fatty acid oxidation are too slow to meet this immediate demand and dominate prolonged, lower intensity work.
  32. The correct answer is B (Rounded corticated margins, with a similar ossicle often present in the opposite knee): A bipartite patella is an unfused accessory ossification center at the superolateral margin. Its edges are smooth and corticated, and it is bilateral in a large share of cases, so a film of the other knee helps. An acute fracture shows sharp, non-corticated edges, soft tissue swelling, and often displacement. Inferior pole findings with tendon thickening suggest a traction injury and are not the superolateral variant.
  33. The correct answer is A (Grade II, a displacement between 25 and 50 percent of the sacral width): The Meyerding system divides the sacral body width into quarters, so grade I is up to 25 percent, grade II is 25 to 50, grade III is 50 to 75, grade IV is 75 to 100, and complete displacement is grade V. A 30 percent slip falls in the second quarter and is grade II. The other options state incorrect percentage bands.
  34. The correct answer is B (Send him to the emergency department for urgent neuroimaging by ambulance): A thunderclap headache described as the worst ever, with neck pain and vomiting, is subarachnoid hemorrhage until proven otherwise and delay can cost life, so this is an emergency referral. Manipulation could worsen bleeding or injury. Cervical films cannot show intracranial blood. An outpatient MRI next week is far too slow for a suspected bleed.
  35. The correct answer is C (Twisting the lumbar spine toward its rotational end range before the thrust, stressing disc and facets): Lumbar facets lie in a sagittal and slightly oblique plane, so the segment tolerates little rotation. Pre-stressing the spine with excess twist adds shear to the disc and facets and makes the thrust less specific. Flexing the upper leg, stabilizing the shoulder, and taking out slack are all normal parts of the setup and help the thrust remain short and localized.
  36. The correct answer is D (Without p53 there is no p21 induction to block cyclin-dependent kinases, so cells pass G1/S unrepaired): The p53 protein senses DNA damage and induces p21, which inhibits cyclin-dependent kinases and holds cells at G1/S, giving time for repair or apoptosis. With loss of function the brake is gone, so damaged cells replicate and mutations accumulate. Mismatch repair is carried out by other proteins, and a constitutively active receptor describes an oncogene.
  37. The correct answer is D (A rotational high-velocity thrust to the upper cervical segments): An atlantodental interval above 3 mm in an adult indicates transverse ligament insufficiency, and in rheumatoid arthritis this risks cord compression with any forceful upper cervical motion. A rotational thrust is the highest-risk maneuver and is absolutely contraindicated here. The remaining options either do not load C1-C2 or keep the neck neutral, so they can be used while the case is referred.
  38. The correct answer is B (Thiamine deficiency, which impairs pyruvate dehydrogenase and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase and limits neuronal aerobic ATP production): Confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia form the triad of Wernicke encephalopathy from thiamine deficiency. Thiamine pyrophosphate is a cofactor for pyruvate dehydrogenase and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, so neurons cannot oxidize glucose efficiently. A glucose load before thiamine raises the demand on these enzymes and can precipitate or worsen the encephalopathy, which is the stem detail that decides. Niacin, pyridoxine and cobalamin deficiencies cause other syndromes.
  39. The correct answer is C (Ca2+ binds troponin C, shifting tropomyosin and exposing myosin binding sites on actin): In skeletal muscle, Ca2+ binds troponin C, which moves tropomyosin off the myosin binding sites of actin so cross-bridges can form. The calmodulin and myosin light chain kinase pathway belongs to smooth muscle. ATP binding detaches myosin from actin, and ADP release occurs during the power stroke after attachment.
  40. The correct answer is B (Glossopharyngeal, vagus and accessory nerves, with the internal jugular vein beginning there): The jugular foramen transmits cranial nerves IX, X and XI and the beginning of the internal jugular vein, explaining the hoarseness, dysphagia and weak shrug. The facial and vestibulocochlear nerves pass through the internal acoustic meatus. The oculomotor, trochlear, abducens and ophthalmic nerves traverse the superior orbital fissure. The hypoglossal nerve uses the hypoglossal canal.
  41. The correct answer is B (Heme pigment from muscle breakdown, so rhabdomyolysis with myoglobinuria, needing urgent hydration and emergency referral): The dipstick detects the heme moiety, so large blood with only 0 to 2 red cells per high-power field means a heme pigment, not intact cells. Clear plasma argues against hemoglobin from hemolysis, which turns plasma pink, and a CK of 18,000 U/L after extreme exertion confirms rhabdomyolysis with myoglobinuria. This needs aggressive fluids and emergency care to prevent acute kidney injury, and glomerular bleeding would show dysmorphic red cells.
  42. The correct answer is A (A vertebral body, with a posterior tubercle in place of a spinous process): The atlas has no vertebral body, which embryologically joins C2 as the dens, and it has no spinous process, only a small posterior tubercle. It does have transverse foramina for the vertebral artery, lateral masses with superior and inferior articular facets, and a posterior arch. The transverse ligament holds the dens against the anterior arch.
Case N-B18

Patient: M, 50 y, 70 in, 262 lb

Vitals: Pulse 80, resp 16, BP 138/86 mmHg, temp 98.4 F

Chief complaint and history: Office manager, BMI 37.6. Six weeks of burning and numbness over the anterolateral right thigh, worse with standing, walking and tight belts or jeans, relieved by sitting. No back pain, no weakness, no bowel or bladder change. Gained 30 lb in the past year.

Examination: Reduced light touch and pinprick over a palm-sized area of the anterolateral right thigh; medial thigh and leg sensation normal. Tender medial to the anterior superior iliac spine near the inguinal ligament. Gait normal. Hip motion full and painless.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Quadriceps, hip flexor and adductor strength 5/5. Patellar and Achilles reflexes 2+ bilaterally. Tinel near the inguinal ligament reproduces burning. Sitting pelvic compression for 45 seconds reproduces symptoms. Femoral stretch and straight leg raise negative.

Laboratory and imaging: Fasting glucose 118 mg/dL, hemoglobin A1c 6.1 percent. Lumbar radiographs 2 years ago: mild L4-L5 disc narrowing. No history of pelvic surgery or abdominal mass.

  1. The correct answers are D and E: A sharply bounded patch of sensory change over the anterolateral thigh with normal medial thigh sensation matches the lateral femoral cutaneous nerve territory, and positional provocation by belts and standing with a local Tinel sign places the entrapment near the inguinal ligament. Old disc narrowing is incidental when there is no back pain or motor sign. Glucose and weight gain are risk factors, not localizers.
  2. The correct answers are A and D: A pure sensory nerve causes no weakness, so normal L2 to L4 strength and a normal patellar reflex argue against an upper lumbar root lesion. Reproduction of burning at the inguinal ligament and with sustained pelvic compression localizes the entrapment. The femoral stretch is negative in this chart, hip rotation findings do not prove the site, and the straight leg raise mainly tests lower roots.
  3. The correct answers are A and E: Most cases resolve once the compression is removed, and weight loss lowers recurrence risk. A glucose follow-up is reasonable because he has prediabetic values, and further workup is warranted if symptoms progress or do not improve after about 3 months. Lumbar traction targets a root that is not involved. Surgery is a last resort, and there is no motor loss to strengthen.
  4. The correct answer is C (Prolonged static loading of cervical ligaments and extensor muscles from habitual sustained postural strain): The symptoms build with hours at the desk and resolve on weekends, which points to cumulative postural loading of ligaments and muscles rather than a structural or traumatic cause. Sustained forward head posture raises the load on the posterior cervical tissues and alters segmental mechanics over time. A congenital fusion would not fluctuate with work, and a single traction tear would have a dated onset. Degenerative disc narrowing develops slowly and would not ease on a weekend.
  5. The correct answer is C (Blastic metastases from prostate cancer, with a raised prostate specific antigen): Multiple dense foci in the pelvis and femora of an older man with urinary symptoms and weight loss are osteoblastic metastases, and prostate cancer is the classic source. Prostate specific antigen is the matching marker. Paget disease enlarges and thickens individual bones, myeloma is lytic and causes a monoclonal spike, and osteopetrosis is a generalized sclerosing disorder of younger patients.
  6. The correct answer is C (Right lumbar sprain and strain, no neurologic deficit, no red flags identified at this visit): A strong Assessment states a specific working impression supported by the findings and also records that red flags and neurologic deficits were looked for. Back pain is a symptom, not an impression. A disc herniation with root compromise is not supported by negative neurologic testing, and a vague probable problem gives no specific impression to guide the plan.
  7. The correct answer is D (Cryptococcus neoformans, an encapsulated yeast acquired by inhaling aerosolized dried bird droppings from contaminated soil): A capsule excludes India ink and appears as a clear halo around a budding yeast, which in an AIDS patient with meningitis points to Cryptococcus neoformans. Pigeon droppings are a classic source, and raised opening pressure is typical. Candida lacks a capsule and rarely causes meningitis, Histoplasma is a small intracellular yeast seen in macrophages, and Toxoplasma is a protozoan that produces mass lesions rather than yeast on the stain.
  8. The correct answer is C (Primary syphilis, treated with one intramuscular dose of benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units): A single painless indurated chancre with nontender nodes is primary syphilis, caused by Treponema pallidum. A single intramuscular dose of benzathine penicillin G 2.4 million units cures early syphilis. The weekly three-dose course is for late latent disease. Chancroid and herpes ulcers are painful, so their treatments do not apply.
  9. The correct answer is C (Bronchogenic carcinoma, the cause that requires prompt chest imaging in a long term smoker): Acquired clubbing with weight loss in a heavy smoker points to lung cancer until proven otherwise, so chest imaging is urgent. Uncomplicated COPD does not cause clubbing, and its presence should prompt a search for another process. Endocarditis requires fever or a new murmur to rise to the top of the list. Cirrhosis can cause clubbing but is not the leading cause and has no support in this history.
  10. The correct answer is C (A high-velocity thrust that loads the fused L4-L5 segments): A fused segment does not move, so a thrust passing through it transfers force to the adjacent levels and risks hardware and adjacent segment stress. Adjacent segment pain is common above a fusion. Gentle mobilization above the fusion, soft tissue work and stabilization exercises are acceptable alternatives that avoid loading the fused levels.
  11. The correct answer is A (Syringomyelia, a cavity in the central cervical cord that damages crossing spinothalamic fibers): A central cavity interrupts spinothalamic fibers as they cross in the anterior commissure, so pain and temperature are lost across the shoulders and arms while the dorsal columns keep touch and vibration. Enlargement into the anterior horns weakens and wastes the hand muscles. Anterior spinal artery syndrome is sudden and affects levels below the lesion. Brown-Sequard is unilateral, and combined degeneration spares pain and temperature.
  12. The correct answer is D (Right lower lobe bronchus, reached through the shorter, wider and more vertical right main bronchus): The right main bronchus is shorter, wider and runs more vertically than the left, so aspirated material tends to pass into it and then into the right lower lobe bronchus. The left main bronchus is longer, narrower and angled more horizontally beneath the aortic arch, making aspiration there less common. The right upper lobe bronchus branches off high and is less in line with the airstream.
  13. The correct answer is A (Tyrosine, which is iodinated in thyroglobulin for thyroid hormones and hydroxylated to DOPA for catecholamines): Thyroid hormones are made by iodination of tyrosine residues within thyroglobulin, and the catecholamines are made from tyrosine by tyrosine hydroxylase to DOPA, then dopamine, norepinephrine and epinephrine. Tryptophan is the precursor for serotonin and melatonin, and histidine gives histamine. Phenylalanine must first be converted to tyrosine by phenylalanine hydroxylase before either pathway can proceed.
  14. The correct answer is D (Myelomeningocele, a sac holding meninges, spinal fluid and cord tissue or nerve roots): Myelomeningocele is the most severe common neural tube defect in which the sac contains neural tissue as well as meninges and fluid. Neural tissue explains the leg weakness and bladder dysfunction. A meningocele has no neural elements and usually produces few deficits. Spina bifida occulta and tethered cord present without a sac.
  15. The correct answer is B (Marked hypolordosis, about 26 degrees below the lower reference limit): A posterior tangent angle of 5 degrees compared with the lower reference limit of 31 degrees gives a deficit of 26 degrees, which is marked hypolordosis. A kyphotic curve would be recorded as a negative value, not a small positive one. The measurement itself is not the deficit, so calling it a 5 degree shortfall misapplies the number. The finding is a descriptive radiographic result and must be correlated with the clinical picture.
  16. The correct answer is C (Lower than 51 pounds, because horizontal distance and asymmetry multipliers both fall below 1.0): The load constant of 51 pounds applies only under ideal conditions with the load close to the body, no twisting, and a favorable height. Each deviation multiplies the constant by a factor less than 1.0, and horizontal distance and asymmetry are both included. Holding the load 20 inches away and twisting therefore lowers the limit well below 51 pounds.
  17. The correct answer is A (Rightward shift, lowering hemoglobin affinity and unloading more oxygen to tissues at a given tissue PO2): Fever, acidosis and raised CO2 all stabilize the tense, low affinity form of hemoglobin, shifting the curve to the right. This lowers affinity and releases more oxygen to tissues at the same PO2, which is the Bohr effect. Raised 2,3-BPG shifts the curve in the same direction, while a left shift would impair unloading and the number of binding sites per hemoglobin is fixed.
  18. The correct answer is B (Colles fracture of the distal radius with dorsal angulation of the distal fragment): A fall on the outstretched hand in an older, osteopenic woman produces a transverse distal radial metaphyseal fracture with dorsal angulation, the classic Colles pattern, often with an ulnar styloid fracture. A Smith fracture angulates volarly after a fall on a flexed wrist. A scaphoid fracture sits in the carpus, and a Barton fracture involves the articular rim with carpal subluxation.
  19. The correct answer is A (Skip cryotherapy; use a brief low-intensity warm pack with skin checks and gentle active motion): Raynaud phenomenon is a cold-triggered vasospastic disorder, so cryotherapy is contraindicated even in short doses or through a barrier. Gentle warmth with skin checks and early active motion address the strain without provoking vasospasm. Cold sprays also cool the skin and can trigger the same response.
  20. The correct answer is D (Have you noticed blood, a change in color, or burning when you urinate?): Pain that does not vary with position or movement should prompt a visceral screen, and urinary changes such as red or dark urine, blood and burning are the key renal and urinary questions. Foot symptoms, positional relief and mechanical exposures help characterize a musculoskeletal problem, but none of them screens for a kidney source.
  21. The correct answer is C (Performance and detection bias, with expectations shaping treatment delivery and subjective reports of improvement): When clinician and patient know the treatment, expectations can change how care is delivered and how pain is reported, which are performance and detection biases. Selection bias is addressed by concealed randomization, attrition by follow-up completeness, and publication bias by trial registration and complete reporting.
  22. The correct answer is D (Head of the pancreas, lying against the posterior abdominal wall): The mnemonic SAD PUCKER lists the retroperitoneal organs: suprarenal glands, aorta and IVC, duodenum, pancreas except the tail, ureters, colon (ascending and descending), kidneys, and esophagus and rectum. The head of the pancreas lies against the posterior wall. The stomach, transverse colon, small bowel and spleen are intraperitoneal.
  23. The correct answer is A (Calcific aortic valve stenosis causing fixed left ventricular outflow obstruction): The triad of exertional syncope, a systolic ejection murmur radiating to the carotids and a slow-rising pulse with a soft second sound is classic for aortic stenosis, which in older adults is usually calcific. Mitral regurgitation is holosystolic at the apex and radiates to the axilla. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy murmurs change with Valsalva and squatting, which this one does not. Aortic regurgitation produces a diastolic murmur and a wide pulse pressure.
  24. The correct answer is B (Calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate with weakly positive birefringence, forming rhomboid crystals): Pseudogout is caused by calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate crystals, which are rhomboid and weakly positive, and appear blue when parallel to the axis. Chondrocalcinosis supports the diagnosis. Urate crystals are needle shaped and negatively birefringent, appearing yellow when parallel, and cholesterol crystals are not typical.
  25. The correct answer is A (Osteosarcoma, whose spiculated and interrupted periosteal reactions show rapid growth through the cortex): Osteosarcoma most often arises in the metaphysis of the distal femur or proximal tibia in adolescents and makes mixed sclerotic and lytic bone with soft tissue extension. Tumor outgrowing the periosteum produces sunburst spicules and a Codman triangle where the periosteum is lifted. Ewing sarcoma favors the diaphysis, and a cyst or old infection gives expansile or solid reactions rather than a spiculated one.
  26. The correct answer is A (Oxygen content is markedly reduced and the curve shifts left, impairing unloading despite normal PaO2): Carbon monoxide binds hemoglobin with about 200 times the affinity of oxygen, so fewer sites carry oxygen and total content falls. The remaining oxyhemoglobin holds oxygen more tightly, shifting the curve left. PaO2 reflects only dissolved oxygen, and standard oximeters misread carboxyhemoglobin as oxyhemoglobin, which explains the normal readings and cherry red skin.
  27. The correct answer is A (Apparent inequality, because the ASIS to malleolus lengths match and pelvic or spinal factors explain it): ASIS to medial malleolus spans the bony lower limb, so equal values mean the leg bones are the same length. The umbilicus to malleolus difference crosses the pelvis and therefore reflects pelvic obliquity, rotation or spinal factors, which defines an apparent inequality. Radiographs give the best quantification but tape measurement already separates the two categories clinically.
Case N-B08

Patient: F, 66 y, 63 in, 128 lb

Vitals: Pulse 84, resp 16, BP 134/80 mmHg, temp 98.4 F

Chief complaint and history: Retired bookkeeper with 3 months of progressive mid back pain, worse at night and not eased by lying still. Fatigue, 12 lb unintentional weight loss and constipation. Felt a snap in the right lateral chest while coughing 2 weeks ago, with focal pain since. No fever. Takes lisinopril and calcium. Never smoked. No cancer history.

Examination: Pale. Point tenderness over the T8 spinous process and the right 7th rib laterally. Thoracic percussion painful. Mild thoracic kyphosis. Cervical and lumbar motion full. Lungs clear. Abdomen soft, no mass. Height 1.5 inches less than on her licence from 10 years ago.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Kemp test and straight leg raise negative. Rib spring test reproduces localized right lateral pain. Lower limb strength 5/5, reflexes 2+, plantar responses flexor. Sensation intact. Gait normal. No clonus.

Laboratory and imaging: Hemoglobin 9.8 g/dL (12 to 16), MCV 90 fL, calcium 11.2 mg/dL (8.5 to 10.5), creatinine 1.5 mg/dL (0.6 to 1.1), total protein 9.4 g/dL (6.0 to 8.3), albumin 3.4 g/dL, ESR 98 mm/hr. Thoracic film not yet obtained.

  1. The correct answers are A and B: Pain that does not ease with rest, unexplained weight loss and a fracture from minimal force in an older patient are classic red flags for malignancy or pathologic bone weakness. Constipation here is better explained by hypercalcemia than by bowel disease. Lisinopril is not a cause of bone pain. Negative orthopedic tests for lumbar nerve root involvement say nothing about a thoracic or systemic process.
  2. The correct answers are D and E: The protein gap is 9.4 minus 3.4, or 6.0 g/dL, which indicates excess immunoglobulin, and with anemia, hypercalcemia, renal impairment, a very high ESR and lytic bone disease this fits multiple myeloma. Osteoporosis does not raise globulins or the ESR this much. Metastatic carcinoma seldom raises globulins, and hyperparathyroidism would not explain the anemia, high ESR and protein gap.
  3. The correct answers are A and B: Serum protein electrophoresis with immunofixation identifies the monoclonal immunoglobulin, and a skeletal survey shows punched out lytic lesions, fractures and diffuse osteopenia. A bone scan relies on osteoblastic activity and often misses myeloma lesions. DEXA measures density, not marrow infiltration. Parathyroid hormone testing addresses one cause of hypercalcemia but would not confirm the plasma cell disorder.
  4. The correct answer is D (Ipsilateral lateral flexion of the trunk, and fixation of the twelfth rib against the diaphragm): Quadratus lumborum bends the trunk to its own side when one muscle contracts, and both together assist extension. It anchors the twelfth rib so the diaphragm can pull against a stable base during inspiration. Hip flexion and lordosis belong to psoas major. The muscle does not rotate the trunk or act on the first rib.
  5. The correct answer is C (The stabilization hand: state that the cephalad hand holds the shoulder to fix the torso): The torso rotates freely because no stabilizing contact has been named, so the missing element is the stabilization hand, which holds the upper shoulder or arm to maintain torso position during the thrust. Thrust speed, a breathing cue and table height are real parts of an adjustment, but none of them fixes the patient's torso. A complete technique statement names the stabilizing contact explicitly so the examiner can score it.
  6. The correct answer is C (Osteoporosis has a reduced amount of normally mineralized bone, whereas osteomalacia has unmineralized osteoid): Osteoporosis is a loss of bone mass in which the remaining bone is normally mineralized, and calcium, phosphate and alkaline phosphatase are usually normal. In osteomalacia and rickets, lack of vitamin D leaves osteoid unmineralized. The two diseases are therefore not the same and are not reversed.
  7. The correct answer is A (Anterior horn motor neurons in the spinal cord, producing lower motor neuron paralysis with preserved sensation): Poliovirus infects and destroys anterior horn motor neurons, so the limb shows flaccid, asymmetric lower motor neuron paralysis with hypotonia and absent reflexes, and sensation is spared. Dorsal root ganglion injury would impair sensation. Corticospinal tract disease gives spasticity and hyperreflexia. Junction disease such as myasthenia gravis causes fatigable symmetric weakness.
  8. The correct answer is C (External branch of the superior laryngeal nerve, supplying the cricothyroid muscle that tenses the vocal folds): The external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve runs beside the superior thyroid artery and supplies cricothyroid, which tenses the vocal folds for high pitch. Injury during high ligation causes a monotone voice and loss of range. The recurrent nerve lies near the inferior thyroid artery and injury causes hoarseness, while the internal branch is sensory above the folds.
  9. The correct answer is B (Right hemisection of the cord, because spinothalamic fibers cross near entry and other tracts cross higher): The corticospinal tract and dorsal columns cross in the medulla, so a hemisection causes weakness and vibration loss on the same side as the lesion. Spinothalamic fibers cross within a segment or two of entering, so pain and temperature loss occurs on the opposite side. A right hemisection therefore causes right weakness and left pain loss. Central and anterior cord syndromes would be bilateral.
  10. The correct answer is C (C5 and C6, documented as 2+ and symmetric when both sides respond briskly and equally): The biceps reflex is mediated mainly by C5 with C6 contribution through the musculocutaneous nerve. A normal response is graded 2+ and is recorded together with the opposite side, since symmetry matters as much as the grade. A 4+ response suggests an upper motor neuron lesion and a 1+ response suggests diminished reflex activity. The C6 and C7 pairing belongs closer to the brachioradialis and triceps reflexes.
  11. The correct answer is A (Chronic aortic regurgitation, with large stroke volume and rapid diastolic runoff back into the ventricle): Blood flowing backward through an incompetent aortic valve lowers diastolic pressure, while the larger stroke volume raises systolic pressure, giving a wide pulse pressure here of 110 mmHg. Bounding pulses and head bobbing follow the same physiology. A decrescendo diastolic murmur right after the second sound confirms the diagnosis. Aortic stenosis narrows pulse pressure, and the mitral lesions produce murmurs in other timing and locations.
  12. The correct answer is A (The end product binds an allosteric site on enzyme 1, the first committed step, lowering activity): Feedback inhibition is allosteric: the pathway end product binds a regulatory site on the first committed enzyme and reduces activity immediately, saving intermediates. Because it acts on existing protein, the effect appears in seconds, unlike repression of transcription, which needs hours. Inhibiting the last enzyme would let intermediates accumulate wastefully. The stem rules out gene expression changes.
  13. The correct answer is A (Primary hyperparathyroidism, with subperiosteal resorption on the radial side of the middle phalanges): With hypercalcemia and a high parathyroid hormone level, the diagnosis is primary hyperparathyroidism. Subperiosteal resorption on the radial side of the middle phalanges of the second and third fingers is its most specific radiographic sign. Acro-osteolysis and skull changes occur but are less specific, and sarcoidosis would have a low parathyroid hormone level.
  14. The correct answer is D (Restricted left sacroiliac joint motion, indicating fixation on the left side): On hip flexion the PSIS of the moving side should glide inferiorly relative to the sacrum. When it does not drop, the sacroiliac joint on that side is fixed, here the left. The stance side is the reference, not the side that is flexed. Hypertonic iliopsoas would limit hip flexion height but would not by itself prevent the PSIS landmark from dropping.
  15. The correct answer is C (Slipped capital femoral epiphysis, with the femoral head displaced on the neck through the physis): Slipped capital femoral epiphysis typically affects obese adolescents and often refers pain to the knee. Limited internal rotation and obligatory external rotation on hip flexion point to the hip, and Perthes disease occurs at younger ages. The knee itself is normal, which excludes tibial tubercle and patellofemoral disorders. Delay in diagnosis risks avascular necrosis, so non weight-bearing referral is needed.
  16. The correct answer is D (Type IIx fast glycolytic fibers, which fatigue quickly but give the greatest peak force and speed): Type IIx fibers are large, glycolytic, and contract fastest with the highest force, so they dominate in elite sprinters. Their sparse mitochondria match the biopsy finding and explain their rapid fatigue. Type I fibers are slow, oxidative, and typical of the marathon runner. Type IIa fibers are intermediate and mitochondria-rich, which does not fit a sample with few mitochondria and abundant glycogen.
  17. The correct answer is B (Klippel-Feil syndrome, a congenital failure of cervical segmentation): Short neck, low posterior hairline, limited motion and congenital fusion of cervical segments define Klippel-Feil syndrome, and Sprengel deformity of the scapula often accompanies it. Juvenile arthritis can fuse upper cervical facets but would show inflammatory signs. Os odontoideum involves one segment, and ankylosing spondylitis is rare in children and causes bridging syndesmophytes rather than congenital block vertebrae.
  18. The correct answer is C (Osteoarthritis, in which osteophytes and cartilage loss produce Heberden nodes at the distal interphalangeal joints): Heberden nodes are osteophytes at the distal interphalangeal joints, typical of osteoarthritis, with brief morning stiffness and use-related pain. Rheumatoid arthritis spares the distal joints, favors the metacarpophalangeal and proximal joints and causes prolonged stiffness. Psoriatic arthritis requires skin or nail findings, and gout presents with acute attacks.
  19. The correct answer is B (Paget disease of bone with an enlarged vertebral body and thickened cortices): An enlarged vertebral body with a thick, dense outer border and coarse trabeculae is the picture frame vertebra of Paget disease. Skull enlargement and a raised alkaline phosphatase with normal calcium support it. A blastic metastasis leaves the body its normal size, osteoporosis lowers density, and fluorosis needs a known exposure and shows ligament calcification elsewhere.
  20. The correct answer is D (Urgent medical referral for bleeding evaluation including head imaging, plus a full medication review): Warfarin with an INR of 4.6 plus an NSAID raises bleeding risk, and a fall with new confusion in an anticoagulated patient suggests intracranial hemorrhage until proven otherwise. She needs urgent medical evaluation with head imaging. The combination of warfarin, a sedative and an NSAID is also a classic polypharmacy hazard that needs review. Manipulation or watchful waiting would be dangerous.
  21. The correct answer is A (Expectation, attention, and clinician interaction that could improve symptoms apart from the specific thrust): A sham procedure keeps the ritual, touch, and attention constant so that the added effect of the real thrust can be isolated from expectation and interaction effects. Natural history is addressed by a no-treatment arm. Measurement error is handled by rater training and valid instruments, and selection bias is handled by concealed randomization.
  22. The correct answer is B (Infectious mononucleosis caused by Epstein-Barr virus infecting B lymphocytes through the CD21 receptor): Fever, pharyngitis, posterior cervical adenopathy and atypical lymphocytes in a teenager suggest mononucleosis, and a positive heterophile antibody test confirms Epstein-Barr virus. The virus enters B cells via CD21, and the atypical cells are reactive CD8 T cells. Cytomegalovirus and acute HIV can mimic the syndrome but give negative heterophile tests. Group A streptococcal pharyngitis does not produce atypical lymphocytosis.
  23. The correct answer is B (Polymyalgia rheumatica, an inflammatory girdle condition that responds quickly to low-dose corticosteroids): Bilateral shoulder and hip girdle stiffness lasting over an hour in a patient older than 50 with a very high ESR indicates polymyalgia rheumatica. Strength is normal, and apparent weakness comes from pain. Rotator cuff disease is usually unilateral with normal inflammatory markers. Polymyositis produces true weakness and raised creatine kinase, and fibromyalgia does not raise ESR.
  24. The correct answer is D (Obtain thoracic imaging and avoid manipulating the region until a compression fracture is excluded): Long-term corticosteroids cause bone loss, so even a minor load such as a sneeze can produce an osteoporotic compression fracture. Focal tenderness supports this, and imaging should come before treatment while manipulation of the region is withheld. Reduced-force manipulation still risks the weakened vertebra. Waiting two weeks delays diagnosis. Tapering the steroid does not address the new pain.
  25. The correct answer is B (Primary thyroid failure from autoimmune thyroiditis, with high TSH and low free T4): A markedly elevated TSH of 18.6 with low free T4 of 0.5 indicates failure at the gland, and strongly positive anti-TPO antibodies identify Hashimoto thyroiditis as the cause. The raised cholesterol and slowed reflexes fit. Central disease would have low or inappropriately normal TSH. Subclinical disease has a normal free T4, and Graves disease would show suppressed TSH with high free T4.
  26. The correct answer is A (Common fibular nerve, with numbness over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot): The common fibular nerve winds around the neck of the fibula where it lies superficial and is vulnerable to a tight cast. Injury produces foot drop, loss of dorsiflexion and eversion, and numbness over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot. Tibial injury would weaken plantar flexion and affect the sole, and femoral injury affects the thigh. A high sciatic lesion would also weaken the hamstrings, which are normal here.
  27. The correct answer is D (Absent bile salts reduce vitamin K absorption, so factors II, VII, IX and X are underproduced): Bile salts form micelles that carry dietary fat and fat-soluble vitamins to the intestinal wall. With the common bile duct blocked, fat is lost in the stool as steatorrhea, and vitamins A, D, E and K are poorly absorbed. Vitamin K is needed to carboxylate factors II, VII, IX and X, so their deficiency prolongs the prothrombin time and causes bruising. Water-soluble vitamins do not need bile for absorption, and the prolonged prothrombin time points toward clotting factors rather than collagen or platelets.
  28. The correct answer is C (Right sacroiliac fixation, treated with a low-amplitude posterior to anterior thrust on the PSIS): A Gillet test in which the PSIS does not descend marks hypomobility of the sacroiliac joint, and the negative active straight leg raise argues against laxity. A FABER test that causes stiffness without groin pain also makes primary hip disease less likely. A fixed sacroiliac joint is reasonably treated with a low-amplitude adjustment, whereas laxity or a sprain would call for stabilization instead.
  29. The correct answer is A (Neutrophils at twelve hours, followed by macrophages predominating by the third day): In acute inflammation neutrophils arrive within the first hours, peak around 24 hours, and are replaced by monocytes that mature into macrophages over the next one to three days. Macrophages clear debris and drive repair. The reversed order is wrong, and eosinophils and plasma cells belong to allergic or chronic responses. Lymphocytes dominate chronic or viral inflammation.
  30. The correct answer is B (Call emergency services immediately, note the time symptoms began, and keep him seated and monitored.): Sudden one sided weakness with slurred speech is a stroke until proven otherwise, and the time of onset determines eligibility for clot dissolving treatment. The doctor calls emergency services at once and records the onset time. Neck testing or adjusting could worsen a vascular event, and waiting an hour or scheduling a scan next week wastes critical time.
  31. The correct answer is D (Rheumatoid arthritis, an inflammatory polyarthritis of small hand joints with synovial proliferation): Symmetric swelling of the MCP and PIP joints with over an hour of morning stiffness and positive rheumatoid factor and anti-CCP antibodies is typical of rheumatoid arthritis. Osteoarthritis spares the MCP joints early and stiffens only briefly. Psoriatic arthritis tends to be asymmetric and is seronegative. Hemochromatosis arthropathy causes bony changes at the second and third MCP joints without these serologic findings.
  32. The correct answer is A (Raise the kVp from 80 to 92 and keep the mAs the same): The 15 percent rule says a 15 percent increase in kVp has about the same effect on density as doubling the mAs, and 80 times 1.15 equals 92. The higher kVp penetrates better and lowers the dose compared with doubling the mAs, but it produces longer scale and lower contrast. A 5 percent increase gives only a small gain. Dropping kVp by 15 percent halves density, and a longer distance also reduces exposure.
  33. The correct answer is D (Calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition in cartilage, associated with hemochromatosis and hyperparathyroidism): Linear calcification of the triangular fibrocartilage and radiocarpal cartilage with scapholunate widening indicates calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate deposition disease. Associated conditions include hemochromatosis, hyperparathyroidism, hypomagnesemia and hypophosphatasia.
