A low-amplitude force on the humeral head in the restricted direction fits an extremity adjustment. A suspected cuff tear or bursitis would not be treated with a thrust.
Case N-B02

Patient: F, 52 y, 65 in, 188 lb

Vitals: Pulse 76, resp 16, BP 134/84 mmHg, temp 98.4 F

Chief complaint and history: Data entry clerk, right hand dominant, with 4 months of numbness and tingling in the right hand that wakes her at night and eases when she shakes the hand. She drops her coffee cup. Neck feels normal with no arm pain above the wrist. Hypertension controlled with medication. Menopause 2 years ago.

Examination: BMI 31. Cervical range of motion full and painless. Mild reduction of right thenar muscle bulk compared with the left. Right grip 38 lb, left 52 lb. Skin of the thenar eminence normal in color and temperature. Radial pulses 2+ bilaterally.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Phalen test reproduces tingling in the thumb, index and middle fingers in 40 seconds. Tinel sign over the carpal tunnel positive. Spurling compression, cervical distraction and Adson tests negative. Biceps and brachioradialis reflexes 2+ bilaterally. Abductor pollicis brevis 4/5. Sensation reduced over palmar thumb, index and middle fingertips but normal over the thenar eminence.

Laboratory and imaging: Cervical radiographs: mild C5-6 disc narrowing with small anterior osteophytes. TSH 2.1 mIU/L. Fasting glucose 98 mg/dL. Hemoglobin A1c 5.6 percent. Rheumatoid factor negative. Wrist radiographs normal.

  1. The correct answers are A and B: The palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve leaves before the carpal tunnel, so thenar eminence sensation is spared in carpal tunnel syndrome but usually involved in a C6 lesion. Normal biceps and brachioradialis reflexes and negative cervical provocation tests argue against a C6 root problem. Thumb and index numbness fits both conditions. Night symptoms and keyboard work raise suspicion but do not separate the two.
  2. The correct answers are B and D: Phalen test holds the wrist in flexion to raise carpal tunnel pressure, and Tinel sign taps the median nerve at the retinaculum, so each stresses the nerve at the wrist. Spurling compression and cervical distraction assess the cervical roots. The Adson maneuver evaluates for thoracic outlet vascular compromise. All three of these were negative in the chart, which fits a distal lesion.
  3. The correct answers are B and D: A neutral splint at night keeps carpal tunnel pressure lowest, and workstation changes reduce repetitive wrist loading. Electrodiagnostic testing and surgical opinion follow if conservative care fails or weakness progresses. Surgery is considered for constant numbness, marked thenar wasting or failed conservative care, not mild weakness at the first visit. Wrist flexion raises canal pressure, and neck manipulation does not address a distal entrapment.
  4. The correct answer is D (Idiopathic Parkinson disease from loss of dopaminergic neurons in the substantia nigra): A unilateral resting tremor that fades with movement, bradykinesia, micrographia and cogwheel rigidity make up the classic parkinsonian picture. Essential tremor is an action tremor. Drug induced parkinsonism is usually symmetric and needs a causative drug, which is absent. Cerebellar disease causes tremor that grows as the hand nears the target.
  5. The correct answer is B (Peptide hormones bind surface receptors and use second messengers like cAMP, while steroids bind intracellular receptors): Peptide hormones are hydrophilic and cannot cross the membrane, so they bind surface receptors and signal through second messengers such as cAMP or phosphoinositide. Steroid and thyroid hormones are lipophilic, bind intracellular receptors and alter gene transcription over hours. The statements that swap locations or put both classes in one compartment are incorrect.
  6. The correct answer is A (BMI of approximately 32 kg/m2, which falls in the class I obesity range): Height is 66 in, so BMI = 703 x 200 / 66 squared = 140,600 / 4,356, which is about 32.3 kg/m2. Checking again, 66 x 66 = 4,356 and 4,356 x 32 = 139,392, so the result sits just above 32. A BMI of 30.0 to 34.9 is class I obesity, so the overweight, class II and normal choices all come from arithmetic or height errors.
  7. The correct answer is B (HMG CoA reductase, the rate-limiting enzyme that converts HMG CoA to mevalonate in cholesterol synthesis): Statins competitively inhibit HMG CoA reductase, which converts HMG CoA to mevalonate and is the committed, rate-limiting step of cholesterol synthesis. Lower hepatic cholesterol upregulates LDL receptors and lowers plasma LDL. HMG CoA synthase acts earlier and is not the rate-limiting step. ACAT and lipoprotein lipase handle cholesterol esterification and triglyceride hydrolysis respectively.
  8. The correct answer is B (The traversing L5 root, passing behind the L4 to L5 disc toward the L5 foramen): A posterolateral herniation at L4 to L5 typically compresses the traversing L5 root rather than the exiting L4 root. The L5 root supplies extensor hallucis longus and the dorsal foot dermatome, which matches this laborer. Preserved knee reflexes argue against L4 involvement. Cauda equina compression would produce bilateral symptoms and sphincter disturbance.
  9. The correct answer is A (The appendix, whose base usually lies beneath this point on the anterior abdominal wall): McBurney point lies one third of the distance from the right anterior superior iliac spine to the umbilicus and marks the usual surface location of the appendix base. Tenderness there supports acute appendicitis after pain migrates from the periumbilical area. The cecum, ovary and terminal ileum lie nearby, but none is the structure the landmark identifies.
  10. The correct answer is C (Carotid sinus, a dilation at the origin of the internal carotid artery wall): The carotid sinus is a thin-walled dilation at the start of the internal carotid artery whose stretch receptors sense blood pressure and send afferents in the glossopharyngeal nerve. Compression raises firing and slows the heart, which explains the fainting. The carotid body at the bifurcation senses oxygen, carbon dioxide and pH, and the aortic bodies report through the vagus nerve.
  11. The correct answer is B (Anterior superior iliac spine to medial malleolus for true length, umbilicus to medial malleolus for apparent): True anatomic length runs from the anterior superior iliac spine to the medial malleolus, so it reflects only bone length. Apparent length starts at the umbilicus and so includes the effect of pelvic tilt and adduction contracture. A difference in apparent length with equal true lengths points to pelvic obliquity. The other pairs use landmarks that either move with the pelvis or stop short of the ankle.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The tested segment is dysfunctional and should be adjusted with the instrument): In the Pelvic Deficient Leg protocol, a reaction that evens out the short leg when a segment is tested identifies that segment as one that should be adjusted. A normal segment produces no change in leg length. A structural short leg would not change with brief segmental pressure, so it does not explain this response and does not call for a lift first.
  2. The correct answer is A (Reactive arthritis, an asymmetric lower limb spondyloarthropathy that follows genitourinary or enteric infection): Asymmetric lower limb arthritis with enthesitis at the Achilles and plantar fascia, plus conjunctivitis after urethritis, is reactive arthritis. Fluffy periostitis and erosions at the calcaneal attachments support this. Ankylosing spondylitis is symmetric and axial, psoriatic arthritis needs skin or nail disease here, and gout shows tophi and erosions with overhanging edges.
  3. The correct answer is D (Use low-force methods such as drop table or instrument adjusting, avoiding rotational high-velocity thrusts): A T-score of minus 2.5 or lower defines osteoporosis, and her height loss suggests possible vertebral compression fractures. Fracture risk rises with high-velocity, long-lever and rotational forces, so these are avoided. Low-force techniques remain reasonable and allow care to continue. Waiting for a normal scan is unnecessary because osteoporosis is a relative caution, not an absolute contraindication to gentle care.
  4. The correct answer is B (Acute pancreatitis, with premature activation of digestive enzymes causing autodigestion of the pancreatic tissue): Epigastric pain radiating to the back, relief on leaning forward, heavy alcohol use and a lipase more than three times normal establish acute pancreatitis. Enzymes such as trypsin are activated inside the gland and digest tissue. Perforated ulcer causes rigidity and free air, cholecystitis gives right upper quadrant findings with a Murphy sign, and mesenteric ischemia shows lactic acidosis with a soft abdomen.
  5. The correct answer is D (Feet together, eyes closed; loss of balance only when eyes close implicates dorsal column proprioception.): The Romberg test asks the patient to stand with feet together, first with eyes open and then closed. Swaying or falling only after the eyes close means vision was compensating for lost position sense, pointing to a dorsal column or sensory ataxia problem. Cerebellar ataxia is unsteady even with eyes open. Tandem gait and the single leg stance are different tests with different meanings.
  6. The correct answer is A (Benign prostatic hyperplasia with partial bladder outlet obstruction and incomplete emptying at night): Gradual obstructive voiding symptoms with nocturia and a smooth, symmetric, rubbery gland in a man of this age are classic for benign prostatic hyperplasia. Acute prostatitis would give fever, dysuria and a very tender, boggy gland. Cancer typically produces a hard, irregular nodule or asymmetry, which is absent here. Cauda equina compression would add saddle sensory loss, reduced anal tone and often bilateral leg findings, none of which are described.
  7. The correct answer is D (Raise the monitor so its top is at eye level and add lumbar support): A monitor with its top edge at eye level keeps the head over the shoulders and lowers cervical load, and lumbar support maintains the natural lordosis that reduces low back strain. Lowering the screen increases forward head posture. A higher keyboard worsens wrist and shoulder positioning, and a backless stool or side-placed monitor adds sustained trunk or neck rotation.
  8. The correct answer is C (Turning the face to the left, away from the injured muscle): The sternocleidomastoid, supplied by the spinal accessory nerve, rotates the face to the opposite side, so right-sided weakness weakens turning the face left. Ipsilateral side bending is shared with other muscles and is only mildly affected. Bilateral action produces flexion, so the intact left muscle masks any deficit in forward flexion.
  9. The correct answer is C (Joint effusion from an occult supracondylar fracture, so immobilize the elbow and repeat films): At 90 degrees of flexion the posterior fat pad lies hidden in the olecranon fossa, so seeing it, together with an elevated anterior sail sign, indicates intra-articular effusion. In a child after a fall on an outstretched hand, the most likely cause is an occult supracondylar fracture. Treatment is immobilization and follow-up films. A contusion lies outside the joint capsule and does not elevate the fat pads.
  10. The correct answer is B (Meralgia paresthetica from lateral femoral cutaneous nerve entrapment near the inguinal ligament): The lateral femoral cutaneous nerve is purely sensory and passes under the inguinal ligament, where a tight belt, obesity or pregnancy can compress it. The result is burning in the anterolateral thigh with no weakness or reflex loss. An L3 root or femoral nerve lesion would weaken the quadriceps and alter the patellar reflex. Trochanteric bursitis gives local tenderness, not a burning sensory patch.
  11. The correct answer is C (IgG that binds the TSH receptor and stimulates thyroid hormone synthesis and release): Graves disease is caused by thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulins that mimic TSH at its receptor, producing diffuse hyperplasia and excess hormone. The same antibodies are thought to act on orbital fibroblasts, which causes exophthalmos. Anti-peroxidase antibodies characterize Hashimoto thyroiditis, blocking antibodies cause hypothyroidism, and anti-thyroglobulin immune complexes do not cause painful granulomatous thyroiditis.
  12. The correct answer is A (Sitting forward flexed while lifting a weight held away from the body): Intradiscal pressure is lowest lying supine and rises in standing, sitting and forward flexion. The highest values occur when the spine is flexed with a load held away from the body, because the longer lever arm multiplies the compressive force on the disc. Upright sitting and mild unloaded flexion raise pressure above standing but remain well below a flexed lift.
  13. The correct answer is B (The gallbladder contracts and the sphincter of Oddi relaxes, delivering bile and enzymes onward): Fat in the duodenum stimulates I cells to release cholecystokinin. Cholecystokinin contracts the gallbladder and relaxes the sphincter of Oddi, so bile and pancreatic enzymes reach the duodenal lumen together. A relaxed gallbladder with a tight sphincter describes the interdigestive state when bile is stored. Simultaneous contraction of both structures would trap bile and cause back pressure rather than emptying.
  14. The correct answer is D (Trapezius, latissimus dorsi and the medial border of the scapula): The triangle of auscultation is bounded by the upper border of the latissimus dorsi, the lateral border of the trapezius and the medial border of the scapula. Here the rhomboid major forms the floor and the overlying muscle is thin, so lung sounds are clearly heard. Serratus anterior, erector spinae and the axillary fold are not boundaries.
  15. The correct answer is C (Emergency referral to the hospital today for urgent MRI and surgical evaluation): Bilateral weakness, saddle numbness, urinary retention, and low rectal tone indicate cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency where hours matter for bladder, bowel, and motor recovery. He needs emergency department care now. Urgent referral within days is too slow. Co-management and manipulation delay decompression and may worsen compression.
  16. The correct answer is C (Abdominal aortic aneurysm, imaged with ultrasound, or with CT angiography when rupture is suspected): A pulsatile midline abdominal mass with a bruit and non-mechanical deep back pain in an older smoker indicates an abdominal aortic aneurysm. Ultrasound is the usual screening and confirming study, and CT angiography is used when rupture or surgical planning is the concern. A disc lesion would be mechanical and a pancreatic tumor would not pulsate. A renal stone would cause colicky flank pain, and the listed studies would miss an aneurysm.
  17. The correct answer is C (Joint dysfunction of Gillet and Lewit, where restricted movement, not bone position, defines the abnormality): Gillet and Lewit described joint dysfunction as loss of joint play and motion, which can be palpated and treated without any claim that a bone is out of place. The Palmer concept centers on a positional displacement that irritates a nerve. The immune and vascular models rest on different proposed physiology and are not defined by restricted joint play.
Case N-B13

Patient: F, 42 y, 65 in, 150 lb

Vitals: Pulse 72, resp 16, BP 118/76 mmHg, temp 98.2 F

Chief complaint and history: Bookkeeper who types about 7 hours daily. Three months of right lateral elbow ache, sharp when lifting a full coffee cup or turning a door handle. Pain 1/10 at rest, 7/10 with gripping. Right hand dominant. No trauma, no neck pain, no hand numbness. Ibuprofen 400 mg twice gave little change.

Examination: Right elbow shows full passive flexion and extension, no swelling or warmth. Focal tenderness 1 cm distal to the lateral epicondyle. Medial epicondyle and olecranon nontender. Grip strength right 48 lb, left 62 lb, painful on the right. Cervical range full and painless.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Cozen test: lateral elbow pain. Mill test: lateral pain with passive pronation and wrist flexion, elbow extended. Varus and valgus stress: stable, painless. Cubital tunnel Tinel negative. Spurling negative. Biceps, triceps and brachioradialis reflexes 2+ and symmetric. Resisted supination causes mild lateral ache.

Laboratory and imaging: No imaging obtained. Fasting glucose 92 mg/dL. No history of diabetes, steroid use or inflammatory arthritis.

  1. The correct answers are C and E: Tenderness just distal to the lateral epicondyle sits directly over the common extensor origin, and painful grip with full passive motion points to a loaded tendon rather than the joint. Normal reflexes and a full neck argue against radiculopathy but do not name the elbow structure. Typing hours are a risk factor, not a localizing finding. Only the palpation site and the load-provoked weakness place the lesion at the extensor origin.
  2. The correct answers are A and B: Cozen and Mill tests both load or stretch the common extensor tendon, so reproduction of lateral epicondyle pain supports tendinopathy at that origin. In this chart varus and valgus stress are stable and painless, which argues against ligament injury. The cubital tunnel Tinel and Spurling are negative, so ulnar entrapment and radiculopathy are not supported. Only the two tendon-loading tests are positive here.
  3. The correct answers are C and D: Load modification plus progressive eccentric or heavy slow resistance loading is the best supported conservative approach for chronic common extensor tendinopathy. Soft tissue work and a counterforce strap reduce symptoms while loading is rebuilt. Repeated corticosteroid injection gives short-term relief but poorer outcomes at 6 to 12 months. Casting and early surgery are not indicated in a stable patient who has not tried structured conservative care.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ask him to wrinkle his forehead; a smooth weak side means a peripheral lesion): The upper face receives bilateral cortical input, so a central lesion spares forehead wrinkling while a peripheral lesion of the facial nerve paralyzes the whole side including the forehead. Smiling and cheek puffing are weak in both patterns and do not discriminate. Tongue deviation tests the hypoglossal nerve, which is a separate question and does not clarify the facial nerve level.
  5. The correct answer is D (Place the thicker lower thoracic region toward the cathode end of the tube): Photons made at the anode are absorbed in the target on the anode side, so beam intensity is lower at the anode end and higher at the cathode end. The thicker, denser lower thoracic region needs more photons, so it goes toward the cathode and the thinner upper region toward the anode. Reversing the arrangement worsens the density difference, and tube orientation clearly matters.
  6. The correct answer is B (Ziehl-Neelsen acid-fast stain, which retains carbol fuchsin despite decolorization with acid alcohol because of mycolic acids): Mycobacterium tuberculosis has mycolic acids in its cell wall, so it holds carbol fuchsin even after acid alcohol is applied, and it appears red on a blue background. The Gram stain is poor for mycobacteria because the waxy wall resists the usual dyes, so the organism looks faint or beaded. India ink outlines capsules such as that of Cryptococcus, and the potassium hydroxide mount reveals fungi; neither detects a mycolic acid wall.
  7. The correct answer is B (Defer radiography, treat conservatively, and image only if red flags develop, with tight collimation): In a possible or confirmed first trimester pregnancy the fetus is most sensitive, and uncomplicated back pain does not justify elective imaging. The sensible plan is to defer films, treat conservatively, and image only if red flags develop, then collimate tightly and limit views. Shielding does not eliminate scatter or internal dose, a single view still adds exposure without benefit here, and CT delivers a much higher dose than plain film.
  8. The correct answer is B (The atlantoaxial joint, where C1 pivots around the odontoid of C2): The atlantoaxial joint, where the atlas pivots about the odontoid of the axis, provides about 40 degrees of the roughly 80 degrees of rotation to each side. The atlanto-occipital joint is mostly for flexion and extension. The lower segments add small increments of rotation, each far less than C1-C2.
  9. The correct answer is D (Gallstone obstruction at the ampulla of Vater, with serum lipase first): Gallstones are the leading cause of acute pancreatitis, and pain boring through to the back with vomiting is the classic picture. Serum lipase rises early and is more specific than amylase, so it is the first test. Alcohol is the next most common cause but this patient does not drink. A lipid panel only becomes relevant when triglycerides exceed about 1000 mg/dL, and an ulcer would not explain the gallstone history.
  10. The correct answer is A (Probable reflux or dyspepsia: give dietary advice and co-manage with her medical physician): Meal-related burning relieved by antacids with no alarm features fits dyspepsia or reflux, which is managed with lifestyle advice and co-management with a physician. Alarm features such as weight loss, dysphagia, vomiting or melena would change that to urgent referral. Emergency referral for a heart attack lacks cardiac features, and spinal adjustment will not resolve a visceral problem.
  11. The correct answer is D (Osteoid osteoma, with a small round lucent nidus surrounded by dense cortical sclerosis): Osteoid osteoma is a benign tumor under 1.5 centimeters with a small lucent nidus and a large zone of reactive cortical sclerosis, and the pain is classically worse at night in a teenage boy. Brodie abscess is also lucent with sclerosis but has a tract toward the physis. Osteosarcoma shows aggressive periosteal reaction. A stress fracture shows a linear lucency, not a round nidus.
  12. The correct answer is A (Dysfunction or sprain of the sacroiliac joint on the tested side): FABER flexes, abducts and externally rotates the hip, stressing both the hip joint and the sacroiliac joint. Pain felt in the groin points to the hip, while pain felt posteriorly over the sacroiliac joint points to sacroiliac dysfunction or sprain. The location of the pain decides the interpretation here. Iliopsoas and piriformis problems are not the usual source of a posterior joint line response.
  13. The correct answer is D (Coracobrachialis, biceps brachii and brachialis in the anterior arm compartment): The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the anterior arm compartment: coracobrachialis, biceps brachii and brachialis. Its terminal lateral antebrachial cutaneous branch explains the numbness of the lateral forearm. Normal shoulder abduction rules out the axillary nerve, and normal finger movement rules out the median and radial territories.
  14. The correct answer is D (Compressions at 100 to 120 per minute, pressing down at least 2 inches): Current AHA adult CPR calls for 100 to 120 compressions per minute at a depth of at least 2 inches (5 cm). The chest must also recoil fully between compressions, and interruptions are kept minimal. Slower rates reduce coronary perfusion, faster rates shorten recoil and filling time, and shallow compressions do not generate adequate blood flow.
  15. The correct answer is B (Within normal limits for a child, since up to 5 mm is accepted in pediatric patients): The atlantodental interval is the gap between the anterior arch of the atlas and the dens. It is normally up to 3 mm in adults and up to 5 mm in children because ligaments are more lax and ossification is incomplete. A value of 4 mm in a 6-year-old with a normal neurological examination is therefore within limits and does not suggest transverse ligament failure.
  16. The correct answer is D (Large central or paracentral lumbar disc herniation, as the sign has high specificity): Raising the well leg tugs the dural sleeve and roots on the symptomatic side, so reproduced radiating pain indicates a herniation that displaces the root medially. The sign is less sensitive but more specific than the ipsilateral test. Hamstring tightness would produce posterior thigh stretch pain on the same side, and sacroiliac or piriformis problems rarely create true radicular pain.
  17. The correct answer is B (Lactate starts accumulating at a higher percentage of VO2 max, from greater mitochondrial density and capillaries): Endurance training increases mitochondrial number, oxidative enzymes, and capillary density, so muscle uses pyruvate aerobically at higher workloads. Lactate therefore begins to accumulate at a higher fraction of VO2 max, which moves the threshold to the right. A lower threshold would signal detraining. Fiber conversion toward fast glycolytic types is not an endurance adaptation.
  18. The correct answer is C (Rheumatoid arthritis, with anti-CCP antibodies being more specific than rheumatoid factor for confirming the diagnosis): Symmetric small-joint synovitis with prolonged morning stiffness, high RF, strongly positive anti-CCP and raised inflammatory markers indicate rheumatoid arthritis. Anti-CCP is about 95 percent specific, while RF is also positive in chronic infection, hepatitis C and older healthy adults. ANA is negative here, which makes lupus unlikely, and a uric acid of 4.8 mg/dL does not support gout.
  19. The correct answer is B (Cystic fibrosis, a defective CFTR chloride channel producing thick secretions that obstruct pancreatic ducts and bronchi): Salty sweat, steatorrhea, sinusitis and bronchiectasis with hemoptysis point to cystic fibrosis. A defective CFTR channel dehydrates secretions, which block the pancreatic ducts and airways, and sweat chloride is high. Ciliary dyskinesia spares the pancreas, alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency gives emphysema, and celiac disease does not cause salty sweat.
  20. The correct answer is B (Radiographs are required before manipulation, because age 65 or older is a high-risk factor): On the Canadian C-spine rule, age 65 or older is itself a high-risk factor that mandates radiography, and the ability to rotate 45 degrees is considered only if no high-risk factor is present. A negative NEXUS result does not override this in an older patient with trauma. Imaging must precede any manipulation rather than follow a trial of treatment.
  21. The correct answer is B (Fluid is squeezed from the nucleus pulposus under sustained compression and then reabsorbed during recumbent rest): The intervertebral discs lose water under sustained axial load, an effect called creep, so a person is about 1 to 2 cm shorter by evening. Imbibition of fluid into the nucleus when the spine is unloaded in recumbency restores the height. Vertebral compression fractures cause permanent loss of height. Muscle fatigue and facet cartilage thickness change little over a single day.
  22. The correct answer is C (Descending thoracic aorta, thoracic duct and the azygos venous system): The posterior mediastinum lies behind the pericardium and in front of the lower thoracic vertebrae. It contains the descending thoracic aorta, esophagus, thoracic duct, azygos and hemiazygos veins and the sympathetic trunks. The thymic remnant belongs to the anterior compartment, and the ascending aorta and pulmonary trunk lie in the middle compartment. The arch, brachiocephalic veins and trachea sit in the superior compartment.
  23. The correct answer is D (Developmental dysplasia of the hip, assessed with Hilgenreiner, Perkin and Shenton lines and acetabular index): Breech birth, female sex and limited abduction are classic risk features for developmental dysplasia of the hip. The film shows a shallow acetabulum with an increased acetabular index, a small capital femoral epiphysis lateral to the Perkin line, and a broken Shenton line. Perthes disease and slipped epiphysis occur in older children, and coxa vara has a different measurement set.
  24. The correct answer is D (Antalgic gait, with a shortened stance phase on the painful limb): An antalgic gait shortens the stance phase on the painful limb so the patient bears weight for as little time as possible. Trendelenburg gait reflects hip abductor weakness and shows pelvic drop rather than a rushed swing. Steppage gait comes from weak dorsiflexors and a dropped foot, which this sprain does not cause. Parkinsonian gait is a bilateral shuffling pattern with rigidity and would not appear suddenly after an ankle injury.
  25. The correct answer is B (Hold the load close, bend hips and knees, keep the spine neutral, and pivot the feet): Keeping the load close shortens the lever arm on the lumbar spine, bending at the hips and knees lets the legs do the work, and a neutral spine spreads the load evenly. Pivoting with the feet avoids trunk rotation under load. The other plans lengthen the lever arm, flex the spine, or twist while carrying.
  26. The correct answer is B (Phenylalanine hydroxylase deficiency, so phenylalanine accumulates and tyrosine becomes a conditionally essential amino acid): Phenylalanine hydroxylase converts phenylalanine to tyrosine, so its absence raises phenylalanine and phenylketones, which give the musty odor. Low tyrosine reduces melanin, explaining the fair skin and light hair, and tyrosine becomes conditionally essential. Neurotoxicity of phenylalanine causes the intellectual disability when untreated. Alkaptonuria, maple syrup urine disease and homocystinuria each have different odor, urine or physical findings.
  27. The correct answer is D (Urgent urologic referral, since painless gross hematuria may signal bladder or kidney cancer): Painless gross hematuria in an older smoker with weight loss is bladder or kidney cancer until proven otherwise and needs urgent urologic evaluation. The back ache may be referred or metastatic and is not a reason to adjust. Infection usually causes burning and frequency, and delaying with fluids or routine scheduling risks a missed malignancy.
  28. The correct answer is B (The sympathetic trunk, lying posterior to the sheath on the prevertebral fascia): The carotid sheath holds the common or internal carotid artery, the internal jugular vein and the vagus nerve. The sympathetic trunk lies just behind the sheath against the prevertebral fascia, so it is a close neighbor but not a content. The three wrong choices are all true members, which is why the question is a trap for candidates who picture the neck structures as one bundle.
  29. The correct answer is D (Histoplasma capsulatum, a dimorphic fungus that grows in soil contaminated with bat and bird droppings): Histoplasma capsulatum lives in soil enriched with bat and bird droppings in the Ohio and Mississippi river valleys, and small yeast within macrophages is its tissue form. Healed lesions calcify, as seen on this film. Blastomyces shows broad based budding and skin or bone involvement, Coccidioides forms spherules in the desert Southwest, and Cryptococcus is a capsulated yeast that lies outside cells and typically causes meningitis.
  30. The correct answer is D (Ipsilateral loss of vibration and proprioception below the level, with pain and temperature preserved): The dorsal column medial lemniscus carries vibration and proprioception ipsilaterally in the cord and decussates only in the medulla, so a lesion in the cord affects those modalities on the same side below the lesion. Pain and temperature travel in the spinothalamic tract, which crosses within a segment or two and remains intact on that side. The contralateral options would apply only to a lesion above the medullary decussation.
  31. The correct answer is B (Supraspinous ligament, then interspinous ligament, then ligamentum flavum, entering the epidural space): From the skin a midline needle passes subcutaneous tissue, the supraspinous ligament along the spinous tips, the interspinous ligament between them, and finally the ligamentum flavum, whose resistance gives way to the epidural space. The posterior longitudinal ligament lies on the anterior wall of the canal and is never crossed. The ligamentum nuchae is the cervical continuation of the supraspinous ligament.
  32. The correct answer is D (Obtain radiographs of the painful region first and defer manipulation until results return): Age over 50, night pain unrelieved by rest, and unexplained weight loss are warning signs for malignancy or fracture, so imaging comes before manual therapy. Radiographs are the first test and show lytic or blastic lesions and compression fractures, and further imaging may follow. A trial of care would risk harm, and a bone density scan cannot show a tumor. Soft tissue care without imaging misses the warning signs.
  33. The correct answer is B (Tension pneumothorax, with immediate needle decompression in the second intercostal space midclavicular line): Unilateral hyperresonance, absent breath sounds, tracheal shift away from the affected side and hypotension define tension pneumothorax, a clinical diagnosis. Immediate needle decompression in the second intercostal space midclavicular line, followed by a chest tube, saves the patient. Tamponade lacks unilateral signs and hemothorax is dull to percussion. Waiting for a radiograph in an unstable patient risks cardiac arrest.
  34. The correct answer is B (The oblique view, where the band is a defect through the pars interarticularis, called spondylolysis): The oblique lumbar projection places the pars interarticularis in profile as the neck of the Scotty dog, so a lucent cleft or collar around the neck is a pars defect, or spondylolysis. It is common at L5 in adolescent athletes who repeatedly extend the spine. The lateral view may show slippage but not the neck, and the AP view does not display the pars.
  35. The correct answer is C (A disc herniation compressing a nerve root, managed with conservative care and monitoring for neurologic change): A positive Valsalva raises intrathecal pressure and reproduces pain from a space occupying lesion such as a disc, and a positive straight leg raise at 30 degrees points to nerve root tension. With normal strength, reflexes, and sphincter function, conservative care with monitoring is appropriate. Cauda equina needs bowel, bladder, or saddle findings, and facet or sacroiliac pain does not tension the root.
  36. The correct answer is A (LDL delivers cholesterol to peripheral tissue and drives atherosclerosis, while HDL returns cholesterol to the liver): LDL is formed from VLDL remnants and carries cholesterol to peripheral cells; oxidized LDL is taken up by macrophages, forming foam cells and atherosclerotic plaque. HDL performs reverse cholesterol transport, collecting cholesterol from tissue and returning it to the liver. Chylomicrons carry dietary triglyceride and VLDL carries hepatic triglyceride. HDL is protective, not atherogenic.
  37. The correct answer is D (Olfactory nerve fibers passing through the cribriform plate of the ethmoid bone): The cribriform plate of the ethmoid forms part of the anterior cranial fossa floor and transmits olfactory fibers, so a fracture causes anosmia and cerebrospinal fluid rhinorrhea. The optic canal and the foramen rotundum are in the sphenoid and middle fossa. The internal acoustic meatus is in the posterior fossa and carries the facial and vestibulocochlear nerves.
  38. The correct answer is C (It depolarizes the membrane, which inactivates sodium channels and makes cells less excitable): Raising extracellular potassium reduces the potassium concentration gradient, so the resting potential becomes less negative. Sustained depolarization leaves voltage-gated sodium channel inactivation gates closed, so action potentials fail to fire and weakness results. The membrane is not hyperpolarized. The sodium equilibrium potential depends on the sodium gradient, and intracellular potassium does not fall appreciably in the short term.
  39. The correct answer is C (Metastatic carcinoma, because the avascular disc resists tumor cells lacking digestive enzymes): A smoker with weight loss, night pain and a missing pedicle on the AP film points to metastasis, most often from lung carcinoma, which produces the winking owl sign. Tumor cells spread through the vertebral venous plexus but lack the proteolytic enzymes that destroy disc tissue, so the avascular disc is spared. Pyogenic infection destroys the disc early, myeloma tends to spare the pedicles early, and tuberculosis is less likely given the age, smoking history and single pedicle destruction.
  40. The correct answer is B (Minimal change disease with diffuse effacement of podocyte foot processes seen only on electron microscopy): Minimal change disease is the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in young children, and light microscopy looks normal. Electron microscopy shows diffuse effacement of podocyte foot processes, and the disease responds to corticosteroids. Membranous nephropathy is more typical in adults and shows spikes on silver stain. Poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis is nephritic with hematuria, and focal segmental glomerulosclerosis would show scarring on light microscopy.
  41. The correct answer is C (The muscle spindle, whose sensitivity is set by gamma motor drive): The gamma loop adjusts muscle spindle sensitivity, and increased gamma drive can maintain reflex guarding. The Golgi tendon organ reports tension and tends to inhibit. Pacinian corpuscles and Ruffini endings are joint and fascial mechanoreceptors, and neither receives fusimotor control.
  42. The correct answer is A (Reduced vertebral artery flow where the vessel loops above the posterior arch of the atlas): The vertebral artery turns behind the lateral mass of the atlas and is stretched at end-range extension and rotation. Sustained positioning can reduce flow to the brainstem and cerebellum, producing dizziness, nausea and nystagmus. A nerve root lesion would produce arm symptoms, not nystagmus. Benign positional vertigo is triggered by head position changes relative to gravity, not by neck rotation held at end range.
  43. The correct answer is B (Secondary prevention, because it finds latent infection early in symptom-free people and treats it before disease): Screening asymptomatic people to detect disease or infection early, then treating to prevent progression, is secondary prevention. Primary prevention would avert the infection itself, for example by vaccination or exposure control. Tertiary prevention reduces the impact of established disease through treatment and rehabilitation. Primordial prevention targets social and environmental conditions before risk factors arise, which this program does not do.
  44. The correct answer is D (Primary adrenal insufficiency caused by autoimmune destruction of the adrenal cortex): Hyperpigmentation arises because low cortisol drives high ACTH, which shares its precursor with melanocyte stimulating hormone. Loss of aldosterone causes hyponatremia, hyperkalemia and hypotension. Secondary insufficiency has low ACTH and no pigmentation or hyperkalemia. Cushing syndrome and hyperaldosteronism produce hypertension, and hyperaldosteronism lowers potassium.
  45. The correct answer is C (Refer him to his physician for confirmatory testing and diabetes management, with lifestyle counseling): Polyuria, nocturia, fatigue and blurry vision with an HbA1c of 8.5 percent strongly suggest type 2 diabetes, which needs confirmatory testing and medical management shared with the physician. A stable patient without ketoacidosis symptoms does not need the emergency department. Delaying a year or treating the urinary symptoms as prostate disease lets a metabolic disease progress.
  46. The correct answer is A (Arterial insufficiency in the right leg, with pallor from poor perfusion and reactive hyperemia on dependency): In Buerger test, pallor on elevation shows that arterial inflow cannot overcome gravity, and rubor on lowering reflects reactive hyperemia of dilated ischemic vessels. Venous stasis causes dusky congestion and edema, not pallor on elevation. Spinal stenosis does not change skin color, and deep vein thrombosis produces swelling and warmth rather than a pale foot.
  47. The correct answer is D (Bell palsy, a peripheral facial nerve lesion affecting the upper and lower face on one side): A lower motor neuron facial lesion paralyzes the forehead and lower face together, and ear pain, taste change, and eyelid weakness fit facial nerve involvement. Without vesicles or other neurological signs, Bell palsy is most likely. A central stroke spares the forehead and usually causes limb signs. Ramsay Hunt requires the vesicular eruption, and trigeminal neuralgia does not paralyze facial muscles.
  48. The correct answer is B (Re-examine, reconsider the diagnosis, and consider imaging or referral instead of repeating the plan): Six weeks with no change in pain or range means the plan or the working diagnosis is wrong. The right response is a structured re-examination, reconsideration of the diagnosis, and imaging or referral if indicated. Repeating the plan wastes time. More frequent and forceful treatment adds risk without a rationale. Discharging without re-evaluation misses a possible missed diagnosis.
  49. The correct answer is B (Supinated, with the palms facing forward and the radius on the lateral side): In anatomical position the forearm is supinated, so the palm faces forward and the radius lies lateral to the ulna. All directional terms such as lateral, medial, radial and ulnar are defined from this posture, not from how the limb happens to rest. The relaxed neutral hang with palms toward the thighs is a natural stance but is not the reference. A pronated forearm crosses the radius over the ulna and would reverse the meaning of lateral and medial.
  50. The correct answer is C (The adrenal cortex, since failed cortisol output removes negative feedback and the pituitary keeps raising ACTH): Low cortisol with high ACTH means the pituitary is responding appropriately and the adrenal cortex cannot respond, which is primary adrenal insufficiency. ACTH also stimulates melanocytes through shared POMC products, explaining hyperpigmentation. Secondary and tertiary disease would show low ACTH and no pigmentation. The adrenal medulla secretes catecholamines and does not produce cortisol.
  51. The correct answer is A (Vitamin C, the cofactor for hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues in procollagen chains): Scurvy arises from vitamin C deficiency. Ascorbic acid keeps prolyl and lysyl hydroxylase iron in the reduced state, so proline and lysine hydroxylation can proceed and collagen can form stable triple helices. Without it, collagen is weak, producing gum bleeding, perifollicular hemorrhage and poor healing. Vitamin K affects clotting factors, and vitamins A and B6 do not drive collagen hydroxylation.
  52. The correct answer is C (Internal carotid artery dissection interrupting sympathetic fibers along the artery wall): Ptosis, miosis and anhidrosis form Horner syndrome, and with fresh ipsilateral neck pain after a manipulation the likely cause is internal carotid dissection damaging the sympathetic plexus on the artery. Pancoast tumor causes Horner syndrome but not this acute onset after trauma. Cluster headache is transient, and a third nerve palsy dilates the pupil rather than constricting it.
  53. The correct answer is A (C7 root, from a C6 to C7 posterolateral disc herniation): Triceps weakness, a reduced triceps reflex and middle finger paresthesia identify the C7 root, which exits at the C6 to C7 level and is compressed by a posterolateral herniation there. C6 produces thumb numbness and biceps weakness, C8 affects the little finger and finger flexors, and C5 affects the deltoid.
  54. The correct answer is B (Carpal tunnel syndrome; conservative care with wrist splinting and ergonomic changes, referring if weakness appears): Night numbness in the median distribution, relief by shaking the hand and a positive Phalen test point to carpal tunnel syndrome. Early cases respond to neutral wrist splinting and ergonomic change, and weakness or thenar atrophy would call for referral. A C6 root lesion would usually include neck findings, pronator teres syndrome spares night wrist symptoms, and Guyon canal involves the ring and little fingers.
  55. The correct answer is C (Screen for upper cervical instability with a careful Sharp-Purser test, and image if positive): Long-standing rheumatoid arthritis weakens the transverse ligament and can cause atlantoaxial instability, so stability is checked before any provocative loading. A cautious Sharp-Purser test is positive when a clunk or symptom relief occurs as the head is translated posteriorly, and flexion and extension films are obtained when it is positive or uncertain. Compression, overpressure and distraction all stress the upper cervical spine before safety has been established.
  56. The correct answer is D (Spina bifida occulta, an incidental developmental variant that needs no treatment or further imaging): A midline posterior arch defect with a missing spinous process at L5 is spina bifida occulta, present in a notable share of normal adults and usually found incidentally. With a normal neurological examination and no cutaneous markers, no further workup or treatment is needed. A myelomeningocele is an open defect obvious at birth, spondylolysis involves the pars rather than the midline arch, and a tethered cord would bring skin signs or neurological deficit.
  57. The correct answer is C (Interview her privately, document the findings, and report to adult protective services as state law requires): Bruises in varied stages of healing with a caregiver who answers for the patient are classic signs of elder abuse. The doctor should first try to interview her alone and document the findings, then make the report that most states require of health professionals. Accepting the explanation or waiting for her disclosure leaves her at risk, and confronting the son in public may endanger her further.
  58. The correct answer is D (Multiple myeloma with plasma cell infiltration of bone marrow and monoclonal protein production): Punched-out lytic lesions without sclerosis, anemia, a very high ESR, hypercalcemia, and elevated protein point to multiple myeloma. Neoplastic plasma cells replace marrow and secrete monoclonal immunoglobulin that raises total protein. Prostate metastases are typically blastic, and Paget disease usually has normal calcium. Hyperparathyroidism explains hypercalcemia but not the skull lesions, anemia, or protein elevation.
  59. The correct answer is D (Silicosis, with eggshell calcification of hilar nodes and upper lobe nodules): Silica exposure from sandblasting and quarrying causes silicosis, which shows small upper lobe nodules and eggshell calcification in hilar nodes. Asbestosis involves the lower lobes and pleural plaques after asbestos exposure. Sarcoidosis may also show eggshell nodes, but the long silica exposure decides the choice here. Primary tuberculosis tends to give unilateral adenopathy with a Ghon focus.
  60. The correct answer is B (Permanent enlargement of airspaces distal to the terminal bronchiole with destruction of alveolar walls): Emphysema is permanent enlargement of airspaces distal to the terminal bronchioles with destruction of alveolar walls, producing loss of elastic recoil and reduced diffusing capacity. Smoking drives it through protease and oxidant injury. Chronic bronchitis is defined clinically by cough and mucous gland hypertrophy. Asthma is reversible and eosinophilic, and bronchiectasis is bronchial dilation from recurrent infection.
  61. The correct answer is D (History of vascular risk factors with blood pressure and cranial nerve exam, not position tests alone): Provocative positioning tests have poor sensitivity and do not reliably predict dissection, so they cannot stand alone. A history of risk factors such as hypertension, smoking, migraine and recent trauma, together with blood pressure and a cranial nerve screen, gives the most information. A normal pulse or an absence of dizziness in a trial position does not exclude arterial disease.
  62. The correct answer is D (Stratum basale, a single layer resting on the basement membrane): From deep to superficial the epidermis has the basale, spinosum, granulosum, lucidum and corneum layers. The stratum basale is a single layer of cuboidal stem cells attached to the basement membrane, and it is where cell division occurs. The other three listed layers lie progressively closer to the surface.
  63. The correct answer is C (Stage 1 hypertension, because systolic is 130 to 139 or diastolic is 80 to 89 mmHg): The 2017 ACC/AHA guideline defines stage 1 hypertension as an average systolic of 130 to 139 mmHg or diastolic of 80 to 89 mmHg, and 135/85 satisfies both ranges. The elevated category requires systolic 120 to 129 with diastolic below 80, which does not fit because both of his values are higher. Stage 2 starts at 140/90, which he has not reached. The older 140/90 threshold from earlier guidelines no longer defines normal, so calling this reading normal reflects outdated criteria.
  64. The correct answer is B (Weak abdominals and gluteus maximus opposing tight iliopsoas and lumbar erector spinae): Lower crossed syndrome pairs tight hip flexors and lumbar extensors in front and behind with weak abdominals and gluteals, which tips the pelvis forward and deepens the lordosis. The deep neck flexor pattern describes upper crossed syndrome and affects the head and shoulders. Reversing the tight and weak groups would flatten the lordosis and tilt the pelvis backward. Treatment strengthens the weak groups and stretches the tight ones.
  65. The correct answer is B (Inflammatory pattern, suggesting rheumatoid arthritis, which warrants rheumatoid factor and anti-CCP testing): Morning stiffness beyond one hour that eases with activity, together with symmetric swelling of the metacarpophalangeal joints, is an inflammatory pattern typical of rheumatoid arthritis. Rheumatoid factor and anti-CCP antibodies are the appropriate first serologic tests. Osteoarthritis and mechanical overuse give brief stiffness that worsens with use, and gout is typically asymmetric and episodic rather than symmetric in the hands.
  66. The correct answer is D (A pisiform contact on the atlas transverse process or posterior arch, quick shallow thrust, immediate recoil): Toggle recoil is a high speed, low amplitude technique that uses a hypothenar or pisiform contact on the atlas transverse process or posterior arch. The hands release immediately after the thrust so the segment can move freely, which is the recoil. Long lever rotation and sustained deep pressure change the technique and add risk in the upper cervical region.
  67. The correct answer is C (31 pairs: 8 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 sacral and 1 coccygeal): There are 31 pairs of spinal nerves, with 8 cervical pairs because C1 through C7 exit above their vertebrae and C8 exits below C7. The thoracic, lumbar and sacral regions have 12, 5 and 5 pairs, and there is a single coccygeal pair. The vertebral count of 33 does not equal the nerve count. A cervical count of 7 reflects vertebrae, not nerve pairs.
Case N-B17

Patient: M, 16 y, 68 in, 140 lb

Vitals: Pulse 62, resp 14, BP 112/70 mmHg, temp 98.0 F

Chief complaint and history: Competitive gymnast training 20 hours weekly with back walkovers and dismounts. Four months of central low back pain, 6/10 with extension and landing, eased by rest and flexion. No leg pain, numbness, fever, weight loss or night pain. Hamstrings feel tight. No bowel or bladder change. Grew 8 cm in the past year.

Examination: Stiff-legged gait with a short stride. Increased lumbar lordosis. Palpable step defect at the L5 level. Lumbar flexion nearly full, extension painful and limited. Hamstring tightness: straight leg raise 50 degrees bilaterally without radicular pain. Sacroiliac distraction negative.

Orthopedic and neurological tests: Single leg hyperextension test: right low back pain, left mildly painful. Lower limb strength 5/5, patellar and Achilles reflexes 2+, sensation intact. Femoral stretch negative. Heel and toe walking normal. No Waddell signs.

Laboratory and imaging: No prior imaging. ESR 6 mm/h, complete blood count normal. No family history of ankylosing spondylitis. Growth plates not yet closed per history.

  1. The correct answers are C and D: Repetitive extension loading in a growing athlete, with pain on extension relieved by flexion, is the classic pars stress injury pattern. A palpable step with hyperlordosis and hamstring tightness suggests a slip. The absence of red flags and normal neurology only reduce concern for infection, tumor or cord compromise. A limited straight leg raise without leg pain reflects tight hamstrings, not a nerve root.
  2. The correct answers are A and C: Pain on single leg hyperextension loads the pars and supports a defect, and ipsilateral pain is typical. The oblique view profiles the pars as the Scotty dog, and a collar at the neck marks the defect, while the lateral view shows the slip and allows grading. An anteroposterior view alone does not profile the pars. The femoral stretch and straight leg raise are negative in this chart.
  3. The correct answers are A and D: A 20 percent slip is Meyerding grade I, which is managed conservatively with activity restriction, stabilization and hamstring flexibility, with follow-up films because slips can progress during growth. Surgery is considered for high grade slips, failed conservative care or neurological deficit. Early full return to extension sport risks progression, and a thrust at the slipped level is inappropriate.
  4. The correct answer is D (Explain kindly that imaging must come first, decline to adjust, and arrange referral): A patient's request does not override a contraindication. The best response is respectful and clear, explains why the findings need imaging, declines to adjust and arranges the referral. Giving even a gentle adjustment risks a pathologic fracture, and ending the visit abruptly fails the communication domain and the patient.
  5. The correct answer is B (Leydig cells make testosterone under LH, while Sertoli cells support spermatogenesis under FSH): Leydig cells in the interstitium respond to LH and synthesize testosterone, which in turn supports spermatogenesis locally. Sertoli cells in the seminiferous tubules respond to FSH, nurture developing germ cells, and secrete inhibin and androgen binding protein. Every other pairing swaps a cell type, a hormone, or both.
  6. The correct answer is D (Rupture of a saccular berry aneurysm at an arterial branch point of the circle of Willis): Sudden thunderclap headache, xanthochromia and blood in the basal subarachnoid cisterns are typical of ruptured saccular aneurysm, often at the anterior communicating or middle cerebral branch points. Bridging vein tears cause subdural blood, lenticulostriate rupture causes intraparenchymal hemorrhage, and middle meningeal injury causes epidural hematoma.
  7. The correct answer is B (About 45 degrees of rotation, which shows the pars interarticularis in profile as the dog's neck): A 45 degree oblique turns the patient so the central ray runs along the plane of the facet joints, placing the pars interarticularis in profile as the neck of the Scotty dog. At 90 degrees the patient is in a true lateral position and the dog outline is lost. Shallow rotation of 15 or 30 degrees leaves the posterior elements overlapping instead of separated, so a pars defect cannot be judged.
  8. The correct answer is B (Radial, which names the thumb side of the forearm and the hand in this posture): In the forearm and hand, radial replaces lateral and ulnar replaces medial, because the radius lies on the thumb side in anatomical position. Ulnar therefore names the little-finger side. Dorsal and volar, or palmar, describe the back and palm surfaces rather than the sides of the limb. The thumb side is radial.
  9. The correct answer is D (Neuroimmunomodulation, where spinal and autonomic changes modulate immune cell behavior through chemical mediators): The stem names immune cell activity as the target and neuropeptides and autonomic outflow as the route, which defines the neuroimmunomodulation model. The somatovisceral model concerns organ function, not immune cells. Compression and biomechanical models rely on mechanical deformation and load, not chemical signaling. The model remains a hypothesis with limited human support.
  10. The correct answer is A (Duchenne muscular dystrophy, an X-linked dystrophin deficiency with proximal weakness and calf pseudohypertrophy): The Gowers maneuver shows proximal pelvic girdle weakness, and enlarged firm calves with a very high creatine kinase in an early walking delay fit Duchenne dystrophy. Spinal muscular atrophy causes fasciculations and wasting with a normal creatine kinase. Perthes disease causes pain and a limp from the hip, not a Gowers sign. Becker dystrophy is similar but milder and appears later, so age five with early delay favors Duchenne.
  11. The correct answer is C (Immunity from prior hepatitis B vaccination, without any history of natural infection): Vaccine contains only surface antigen, so vaccination produces anti-HBs without anti-HBc. A positive anti-HBs with negative HBsAg and negative anti-HBc indicates vaccine-induced immunity. Natural resolved infection would show positive anti-HBc along with anti-HBs. Chronic infection has positive HBsAg, and the window period has isolated anti-HBc.
  12. The correct answer is D (Explain pain as a sensitized alarm system, not tissue damage, then set graded goals with reassurance): Fear-avoidance is driven by threat beliefs, so pain neuroscience education explains that persistent pain reflects a sensitized nervous system and not ongoing damage. Graded activity then exposes her to feared movements in planned steps, with reassurance, which reduces guarding. Activity restriction and repeat imaging confirm the fear, and passive care alone leaves the beliefs unchanged.
  13. The correct answer is C (Potassium hydroxide preparation of scrapings from the active border, examined microscopically for septate hyphae): The annular, scaly, centrally clearing pattern in a wrestler suggests tinea corporis from a dermatophyte such as Trichophyton. Potassium hydroxide dissolves keratinocytes and leaves fungal elements visible, so hyphae appear when scraped from the active border. A Tzanck smear targets herpesvirus lesions, a Gram stain targets bacteria, and India ink highlights the capsule of Cryptococcus in cerebrospinal fluid.
  14. The correct answer is A (Guillain-Barre syndrome, so send him to an emergency department today for admission and monitoring.): Symmetric ascending weakness, numbness and absent reflexes after a gastrointestinal infection describe Guillain-Barre syndrome, an acute inflammatory polyneuropathy that can progress to respiratory failure. The patient needs same day emergency care for admission and breathing monitoring. A single disc lesion would not abolish reflexes at several levels, and a slow outpatient pathway is unsafe.
  15. The correct answer is D (Chronic gastrointestinal blood loss from the NSAID, so stop the drug and refer for upper endoscopy): Positive occult blood on three samples with microcytic anemia and a ferritin of 8 ng/mL show iron deficiency from chronic gastrointestinal bleeding, and an NSAID user with epigastric pain most likely has ulceration or gastritis. Management is to stop the NSAID and refer for endoscopy to find the source. Inflammatory anemia has normal or high ferritin, B12 deficiency is macrocytic, and iron deficiency in an adult man should never be assumed dietary before the gut is evaluated.
  16. The correct answer is B (Inhibition of cytochrome c oxidase blocks electron transfer to oxygen, halting oxidative phosphorylation): Cyanide binds the ferric iron of complex IV, so electrons cannot reach oxygen and cells cannot use the oxygen delivered. Venous blood stays oxygenated and bright red, and cells shift to anaerobic glycolysis, producing lactate. Carbon monoxide would lower hemoglobin oxygen delivery. Uncouplers cause hyperthermia with increased oxygen consumption, and ATP synthase inhibitors do not leave venous oxygen unused in this pattern.
  17. The correct answer is C (Failure of formation of half a vertebral body, a congenital rather than idiopathic curve): A hemivertebra is a failure of formation, producing a wedge shaped vertebra and a short, sharply angulated congenital scoliosis. Unlike idiopathic scoliosis, the cause is a structural anomaly present from birth, and these curves require surveillance for renal and cardiac anomalies. A unilateral bar is a failure of segmentation, not what a wedge shaped segment shows. Functional curves correct on bending, and idiopathic curves have normal vertebrae.
  18. The correct answer is D (Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, a combined upper and lower motor neuron disease that spares sensation): Fasciculations and wasting mark lower motor neuron loss, while brisk reflexes, spasticity and an extensor plantar response mark upper motor neuron loss. Having both with normal sensation is the signature of ALS. Myelopathy usually causes sensory changes, and spinal muscular atrophy would lower reflexes instead of raising them. Multiple sclerosis typically gives sensory and optic findings over a relapsing course.
  19. The correct answer is A (The right C6 root, which leaves the canal above C6 through the C5 to C6 foramen): Cervical roots exit above the vertebra with the same number, because there are eight cervical roots and only seven vertebrae, so C6 exits through the C5 to C6 foramen. A posterolateral herniation at that level therefore compresses the exiting C6 root. The C7 root exits one level lower, and C8 exits only at C7 to T1.
  20. The correct answer is A (Secretin, released by duodenal S cells in response to acid): Acid in the duodenal lumen stimulates S cells to release secretin, which acts on pancreatic ductal cells to secrete bicarbonate-rich fluid that neutralizes the acid. Cholecystokinin responds to fat and amino acids and drives enzyme secretion and gallbladder contraction. Gastrin raises gastric acid output, and gastric inhibitory peptide augments insulin release after glucose and fat.
  21. The correct answer is C (It exceeds the normal range, indicating a steeper sacral base with greater anterior shear at L5-S1): The Ferguson angle is the angle between the sacral base line and the horizontal, and the normal range is about 26 to 57 degrees. A value of 68 degrees exceeds the range and means a steeper sacral base, which increases anterior shear stress at the lumbosacral junction and loads the facets. Values below 26 degrees would be a flat sacral base. The range is not sex specific.
  22. The correct answer is D (Squamous cell carcinoma, a central tumor with keratin pearls that may secrete parathyroid hormone-related peptide): A central cavitating hilar mass in a heavy smoker with hypercalcemia and suppressed PTH points to squamous cell carcinoma, which can make PTH-related peptide. Small cell carcinoma is central but gives ACTH or ADH syndromes, adenocarcinoma is peripheral, and large cell carcinoma is peripheral and undifferentiated.
  23. The correct answer is B (A systematic review pooling randomized controlled trials of spinal manipulation, using predefined methods): For treatment questions, a systematic review of randomized trials sits at the top of the hierarchy because it pools controlled data and reduces chance and bias. A cohort study cannot control confounding as well, and a case series has no comparison group. A single trial is weaker than a review that combines several.
  24. The correct answer is A (Acute otitis externa, an infection of the skin of the external auditory canal): Pain provoked by movement of the pinna or tragal pressure points to the external canal, and swimming predisposes to canal skin infection. Otitis media produces pain without tenderness to pinna traction, with a bulging, immobile drum. Cholesteatoma is generally painless, and mastoiditis causes postauricular tenderness and auricle displacement. A mobile drum seen through a swollen canal supports an external cause.
  25. The correct answer is A (Reducing pyruvate to lactate regenerates NAD+, which glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate dehydrogenase needs for glycolysis): Glycolysis consumes NAD+ at the glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate dehydrogenase step and nets only 2 ATP per glucose. Without oxygen the electron transport chain cannot reoxidize NADH, so lactate dehydrogenase converts pyruvate to lactate and regenerates NAD+. Pyruvate dehydrogenase is mitochondrial and oxygen dependent, so it cannot rescue the cytosolic NAD+ supply. Lactate itself produces no ATP, and the net yield stays at two.
  26. The correct answer is D (Suspected melanoma, so refer promptly for excisional biopsy rather than monitoring the lesion): Asymmetry, border irregularity, color variation, diameter over 6 mm and evolution make this lesion suspicious for melanoma, which is the deadliest skin cancer and curable when excised early. The next step is urgent referral for excisional biopsy. Observation, photographs or topical treatment would delay diagnosis. Basal cell carcinoma is usually a pearly papule, not a multicolored enlarging mole.
  27.   28.   29. Each sheet covers hazards, handling, storage, first aid, and spill response. The exposure control plan, injury log, and PPE assessment are separate OSHA records with different purposes.
  30.   31. Loss of the right heart border points to the right middle lobe, which touches it.   32.   33. The correct answer is A (Pisiforms on opposite transverse processes with crossed forearms, thrust at end of exhalation): A crossed-bilateral contact places the pisiforms on opposite transverse processes so that the hands produce a rotary couple on the segment. The thrust is applied at the end of exhalation, when the thoracic cage is most relaxed and the joints are closest to their end range of motion. Spinous contacts or rib contacts change the segment being moved, and inhalation timing reduces joint slack.
  34. Pressing clear tape on the skin early in the morning before bathing picks up the eggs for microscopic identification. Repeated stool examinations, eosinophil counts, and duodenal aspirates are tempting parasite tests, but none samples the perianal skin where the eggs are deposited.
  35.   36. The correct answer is A (Muscle spindle stretch afferents monosynaptically excite quadriceps alpha motor neurons at spinal levels L2 through L4): Tapping the patellar tendon briefly stretches the quadriceps, which activates muscle spindle Ia afferents. These fibers synapse directly on alpha motor neurons in the L2 to L4 cord segments, producing a monosynaptic contraction. Golgi tendon organs sense tension and inhibit rather than excite.   37. The correct answer is D (Failing hepatocytes cannot convert ammonia to urea, so ammonia reaches the brain and depletes alpha-ketoglutarate): The urea cycle runs in hepatocytes and converts toxic ammonia into urea. In cirrhosis and portosystemic shunting, ammonia escapes to the brain, where astrocytes use glutamine synthesis and alpha-ketoglutarate to detoxify it, causing swelling and encephalopathy. A low urea nitrogen with high ammonia points to failed urea synthesis, not uremia. Hypoglycemia and renal failure do not fit the picture.
  38. The correct answer is A (L5 root, which supplies extensor hallucis longus and first web space sensation): The L5 root supplies extensor hallucis longus, the main great toe extensor, and sensation over the dorsum of the foot including the web space between the first and second toes. L4 would affect knee reflex and medial leg sensation. S1 would weaken plantar flexion, diminish the Achilles reflex and numb the lateral foot. L3 would involve the quadriceps and anterior thigh.
  39. The correct answer is A (Diabetes is confirmed because two different tests from the same sample both exceed their thresholds): Diabetes is diagnosed by A1c of 6.5 percent or higher, fasting glucose of 126 mg/dL or higher, a 2-hour glucose of 200 mg/dL or higher during an oral glucose tolerance test, or a random glucose of 200 with classic symptoms. Two different abnormal tests from the same sample confirm the diagnosis without repeat testing. A glucose tolerance test is optional, the A1c cutoff is 6.5 not 7.0, and the random glucose criterion is only one of several routes.
  40. The correct answer is A (The medulla continuing as spinal cord, both vertebral arteries and accessory nerve spinal roots): The foramen magnum transmits the medulla as it becomes the spinal cord, the two vertebral arteries and the spinal roots of the accessory nerve ascending from the upper cervical cord. The jugular foramen carries cranial nerves IX, X and XI and the start of the internal jugular vein. The internal carotid artery enters through the carotid canal. The maxillary nerve leaves through foramen rotundum, so it is not at risk here.
  41. The correct answer is C (The conus medullaris ends lower in newborns, near L3, so L3 to L4 risks cord injury): The spinal cord grows more slowly than the vertebral column, so the conus lies near L3 at birth and reaches the adult L1 to L2 position by about age two. A needle at L3 to L4 in a newborn can therefore strike the cord, and L4 to L5 is safer. The dural sac ends at the sacrum, not L1, and the spinous processes are not fused.

Part IV Encounters Answer Key

Encounter E01: Low back pain with leg pain and a weak foot

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset and what he was doing when pain began
  • Asks pain quality and exactly where the leg pain travels
  • Asks about numbness, tingling and weakness in the leg or foot
  • Screens for bowel, bladder and saddle numbness (cauda equina)
  • Screens for fever, weight loss, cancer history and night pain
  • Asks about aggravating and relieving factors including cough and sitting
  • Asks about prior episodes, treatment and medications

Physical exam

  • Observes gait including heel and toe walking
  • Measures lumbar range of motion and notes pain response
  • Performs straight leg raise on both sides
  • Tests lower limb myotomes in both legs
  • Tests lower limb reflexes
  • Tests sensation by dermatome
  • Checks pulses and does a quick abdominal exam
  • Performs Valsalva or slump to support disc involvement

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of left L5 radiculopathy, likely L4 to L5 disc herniation
  • Names a differential such as piriformis syndrome, facet syndrome and spinal stenosis
  • Recognizes the foot drop signs as a motor deficit that needs monitoring
  • Rules out cauda equina syndrome aloud
  • Plans conservative care with strength retesting within 48 to 72 hours and a clear threshold for urgent referral
  • Orders MRI and arranges a surgical opinion because the motor deficit is progressing

Communication

  • Introduces self and confirms patient name
  • Uses plain language to explain a disc problem
  • Explains warning signs that mean go to the emergency room today
  • Checks that the patient understands and invites questions

Professionalism

  • Washes hands and drapes or positions patient respectfully
  • Obtains verbal consent before testing
  • Says aloud what he or she is doing for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Left L5 radiculopathy from a probable L4 to L5 disc herniation with mild motor deficit

Differential:

  • Piriformis syndrome
  • Lumbar facet syndrome with referred pain
  • Lumbar spinal stenosis
  • Peroneal neuropathy at the fibular head

Red flags to catch:

  • Progressive foot weakness over 4 days
  • Possible early cauda equina signs must be asked about and ruled out

Imaging or labs to order: MRI of the lumbar spine promptly because the motor deficit is already progressing, with a spine surgery opinion arranged; repeat strength testing within 48 to 72 hours. Plain films are not needed first in this case.

Referral or emergency: Treat conservatively with careful, non rotational care and shared care with a medical provider for pain control. Send to the emergency room at once if saddle numbness, bladder or bowel change, or rapid worsening of weakness appears. Refer to a spine surgeon if the deficit worsens.

Model SOAP note

S: 38 year old male warehouse worker, 3 weeks of low back pain with left leg pain to dorsal foot and great toe, 7 of 10. Worse with sitting, cough and flexion. Foot slapping for 4 days. Denies bowel, bladder, saddle numbness, fever, weight loss.

O: Antalgic gait, left foot slap. Flexion 40 degrees painful. SLR left positive at 40 degrees, slump and Valsalva positive. Left dorsiflexion 4/5, EHL 4 minus/5. Decreased sensation dorsal left foot. Reflexes 2+ equal. Babinski negative.

A: Left L5 radiculopathy, probable L4 to L5 disc herniation with mild motor deficit. No cauda equina signs.

P: Conservative care with gentle, flexion distraction style care and activity modification. Retest strength in 48 to 72 hours. Order MRI promptly and arrange a spine surgery opinion because the weakness is progressing. Shared care for pain. Go to the ER for saddle numbness, bladder or bowel change, or sudden weakness.

Common errors

  • Skipping the bowel, bladder and saddle numbness screen
  • Testing only the right side or only straight leg raise and missing the motor exam
  • Calling the foot slap normal and not planning a recheck or referral threshold
  • Using rotational manipulation on an acute disc with a motor deficit
  • Ordering plain films only and calling it a full workup
  • Not stating the red flags to the patient in plain words

Encounter E02: Shoulder pain after a fall with trouble lifting the arm

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks how and when the injury happened
  • Asks pain quality, location and 0 to 10 severity
  • Asks about night pain and sleeping position
  • Asks about weakness, numbness and neck involvement
  • Asks about a pop at the time of injury
  • Screens for fever, weight loss, cancer and chest symptoms
  • Asks about handedness, work and daily activities

Physical exam

  • Inspects for atrophy, bruising and deformity
  • Compares active and passive range of motion
  • Performs drop arm test
  • Performs empty can or full can test
  • Performs external rotation lag sign
  • Performs impingement tests such as Neer and Hawkins Kennedy
  • Screens the cervical spine with Spurling or range of motion
  • Tests shoulder myotomes and reflexes

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of a full thickness rotator cuff tear, supraspinatus and infraspinatus
  • Names a differential such as impingement, adhesive capsulitis, biceps tendinopathy and cervical radiculopathy
  • Explains why active motion is much less than passive motion
  • Orders MRI or diagnostic ultrasound of the shoulder
  • Refers to orthopedics for surgical opinion since the weakness persisted 5 weeks
  • Avoids strong stretching or aggressive manual force to the shoulder

Communication

  • Introduces self and confirms name
  • Explains a tendon tear in simple words
  • Addresses her fear about surgery honestly
  • Confirms the patient understands the plan

Professionalism

  • Obtains consent and drapes shoulder appropriately
  • Washes hands before the exam
  • Narrates actions for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Traumatic full thickness tear of the right supraspinatus and infraspinatus tendons

Differential:

  • Subacromial impingement with partial tear
  • Adhesive capsulitis
  • Biceps tendon tear or tendinopathy
  • Cervical radiculopathy at C5 to C6

Red flags to catch:

  • Persistent weakness 5 weeks after trauma
  • Positive drop arm and lag sign suggest full thickness tear that needs early repair decision

Imaging or labs to order: Plain films of the shoulder to rule out fracture, then MRI or ultrasound to confirm and size the tear.

Referral or emergency: Refer to an orthopedic surgeon for a repair decision, since delay lets the tendon retract and the muscle turn to fat. Not an emergency.

Model SOAP note

S: 61 year old right handed female, 5 weeks of right shoulder pain after a fall on ice. Cannot lift arm. Night pain 6 of 10. Heard a pop. No neck pain, numbness, fever or chest symptoms.

O: Supraspinatus and infraspinatus atrophy. Active abduction 70 degrees, passive 150 degrees. Drop arm, empty can and external rotation lag positive. Neer and Hawkins Kennedy positive. Abduction and external rotation 3/5. Spurling negative. Reflexes and sensation normal.

A: Probable full thickness right rotator cuff tear, supraspinatus and infraspinatus. Impingement and adhesive capsulitis considered.

P: Plain films of shoulder to rule out fracture, then MRI or ultrasound. Orthopedic referral for repair opinion. Sling for comfort only short term. Avoid stretching and heavy lifting. Acetaminophen as needed. Recheck in 1 week.

Common errors

  • Labeling it only as impingement and starting strengthening and stretching
  • Missing that passive motion is much better than active motion
  • Skipping the cervical screen so a C5 root problem is not ruled out
  • Applying vigorous mobilization to a torn tendon
  • Not ordering imaging or referring in a traumatic case with weakness

Encounter E03: Neck pain and headache after painting a ceiling

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset, trigger and mechanism including recent neck extension or rotation
  • Asks pain quality and compares with prior headaches
  • Asks about dizziness, vision change, drooping eyelid and double vision
  • Asks about numbness, speech or swallowing problems
  • Asks about risk factors such as birth control pills, smoking and migraine
  • Asks about fever, trauma and prior neck problems
  • Asks about medications and what has helped

Physical exam

  • Takes bilateral blood pressure
  • Inspects eyes for ptosis and pupil size
  • Tests cranial nerves
  • Tests sensation of the face and extremities
  • Tests coordination such as finger to nose and gait
  • Listens to the carotids for a bruit
  • Palpates and tests cervical range of motion gently
  • Avoids vertebral artery provocative tests and neck manipulation

Diagnosis and case management

  • States suspicion of cervical artery dissection (vertebral or carotid) with Horner syndrome and brain stem signs
  • Recognizes this is a medical emergency
  • Does NOT perform or plan neck manipulation
  • Calls for emergency transport and imaging (CT or MR angiography)
  • Names a differential such as cervical strain, migraine and subarachnoid hemorrhage
  • Documents findings and the emergency referral

Communication

  • Introduces self and confirms name
  • Explains the concern calmly without causing panic
  • Tells the patient clearly not to drive herself
  • Confirms she understands and agrees to go

Professionalism

  • Puts patient safety above the patient's request for an adjustment
  • Obtains consent for the exam
  • Says aloud the reason for stopping testing and referring

Diagnosis and management
