Working diagnosis: Suspected left cervical artery dissection with Horner syndrome and early posterior circulation signs

Differential:

  • Acute cervical strain
  • Migraine or cervicogenic headache
  • Subarachnoid hemorrhage
  • Stroke from other cause

Red flags to catch:

  • Worst headache of life at the back of the head with neck pain
  • Horner syndrome (droopy eyelid, small pupil)
  • Transient slurred speech and facial tingling
  • Oral contraceptive use and neck extension as risk and trigger

Imaging or labs to order: Emergency CT angiography or MR angiography of head and neck at a hospital. Not done in office.

Referral or emergency: Emergency department by ambulance now. No manipulation of the neck. A dissection can lead to a stroke within hours or days.

Model SOAP note

S: 44 year old female, neck pain and occipital headache for 3 days after painting a ceiling with neck extended. Worst headache of life, 8 of 10. Dizzy twice, brief slurred speech, left facial tingling, droopy left eyelid. On oral contraceptives. No trauma or fever.

O: BP 142/88 equal arms. Left ptosis and miosis. Decreased pinprick left cheek. Mild left dysmetria, wide gait. Soft left carotid bruit. Neck muscles tender and rotation painful. Vertebral artery tests withheld.

A: Suspected left cervical artery dissection with Horner syndrome and brain stem signs. Cervical strain less likely.

P: Emergency transport by ambulance to the hospital for CT or MR angiography. No neck manipulation. Not to drive. Findings reported to the emergency team. Follow up after medical care.

Common errors

  • Performing vertebral artery provocative tests or a neck adjustment
  • Not noticing the droopy eyelid and small pupil because the eyes were never inspected
  • Calling it a muscle strain and sending her home with exercises
  • Sending her to a regular appointment next week instead of the emergency room
  • Letting the patient drive herself to the hospital
  • Not asking about birth control, smoking or the speech change

Encounter E04: Numb hands at night in a data entry worker

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks which fingers are affected and which spare
  • Asks about timing, especially night symptoms
  • Asks about hand use, work and posture
  • Asks about neck pain, arm pain and the little finger
  • Asks about weakness and dropping objects
  • Screens for diabetes symptoms such as thirst and weight loss
  • Asks about pregnancy, thyroid and family history

Physical exam

  • Inspects the hand for thenar atrophy
  • Performs Phalen test
  • Performs Tinel sign at the wrist
  • Performs carpal compression test
  • Tests grip and thumb abduction strength
  • Tests sensation by nerve territory
  • Screens the neck with Spurling and range of motion
  • Checks feet sensation or glucose when diabetes is suspected

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of right greater than left carpal tunnel syndrome
  • Names a differential including cervical radiculopathy, pronator syndrome and peripheral neuropathy
  • Recognizes possible undiagnosed diabetes as the underlying cause
  • Orders fasting glucose, A1c and thyroid tests
  • Refers for nerve conduction study because of thenar atrophy
  • Plans wrist splint at night, ergonomic changes and shared care with a medical doctor

Communication

  • Introduces self and confirms name
  • Explains what the median nerve does in simple words
  • Explains why blood tests are needed
  • Checks understanding and invites questions

Professionalism

  • Obtains consent before exam and testing
  • Maintains professional boundaries and hand hygiene
  • Narrates actions for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Bilateral carpal tunnel syndrome, right worse, probably related to undiagnosed type 2 diabetes

Differential:

  • Cervical radiculopathy at C6 to C7
  • Pronator teres syndrome
  • Diabetic peripheral neuropathy alone
  • Thoracic outlet syndrome

Red flags to catch:

  • Thenar atrophy and weakness suggest advanced nerve damage
  • Classic diabetes symptoms with glucose 268 mg/dL
  • Stocking pattern foot numbness

Imaging or labs to order: Fasting glucose, A1c, TSH. Nerve conduction study and EMG of the arms.

Referral or emergency: Same week visit with a medical doctor for diabetes. Hand surgery referral if the nerve study shows severe compression or atrophy keeps progressing.

Model SOAP note

S: 52 year old female data entry clerk, 6 months of bilateral hand numbness and burning in thumb, index and middle fingers, right worse, worst at night, dropping objects. Thirst, frequent urination, 8 pound weight loss. Mother has diabetes.

O: Right thenar atrophy. Phalen and Tinel positive on the right. APB 4/5 right. Decreased sensation median distribution right. Spurling negative. Stocking pattern decreased sensation both feet. Office glucose 268 mg/dL.

A: Carpal tunnel syndrome, right worse than left, with suspected new type 2 diabetes. Cervical radiculopathy unlikely.

P: Same week referral to medical doctor for diabetes testing and care. Order A1c and TSH. Nerve conduction study. Night wrist splints, ergonomic changes. Hand surgery referral if severe. Recheck in 2 weeks.

Common errors

  • Treating the wrists only and missing the diabetes signs
  • Not checking the neck or the little finger to separate cervical from median nerve problems
  • Skipping strength testing and not noticing thenar atrophy
  • Forgetting to send to a medical doctor for blood testing
  • Calling a bilateral stocking pattern part of carpal tunnel
  • Doing aggressive wrist manipulation instead of a splint and shared care

Encounter E05: New headache in an older woman with jaw pain

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks onset, quality and location of the headache
  • Asks if this is new compared with past headaches
  • Asks about jaw pain with chewing
  • Asks about vision changes including brief blurry spells
  • Asks about scalp tenderness
  • Asks about morning stiffness in shoulders and hips
  • Screens for fever, night sweats and weight loss
  • Asks about medications and trauma

Physical exam

  • Takes temperature and vital signs
  • Palpates the temporal arteries and compares both sides
  • Checks scalp tenderness
  • Tests visual acuity and fields
  • Tests cranial nerves
  • Examines the cervical spine
  • Checks TMJ and jaw motion
  • Tests shoulder motion and strength

Diagnosis and case management

  • States suspicion of giant cell (temporal) arteritis
  • Recognizes the risk of permanent vision loss
  • Orders ESR and CRP urgently
  • Refers same day to a medical doctor or the emergency room
  • Names a differential such as polymyalgia rheumatica, tension headache and cervicogenic headache
  • Does not start spinal manipulation or plan a wait and see course

Communication

  • Introduces self and confirms name
  • Explains the concern in plain words without fear
  • Explains why she must be seen today
  • Checks that she understands and agrees

Professionalism

  • Puts urgent patient safety first
  • Obtains consent and uses hand hygiene
  • Narrates findings for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Suspected giant cell arteritis (temporal arteritis) with possible polymyalgia rheumatica

Differential:

  • Polymyalgia rheumatica alone
  • Tension type headache
  • Cervicogenic headache
  • Trigeminal neuralgia or TMJ disorder

Red flags to catch:

  • New headache after age 50
  • Jaw claudication
  • Temporal artery tenderness and weak pulse
  • Transient vision loss
  • Weight loss, night sweats and low grade fever

Imaging or labs to order: ESR and CRP the same day. Temporal artery biopsy or ultrasound arranged by the medical team.

Referral or emergency: Same day emergency referral. Delay can cause permanent blindness. Steroids are started by the medical doctor, not by the chiropractor.

Model SOAP note

S: 69 year old female, 3 weeks of new right temporal headache, throbbing 6 of 10, jaw pain with chewing, scalp tenderness, brief right eye blurry vision yesterday, morning shoulder and hip stiffness, night sweats, 6 pound weight loss.

O: Temp 99.4 F. Right temporal artery thickened, tender, decreased pulse. Acuity 20/30 right, 20/25 left. Cranial nerves intact. Neck and TMJ exam benign.

A: Suspected giant cell arteritis with polymyalgia rheumatica. Tension or cervicogenic headache unlikely.

P: Same day referral to emergency room or medical doctor for ESR, CRP and steroid decision. No spinal manipulation. Explained risk of vision loss. Follow up after medical evaluation.

Common errors

  • Treating it as a tension or neck headache and starting care
  • Not palpating the temporal arteries
  • Not asking about jaw pain, vision and morning stiffness
  • Planning a recheck in 1 to 2 weeks instead of same day referral
  • Starting steroids or suggesting herbal remedies instead of referring
  • Failing to check vision at all

Encounter E06: High blood pressure and chest tightness with exertion

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks character, location and radiation of the chest discomfort
  • Asks what triggers and relieves it, including exertion and rest
  • Asks about shortness of breath, sweating, nausea and lightheadedness
  • Asks duration and last episode
  • Asks about cardiac risk factors such as smoking, family history and diet
  • Asks about blood pressure history and medications
  • Asks if neck movement changes the symptoms

Physical exam

  • Takes blood pressure in both arms
  • Takes pulse and respiratory rate
  • Listens to heart and lungs
  • Checks carotid arteries for bruits
  • Inspects for edema and jugular distention
  • Palpates the chest wall to test for musculoskeletal pain
  • Examines the cervical spine and shoulder
  • Checks peripheral pulses

Diagnosis and case management

  • States suspicion of exertional angina (unstable or new onset) with severe hypertension
  • Recognizes this as a probable cardiac problem, not a neck problem
  • Does not perform spinal manipulation
  • Refers urgently to the emergency room or cardiology for ECG and troponin
  • Names a differential such as GERD, cervical radiculopathy and musculoskeletal strain
  • Advises to avoid exertion and not to drive himself

Communication

  • Introduces self and confirms name
  • Explains the concern respectfully to a patient who minimizes
  • Explains why he needs a heart evaluation today
  • Confirms agreement with the plan

Professionalism

  • Puts safety ahead of patient's request for neck care
  • Obtains consent and uses hand hygiene
  • Narrates actions for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: New onset exertional angina, likely unstable, with severe uncontrolled hypertension

Differential:

  • Gastroesophageal reflux
  • Cervical radiculopathy with referred shoulder pain
  • Musculoskeletal chest wall strain
  • Aortic dissection

Red flags to catch:

  • Pressure in the chest with exertion relieved by rest
  • Radiation to jaw and left shoulder
  • Episode lasting 8 minutes with sweating and shortness of breath
  • BP 168/102
  • Strong risk factors: smoker, father died at 54, overweight, carotid bruit

Imaging or labs to order: 12 lead ECG and serial troponin at the hospital; stress testing or angiography only after the cardiology team stabilises him. Lipid panel and glucose later.

Referral or emergency: Same day emergency room by ambulance, since new onset angina can become a heart attack. Not treatable in a chiropractic office.

Model SOAP note

S: 56 year old male truck driver, 2 weeks of neck and left shoulder ache and chest pressure with exertion relieved by rest in 5 minutes, radiating to jaw and left shoulder, sweating and shortness of breath. Last episode yesterday lasted 8 minutes. Smoker 30 pack years, father died of MI at 54, untreated hypertension.

O: BP 168/102 right, 164/100 left. HR 88. S4 gallop, right carotid bruit. Lungs clear. Chest wall nontender. Cervical tests negative. Neuro intact.

A: Probable new onset unstable angina with severe hypertension. Neck and shoulder symptoms likely referred cardiac pain. Cervical strain less likely.

P: Emergency transport to hospital for ECG and troponin. No spinal manipulation and no exertion. Not to drive. Cardiology follow up. Risk factor counseling later.

Common errors

  • Treating the neck and shoulder pain and ignoring the chest symptoms
  • Taking blood pressure in only one arm or skipping it
  • Accepting the patient's claim that it is heartburn
  • Scheduling a follow up visit instead of sending him to the emergency room
  • Performing spinal manipulation on a possibly unstable cardiac patient
  • Not asking about family history, smoking and what relieves the chest tightness

Encounter E07: Pop in the knee during a soccer game

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks the mechanism of injury (twist, contact or no contact)
  • Asks about a pop, timing of swelling, and locking or giving way
  • Asks about numbness, foot color change, or calf pain
  • Asks about past knee injuries and the earlier ankle sprain
  • Asks about medications, allergies, and sports goals
  • Asks screening questions for fever, other joint pain, and rash

Physical exam

  • Checks vitals and watches gait
  • Inspects and palpates for effusion, warmth, and joint line tenderness
  • Measures active and passive range of motion
  • Performs Lachman, anterior drawer, and pivot shift
  • Performs valgus and varus stress and posterior drawer
  • Performs McMurray for meniscus
  • Checks distal pulses and a lower limb neurologic screen
  • Applies Ottawa knee rules

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of anterior cruciate ligament tear
  • Lists meniscus tear, collateral ligament sprain, and tibial plateau fracture as differentials
  • Orders knee radiographs and refers for MRI and orthopedic evaluation
  • Gives RICE style care, a brace, crutches, and no return to sport
  • Does not manipulate or stress the knee further

Communication

  • Introduces self and uses plain language
  • Explains the likely diagnosis and why sport must stop
  • Sets honest expectations about recovery time
  • Asks the patient to repeat back the plan

Professionalism

  • Washes hands and drapes appropriately
  • Respects patient goals while putting safety first
  • Documents clearly and states referral plan

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Right anterior cruciate ligament tear, likely with lateral meniscus involvement

Differential:

  • Meniscus tear
  • Medial collateral ligament sprain
  • Patellar dislocation with spontaneous reduction
  • Tibial plateau fracture

Red flags to catch:

  • Fast swelling within hours suggests bleeding into the joint
  • Unable to bear weight, so radiographs per Ottawa rules
  • Check pulses to rule out vascular injury with knee instability

Imaging or labs to order: Knee radiographs to rule out fracture, then MRI to confirm ligament and meniscus damage.

Referral or emergency: Refer to orthopedics or sports medicine within days. Send to the emergency department if the foot is cold, pale, or pulseless.

Model SOAP note

S: 19 year old male, right knee pain and swelling 2 days after a non contact twist playing soccer. Heard a pop, swelling within hours, giving way twice. No numbness. Takes ibuprofen. No allergies.

O: BP 118/72, HR 70. Effusion, warm joint, flexion 100 degrees, extension lacks 5. Lachman and anterior drawer positive, pivot shift positive. Ligament stress at 0 and 30 stable. Pulses 2 plus. Ottawa rules positive.

A: Probable right ACL tear, possible lateral meniscus injury. Rule out fracture.

P: Knee radiographs today, MRI and orthopedic referral. Brace, crutches, ice, elevation, no sports. Return precautions for cold or pale foot or calf swelling. Follow up in 1 week.

Common errors

  • Skipping Lachman and drawer tests because the knee is swollen
  • Forgetting to check distal pulses
  • Skipping radiographs even though Ottawa rules are positive
  • Telling the patient they can return to sport in two weeks
  • Doing repeated stress tests that further injure the knee

Encounter E08: Mid back pain after lifting a grandchild

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks the mechanism and force involved
  • Asks about night pain, weight loss, and cancer history
  • Asks about steroid use and its duration
  • Asks about falls, menopause, smoking, and family fracture history
  • Asks about bowel, bladder, and leg weakness
  • Asks about medications and allergies

Physical exam

  • Measures height and compares to past height
  • Inspects posture and kyphosis
  • Palpates the spine for point tenderness
  • Uses gentle spinal percussion rather than force
  • Performs thoracic range of motion within comfort
  • Does a full lower limb neurologic exam
  • Checks lungs and abdomen

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of thoracic vertebral compression fracture
  • Lists strain, metastatic disease, and myeloma as differentials
  • Orders thoracic and lumbar radiographs and a DEXA scan
  • Avoids spinal manipulation and heavy soft tissue work at the fracture
  • Refers to the primary care physician for osteoporosis treatment and workup

Communication

  • Uses calm language for a worried older patient
  • Explains why manipulation is not safe for her
  • Gives clear lifting and activity advice
  • Checks that she understands the referral

Professionalism

  • Helps patient safely on and off the table
  • Does not promise a cure
  • Documents the contraindication to manipulation

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Thoracic compression fracture at T10 from osteoporosis, made worse by long term steroid use

Differential:

  • Muscle strain
  • Metastatic bone disease
  • Multiple myeloma
  • Pathologic fracture from infection

Red flags to catch:

  • Age over 70
  • Long term corticosteroid use
  • Height loss and increased kyphosis
  • Minor trauma causing severe pain
  • Unexplained weight loss

Imaging or labs to order: Thoracic and lumbar radiographs, DEXA scan, and blood tests such as CBC, calcium, ESR, and vitamin D. Consider serum protein electrophoresis.

Referral or emergency: Same week referral to her physician for osteoporosis care and cancer screening. Emergency referral if leg weakness, numbness, or loss of bladder or bowel control appears.

Model SOAP note

S: 72 year old female, 5 days of sharp mid back pain that began when lifting a child. Long term prednisone, 3 inch height loss, mild weight loss, prior smoker. No leg weakness or bladder change.

O: BP 138/80. Marked kyphosis. Point tenderness and positive percussion at T10. Neurologic exam normal. Lungs clear, abdomen soft.

A: Probable T10 osteoporotic compression fracture. Rule out malignancy or myeloma.

P: No spinal manipulation. Thoracic and lumbar radiographs, DEXA, blood tests. Refer to primary physician this week. Lifting limits, walking, and bracing as advised. Return at once for leg weakness or bladder changes.

Common errors

  • Performing spinal manipulation on a high risk older patient
  • Treating as a simple muscle strain without radiographs
  • Forgetting to ask about steroid use
  • Missing the height loss and kyphosis
  • Not referring for bone density and cancer workup

Encounter E09: Spinning when rolling over in bed

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks what the dizziness feels like (spinning versus lightheaded)
  • Asks about triggers and how long each spell lasts
  • Asks about hearing loss, tinnitus, and ear fullness
  • Asks about stroke symptoms such as double vision, speech or facial changes
  • Asks about headache, neck pain, and neck trauma
  • Asks about recent illness and medications

Physical exam

  • Checks vitals and general appearance
  • Examines for resting nystagmus
  • Tests cranial nerves
  • Performs cerebellar tests and Romberg and gait
  • Performs Dix Hallpike on both sides
  • Performs head impulse test and supine roll test
  • Examines ears and does Weber and Rinne
  • Auscultates carotids

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of right posterior canal benign paroxysmal positional vertigo
  • Lists vestibular neuritis, Meniere disease, and central causes in the differential
  • Explains why this is not stroke and states stroke warning signs to watch
  • Performs or plans the Epley maneuver after ruling out central signs
  • Refers if signs are central or do not resolve in a few weeks

Communication

  • Reassures the patient without dismissing her fear
  • Explains the maneuver in plain steps
  • Warns that brief dizziness may recur
  • Gives fall prevention advice

Professionalism

  • Obtains consent before moving the head
  • Stays alert for nausea and has a bucket ready
  • Documents negative central findings

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Right posterior canal benign paroxysmal positional vertigo

Differential:

  • Vestibular neuritis
  • Meniere disease
  • Vertebrobasilar insufficiency or cerebellar stroke
  • Medication related dizziness

Red flags to catch:

  • New severe headache or neck pain
  • Double vision, slurred speech, or facial droop
  • Vertical or direction changing nystagmus
  • Inability to walk or truncal ataxia

Imaging or labs to order: None needed when the exam is typical. MRI if any central sign, or if the vertigo does not respond to repositioning.

Referral or emergency: Emergency referral for any stroke sign. Refer to ENT or neurology if the vertigo persists beyond 3 to 4 weeks or findings change.

Model SOAP note

S: 58 year old female, 10 days of spinning vertigo on rolling or looking up, each lasting under 1 minute. No hearing loss, headache, or stroke symptoms. Recent cold. Takes lisinopril and atorvastatin.

O: BP 128/78. Cranial nerves, cerebellar tests, Romberg, and gait normal. No resting nystagmus. Right Dix Hallpike: delayed torsional upbeating nystagmus 25 seconds, fatigues. Left negative. Head impulse normal.

A: Right posterior canal BPPV. Central cause unlikely.

P: Epley maneuver right today. Avoid lying flat for a short time and sleep slightly propped. Fall precautions. Recheck in 1 week. Go to the emergency department for double vision, speech trouble, weakness, or severe headache.

Common errors

  • Skipping the neurologic exam before positional testing
  • Doing cervical manipulation as the treatment for vertigo
  • Doing Dix Hallpike on one side only
  • Not asking about stroke warning signs
  • Doing the wrong direction for the Epley maneuver

Encounter E10: Leg pain when walking in the grocery store

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks what makes symptoms better or worse, including sitting and bending forward
  • Asks about walking distance and uphill versus downhill
  • Asks about bowel, bladder, and saddle numbness
  • Asks about diabetes control and foot symptoms
  • Asks about cold feet, skin changes, and cardiovascular risk
  • Asks about weight loss, fever, and cancer history
  • Asks about medications

Physical exam

  • Checks vitals and observes gait and posture
  • Inspects feet and skin
  • Palpates pulses in the legs and feet
  • Tests lumbar extension and flexion for symptom reproduction
  • Performs straight leg raise and Kemp test
  • Does full lower limb strength, reflex, and sensory testing
  • Tests vibration and monofilament
  • Examines the abdomen for a pulsatile mass

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of lumbar spinal stenosis with neurogenic claudication
  • Lists vascular claudication, diabetic peripheral neuropathy, and hip arthritis as differentials
  • Explains how pulses and relief by flexion separate stenosis from vascular claudication
  • Orders MRI of the lumbar spine and refers if weakness progresses
  • Plans flexion based exercise and weight loss, and avoids extension and high velocity thrust in the lumbar spine

Communication

  • Explains stenosis in simple words
  • Addresses his frustration about activity
  • Sets realistic goals
  • Reviews warning signs of cauda equina

Professionalism

  • Coordinates with his primary doctor about diabetes
  • Respects patient preferences about treatment
  • Documents the neurologic findings clearly

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Lumbar spinal stenosis with neurogenic claudication, with early diabetic neuropathy as a contributing finding

Differential:

  • Vascular claudication from peripheral artery disease
  • Diabetic peripheral neuropathy
  • Hip osteoarthritis
  • Lumbar disc herniation

Red flags to catch:

  • New bowel or bladder changes or saddle numbness
  • Progressive leg weakness
  • Absent pulses or cold feet suggesting vascular disease
  • Unexplained weight loss or fever

Imaging or labs to order: MRI lumbar spine. Consider ankle brachial index and nerve conduction testing if the picture is unclear. Check A1c and B12.

Referral or emergency: Surgical or spine specialist referral if weakness progresses or conservative care fails. Emergency care for cauda equina signs.

Model SOAP note

S: 67 year old male, 8 months of bilateral leg heaviness and tingling when walking, relieved by sitting or bending forward, better uphill. Type 2 diabetes. No bowel or bladder changes. Takes metformin, lisinopril, aspirin.

O: BP 144/86. Lumbar extension reproduces symptoms, flexion relieves. SLR negative. Right EHL 4 out of 5, ankle reflexes 1 plus, reduced vibration at toes. Pulses 2 plus, feet warm.

A: Lumbar spinal stenosis with neurogenic claudication. Mild diabetic neuropathy. Vascular claudication unlikely.

P: MRI lumbar spine. Flexion based exercise, walking program with cart or bike, weight loss. No lumbar extension thrust. Check A1c and B12 with primary doctor. Return for weakness or bladder changes.

Common errors

  • Calling it neuropathy and ignoring the positional pattern
  • Not checking leg pulses
  • Using high velocity lumbar extension techniques
  • Forgetting to screen for cauda equina signs
  • Not coordinating diabetes care

Encounter E11: Cough that will not go away

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset, timing, and night waking
  • Asks about triggers such as cold air, exercise, and allergens
  • Asks about sputum, blood, fever, and weight loss
  • Asks about heartburn and postnasal drip
  • Asks about smoking, family history of asthma, and eczema
  • Asks about medications including ibuprofen and any ACE inhibitor
  • Asks about travel and sick contacts

Physical exam

  • Checks vitals including SpO2 and respiratory rate
  • Inspects for clubbing, cyanosis, and nasal findings
  • Palpates for chest expansion
  • Percusses the chest
  • Auscultates all lung fields including forced expiration
  • Measures peak flow or requests spirometry
  • Examines throat and lymph nodes
  • Checks heart and legs

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of asthma
  • Lists GERD, postnasal drip, ACE inhibitor cough, and infection in the differential
  • Orders spirometry before and after bronchodilator and refers to her physician
  • Advises avoiding ibuprofen and triggers pending evaluation
  • Gives return precautions for shortness of breath

Communication

  • Uses clear, simple words about asthma
  • Acknowledges her frustration
  • Explains why she needs medical testing
  • Checks understanding of warning signs

Professionalism

  • Wears a mask or washes hands with a coughing patient
  • Does not claim chiropractic care treats asthma itself
  • Documents clearly and communicates the referral

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Asthma with chronic cough and chest tightness, possible NSAID sensitivity

Differential:

  • Gastroesophageal reflux
  • Upper airway cough syndrome
  • ACE inhibitor cough
  • Post infectious cough or pertussis
  • Early COPD

Red flags to catch:

  • Coughing blood
  • Weight loss or night sweats
  • Oxygen saturation under 94 percent
  • Severe shortness of breath or inability to speak in full sentences

Imaging or labs to order: Spirometry with bronchodilator response, chest radiograph if the cough persists or findings atypical.

Referral or emergency: Refer to primary care or pulmonology for spirometry and a controller or rescue inhaler plan. Send to emergency care for severe breathlessness, low saturation, or blue lips.

Model SOAP note

S: 34 year old female, 3 months of dry cough, worse at night and with cold air and exercise. Chest tightness, no fever or weight loss. Never smoked. Brother has asthma. Takes ibuprofen and gets more tightness after it.

O: BP 112/70, RR 18, SpO2 97 percent. End expiratory wheeze both bases, prolonged expiration. Peak flow 340, predicted 450. Pale swollen nasal mucosa. No clubbing, normal heart.

A: Probable asthma. NSAID sensitivity possible. Reflux or upper airway cough less likely.

P: Refer to physician for spirometry and inhaler plan. Avoid ibuprofen until reviewed. Trigger control. Return or call emergency for severe breathlessness.

Common errors

  • Treating a three month cough as a simple cold
  • Not listening during forced expiration
  • Missing the NSAID link to tightness
  • Failing to ask about coughing blood and weight loss
  • Offering spinal adjustments as a treatment for asthma

Encounter E12: Back pain that gets better leaning forward

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset, relation to meals, and pain character
  • Asks what positions or movements change the pain
  • Asks about nausea, vomiting, fever, and bowel changes
  • Asks about alcohol use and gallstone history
  • Asks about urinary symptoms and chest pain
  • Asks about previous similar episodes
  • Asks about medications and allergies

Physical exam

  • Checks full vitals and hydration
  • Observes position of comfort
  • Palpates spine and shows pain is not mechanical
  • Examines the abdomen with gentle palpation and listening first
  • Checks for guarding, rebound, and Murphy sign
  • Palpates for a pulsatile abdominal mass and femoral pulses
  • Checks costovertebral angle for kidney involvement
  • Performs a brief neurologic screen

Diagnosis and case management

  • States working diagnosis of acute pancreatitis
  • Lists cholecystitis, perforated ulcer, aortic aneurysm, and kidney stone in the differential
  • Recognizes this is not a mechanical back problem and does no spinal manipulation
  • Sends the patient to the emergency department today for lipase and imaging
  • Tells the patient not to eat or drink and arranges safe transport

Communication

  • Stays calm and explains the urgency clearly
  • Asks about alcohol without judgment
  • Explains why chiropractic treatment is not appropriate today
  • Confirms the patient has a ride and understands

Professionalism

  • Calls the emergency department with a short handoff
  • Documents vitals and the reason for referral
  • Does not delay care to try treatment first

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Acute pancreatitis presenting as mid back pain, likely alcohol or gallstone related

Differential:

  • Acute cholecystitis or biliary colic
  • Perforated peptic ulcer
  • Abdominal aortic aneurysm
  • Kidney stone
  • Myocardial infarction presenting in the upper abdomen

Red flags to catch:

  • Pain unchanged by spinal movement
  • Pain relieved by leaning forward and worse lying flat
  • Fever and fast heart rate
  • Repeated vomiting and abdominal guarding
  • Alcohol use and gallstone history

Imaging or labs to order: Serum lipase and amylase, liver tests, CBC, and abdominal ultrasound or CT. Consider ECG if any chest symptoms.

Referral or emergency: Same day emergency department referral. Pancreatitis can lead to shock, so do not let him drive himself.

Model SOAP note

S: 49 year old male, 2 days of epigastric pain through to mid back, after heavy meal and alcohol. Vomiting, fever last night. Better leaning forward, worse lying flat. Daily beer use, known gallstones.

O: BP 146/92, HR 108, temp 100.4 F. Dry mucosa. Spinal motion and palpation do not change pain. Epigastric tenderness with guarding. No pulsatile mass. Femoral pulses equal. Neurologic screen normal.

A: Suspected acute pancreatitis. Biliary disease, ulcer, and aneurysm in the differential. Not a mechanical spine problem.

P: No spinal treatment. Same day emergency department by ambulance or driver for lipase and imaging. Nothing by mouth. Handoff called. Return to care after medical clearance.

Common errors

  • Treating with spinal manipulation because the pain is in the back
  • Not checking the abdomen
  • Missing that leaning forward helps and lying flat worsens the pain
  • Skipping vitals that show fever and fast heart rate
  • Sending the patient home to rest or drive himself

Encounter E13: Back pain with epigastric burning and dark stools on daily ibuprofen

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset and mechanism of the back pain
  • Asks about all medications including dose and frequency of NSAIDs
  • Asks about stomach pain, timing and relation to meals
  • Asks about stool color, vomiting blood and nausea
  • Asks about lightheadedness, fatigue and weight loss
  • Asks about alcohol and tobacco use
  • Screens for back pain red flags such as night pain, fever and bowel or bladder change

Physical exam

  • Measures vitals including orthostatic blood pressure and pulse
  • Checks conjunctival and skin pallor
  • Palpates the abdomen in all quadrants including epigastrium
  • Checks for guarding and rebound
  • Performs lumbar range of motion
  • Performs straight leg raise and a lower extremity neurologic screen
  • Palpates for pulsatile abdominal mass

Diagnosis and case management

  • Recognizes probable upper GI bleed from NSAID induced ulcer
  • Lists differential: gastritis, peptic ulcer, esophageal varices or cancer, mechanical low back pain
  • Does not treat with manipulation or advise continuing NSAIDs
  • Arranges same day emergency or urgent medical evaluation
  • Advises stopping ibuprofen until a physician decides
  • Documents the back pain as a secondary, likely mechanical problem to revisit

Communication

  • Explains in plain words that the stomach and black stools are the priority
  • Uses a calm tone and avoids scaring the patient
  • Confirms the patient understands why he is being sent today
  • Asks about transportation and tells him not to drive himself
  • Invites questions

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and washes hands
  • Drapes and positions the patient respectfully
  • Stays within scope and states the referral clearly
  • Speaks aloud what is being done for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Suspected NSAID induced peptic ulcer with upper gastrointestinal bleeding and early volume loss; coexisting mechanical low back pain

Differential:

  • Erosive gastritis
  • Duodenal or gastric ulcer
  • Esophageal varices from alcohol use
  • Gastric cancer

Red flags to catch:

  • Melena for one week
  • Orthostatic drop in blood pressure with rising heart rate
  • Heavy NSAID dose with alcohol use
  • Unintentional weight loss
  • Pallor and shortness of breath on exertion

Imaging or labs to order: Stat CBC and hemoglobin, stool occult blood, BUN and creatinine, then upper endoscopy by the receiving physician; H. pylori testing

Referral or emergency: Same day emergency department referral. Do not adjust. The orthostatic changes and melena suggest active blood loss that can worsen quickly.

Model SOAP note

S: 58 year old man, low back pain 3 months, dull, 4 of 10, no leg symptoms. Taking ibuprofen 2400 mg a day. Epigastric burning 4 weeks, wakes him at night. Black sticky stools 1 week, lightheaded on standing, 5 lb weight loss. Drinks 2 to 3 beers nightly, smokes half a pack daily.

O: Sitting BP 118/74 HR 96, standing BP 100/62 HR 112. Pale conjunctivae. Epigastric tenderness with mild guarding, no rebound. Lumbar flexion 60 degrees, SLR negative, neuro normal.

A: Suspected NSAID induced peptic ulcer with upper GI bleed and orthostatic changes. Differential gastritis, varices, gastric cancer. Mechanical low back pain, secondary.

P: No manipulation. Stop ibuprofen. Sent to emergency department today for CBC, stool testing and endoscopy. Patient told not to drive. Call hospital with report. Revisit back pain after medical clearance.

Common errors

  • Focusing on the back and adjusting the spine while ignoring the black stools
  • Never asking the total daily NSAID dose
  • Skipping orthostatic vitals
  • Telling the patient to switch to a different pain pill and come back next week
  • Referring to a routine primary care visit instead of same day emergency care

Encounter E14: Cramping and bloody diarrhea with joint and back stiffness in a young woman

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about back stiffness pattern, morning duration and response to movement
  • Asks about stool frequency, blood, mucus and night time bowel movements
  • Asks about weight loss, fever and appetite
  • Asks about eye, skin and mouth symptoms
  • Asks about family history of bowel or autoimmune disease
  • Asks about recent travel, antibiotics, diet and stress
  • Asks about NSAID use and its effect on the bowels

Physical exam

  • Measures vitals and weight
  • Inspects skin, eyes and mouth
  • Palpates the abdomen with attention to tenderness, guarding and masses
  • Performs lumbar range of motion and chest expansion
  • Performs sacroiliac provocation tests such as FABER, compression and Gaenslen
  • Performs straight leg raise and lower extremity neurologic screen

Diagnosis and case management

  • Recognizes probable inflammatory bowel disease with enteropathic spondyloarthritis
  • Differentiates from irritable bowel syndrome using blood, nocturnal symptoms, weight loss and extra intestinal signs
  • Lists differential: Crohn disease, ulcerative colitis, infectious colitis, celiac disease
  • Arranges same or next day medical assessment for labs, then gastroenterology for colonoscopy
  • Advises limiting NSAIDs, which can flare IBD
  • Defers high velocity manipulation of the spine and pelvis pending medical workup

Communication

  • Asks sensitive bowel questions privately and gently
  • Explains that this is a medical condition and not caused by stress
  • Uses plain language for the report of findings
  • Checks that the patient agrees with the referral plan

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and washes hands
  • Protects privacy and drapes appropriately
  • Stays within scope and coordinates care
  • Speaks aloud what is being done for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Probable inflammatory bowel disease (Crohn disease or ulcerative colitis) with enteropathic spondyloarthritis, uveitis and erythema nodosum

Differential:

  • Irritable bowel syndrome
  • Infectious colitis
  • Celiac disease
  • Ankylosing spondylitis without bowel disease

Red flags to catch:

  • Blood and mucus in the stool
  • Night time diarrhea
  • Weight loss
  • Inflammatory back pain pattern in a young adult
  • Red eye and erythema nodosum

Imaging or labs to order: CBC, CRP, ESR, albumin, fecal calprotectin, stool studies, then colonoscopy; HLA B27 and sacroiliac imaging as the medical team sees fit

Referral or emergency: Same day or next day medical assessment (urgent care or emergency room) for CBC, CRP and stool studies, then gastroenterology; emergency if fever above 100.4 F, resting heart rate above 90, severe pain, rigid abdomen or heavier bleeding. Co management of the spine is possible after diagnosis.

Model SOAP note

S: 26 year old woman, 2 months of low back and buttock stiffness, worst for an hour in the morning, better with movement. 4 months of 6 to 8 loose stools daily with blood, mucus and night episodes. 10 lb weight loss, evening low fevers, red left eye last month, tender shin bump. Uncle has Crohn disease. Uses ibuprofen.

O: Temp 99.1 F, BMI low. Mild pallor, mouth ulcers, tender red nodule right shin, left eye mildly injected. Left lower quadrant tenderness, no guarding. FABER and compression positive, Gaenslen positive left. Chest expansion 2 cm. Neuro normal.

A: Probable inflammatory bowel disease with enteropathic arthritis and extra intestinal signs. Differential IBS, infectious colitis, celiac disease.

P: Same or next day medical assessment for labs, then gastroenterology for colonoscopy. Limit NSAIDs. No spinal or pelvic manipulation until diagnosed. Explained findings. Return of fever or heavy bleeding means emergency care.

Common errors

  • Labeling the problem irritable bowel syndrome despite blood and weight loss
  • Adjusting the sacroiliac joints on a patient with active inflammatory disease
  • Missing the eye and shin findings because only the spine was examined
  • Recommending more ibuprofen for the back stiffness
  • Asking bowel questions in a rushed or embarrassing way and getting no real answers

Encounter E15: Burning feet and numbness in a woman with type 2 diabetes

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset, progression and location of foot symptoms
  • Asks about the quality and night pattern of the pain
  • Asks about the foot sore, how long and any drainage
  • Asks about diabetes history, A1c and home glucose readings
  • Asks about thirst, urination and vision changes
  • Asks about eye exams, kidney testing and cardiovascular history
  • Asks about smoking, alcohol and medication use

Physical exam

  • Measures blood pressure, pulse and weight
  • Inspects both feet including between the toes and plantar surface
  • Palpates dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses
  • Tests monofilament and vibration sensation
  • Tests ankle and knee reflexes
  • Tests pinprick in a stocking distribution
  • Checks capillary refill and skin temperature
  • Measures the ulcer size and notes drainage

Diagnosis and case management

  • Recognizes probable diabetic peripheral neuropathy with poor glycemic control
  • Recognizes the plantar neuropathic ulcer as urgent and a risk for infection or osteomyelitis
  • Recognizes signs of peripheral arterial disease
  • Orders or recommends A1c, fasting glucose, kidney function and urinalysis
  • Refers to primary care or endocrinology and to wound or podiatry care promptly
  • Advises daily foot checks, proper footwear and off loading the ulcer

Communication

  • Uses a nonjudgmental tone about diabetes control
  • Explains in plain words why the foot sore is serious
  • Teaches foot care steps simply
  • Confirms the patient will make the appointments

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and washes hands
  • Handles the foot with gloves and care
  • Stays within scope and coordinates with the medical team
  • Speaks aloud what is being done for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Diabetic distal symmetric polyneuropathy with a neuropathic plantar ulcer and probable peripheral arterial disease, poorly controlled type 2 diabetes

Differential:

  • Vitamin B12 deficiency from long term metformin
  • Lumbar spinal stenosis
  • Alcoholic or toxic neuropathy
  • Hypothyroid neuropathy

Red flags to catch:

  • Non healing plantar ulcer for five weeks
  • Absent monofilament sensation
  • Diminished pulses and slow capillary refill
  • Hyperglycemia symptoms with an A1c of 9.2
  • Overdue eye exam

Imaging or labs to order: A1c, fasting glucose, B12 level, kidney function, urinalysis for albumin, foot x ray to look for osteomyelitis, ankle brachial index

Referral or emergency: Urgent medical or wound care assessment within 24 to 48 hours with ABI and foot x ray, because absent or weak pulses suggest ischemia on top of neuropathy. Same day emergency care if the ulcer shows redness, swelling, pus, fever or odor. Not suitable for spinal treatment of the foot symptoms.

Model SOAP note

S: 62 year old woman, type 2 diabetes 11 years. Burning, numb feet 2 years, worse at night. Plantar sore on right foot 5 weeks with mild drainage. A1c 9.2 a year ago. Thirst, frequent urination, blurry vision. No eye exam in 4 years. On metformin and atorvastatin. Former smoker.

O: BP 144/88, BMI 38. Dry hairless shins, cool feet, DP pulses diminished, cap refill 4 seconds. 1 cm right plantar ulcer at first metatarsal head, shallow, clear drainage, no erythema. Monofilament and vibration absent, ankle reflexes absent, stocking pinprick loss.

A: Diabetic peripheral neuropathy with neuropathic ulcer, probable peripheral arterial disease, poorly controlled type 2 diabetes. Differential B12 deficiency, stenosis.

P: Urgent referral to primary care for A1c, B12, kidney tests, foot x ray and ABI; wound care or podiatry assessment within 24 to 48 hours. Off load foot, daily foot checks, proper shoes. Eye exam. Emergency care if redness, pus or fever.

Common errors

  • Treating the lumbar spine and calling the foot symptoms a pinched nerve
  • Not removing the shoes and socks to inspect the foot
  • Testing light touch only and skipping monofilament and vibration
  • Overlooking pulses and circulation
  • Not asking about the last A1c, eye exam or kidney testing

Encounter E16: Neck and shoulder pain with fatigue and cold intolerance

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset and pattern of neck and shoulder pain
  • Asks about fatigue, sleep and daytime sleepiness
  • Asks about weight change, cold intolerance and skin and hair changes
  • Asks about bowel habits and menstrual changes
  • Asks about mood, memory and concentration
  • Asks about family history of thyroid disease
  • Asks about neck fullness, swallowing and hand symptoms

Physical exam

  • Measures vitals including pulse rate and temperature
  • Inspects face, skin and hair
  • Palpates the thyroid and cervical nodes
  • Performs cervical range of motion and Spurling test
  • Tests deep tendon reflexes and notes relaxation phase
  • Performs Phalen and Tinel for carpal tunnel
  • Checks for peripheral edema

Diagnosis and case management

  • Recognizes probable primary hypothyroidism likely Hashimoto thyroiditis
  • Links the carpal tunnel signs and muscle aches to hypothyroidism
  • Lists differential: depression, anemia, sleep apnea, fibromyalgia
  • Orders or recommends TSH, free T4 and thyroid antibodies
  • Co manages: refers to primary care for medical treatment, may provide soft tissue care for the neck after workup
  • Gives return precautions for chest pain, confusion or severe slowing

Communication

  • Validates that the fatigue is real and not just stress
  • Explains thyroid function in plain words
  • Avoids promising a cure with chiropractic care
  • Checks understanding of the lab and referral plan

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and washes hands
  • Maintains respectful draping for neck and shoulder exam
  • Stays within scope and communicates with the physician
  • Speaks aloud what is being done for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Probable primary hypothyroidism (Hashimoto thyroiditis) with secondary myalgia and bilateral carpal tunnel symptoms

Differential:

  • Depression
  • Iron deficiency anemia
  • Obstructive sleep apnea
  • Fibromyalgia

Red flags to catch:

  • Pulse of 56 with low temperature
  • Delayed relaxation of reflexes
  • Diffuse firm goiter
  • Heavy periods with weight gain
  • Early signs of myxedema such as puffy face and leg swelling

Imaging or labs to order: TSH, free T4, thyroid peroxidase antibodies, CBC, lipid panel, vitamin D and B12; thyroid ultrasound if a nodule appears

Referral or emergency: Non emergency referral to primary care within a week or two for thyroid replacement. Emergency care if confusion, hypothermia, very slow pulse or breathing trouble appear. Spinal care for the neck only after thyroid status is clarified.

Model SOAP note

S: 41 year old woman, 4 months neck and shoulder ache, 5 of 10, with hand tingling and cramps. Fatigue 6 months, 18 lb weight gain, cold intolerance, constipation, dry skin, hair loss, heavy periods, low mood and fog. Mother and sister have thyroid disease. Neck feels full.

O: HR 56, temp 97.1 F, BMI 30. Puffy face, dry skin, thinning eyebrows. Diffusely enlarged firm nontender thyroid, no nodule. Cervical rotation 60 degrees, Spurling negative. Phalen and Tinel positive both wrists. Achilles reflex delayed relaxation. Mild leg edema.

A: Probable primary hypothyroidism, likely Hashimoto, with myalgia and carpal tunnel symptoms. Differential depression, anemia, sleep apnea, fibromyalgia.

P: Refer to primary care for TSH, free T4, antibodies and CBC within one to two weeks. Hold neck manipulation until thyroid status is known; gentle soft tissue care allowed. Return or emergency care for confusion, chest pain or breathing trouble.

Common errors

  • Treating only the neck and attributing fatigue to stress
  • Never palpating the thyroid or checking reflex relaxation
  • Overlooking the heart rate and temperature in the vitals
  • Stopping at carpal tunnel and not asking why it appeared
  • Promising that adjustments will fix the fatigue

Encounter E17: Low back pain in a man with a large waist, high blood pressure and high sugar

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset, mechanism and pattern of the low back pain
  • Asks about night pain, weight loss and neurologic symptoms
  • Asks about diet, activity and sleep
  • Asks about snoring and daytime sleepiness
  • Asks about thirst, urination and slow healing
  • Asks about chest pain and shortness of breath
  • Asks about family history of heart disease and diabetes
  • Asks about tobacco and alcohol use

Physical exam

  • Measures blood pressure in both arms and repeats it
  • Measures height, weight, BMI and waist circumference
  • Inspects skin for acanthosis nigricans
  • Auscultates heart and lungs
  • Performs lumbar range of motion
  • Performs straight leg raise and a lower extremity neurologic screen
  • Checks for abdominal mass and pedal pulses

Diagnosis and case management

  • Recognizes metabolic syndrome with stage 2 hypertension and probable type 2 diabetes
  • Notes probable obstructive sleep apnea and high cardiovascular risk
  • Identifies mechanical low back pain as the presenting problem
  • Orders or recommends fasting glucose, A1c, lipid panel and a sleep study
  • Refers to primary care within days for blood pressure and diabetes care
  • Provides lifestyle counseling and a gradual activity plan and may treat the back as co management after referral

Communication

  • Uses a respectful, non shaming tone about weight
  • Connects the findings to his family history in plain words
  • Gives clear, small first steps for diet and activity
  • Confirms he will see a physician soon

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and washes hands
  • Uses an appropriately sized cuff and gown
  • Stays within scope and coordinates care
  • Speaks aloud what is being done for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Metabolic syndrome with stage 2 hypertension, probable undiagnosed type 2 diabetes and likely obstructive sleep apnea; mechanical low back pain

Differential:

  • Isolated mechanical low back pain
  • Hypothyroidism
  • Cushing syndrome
  • Nonalcoholic fatty liver disease

Red flags to catch:

  • Blood pressure 150 over 95 on repeat
  • Acanthosis nigricans suggesting insulin resistance
  • Polyuria and polydipsia
  • Witnessed apneas and sleepiness while driving
  • Smoker with early family heart disease

Imaging or labs to order: Fasting glucose, A1c, lipid panel, kidney function, liver enzymes, TSH, ECG; sleep study; lumbar x ray not needed without red flags

Referral or emergency: Non emergency referral to primary care within a few days for blood pressure and diabetes evaluation. Emergency care if chest pain, severe headache, neurologic deficit or very high blood pressure symptoms develop. Spinal care is appropriate for mechanical back pain once the medical plan is in motion.

Model SOAP note

S: 49 year old male truck driver, low back ache for 1 year, 3 to 4 of 10, worse after sitting, no leg symptoms. Loud snoring with witnessed pauses and daytime sleepiness. Thirst, night urination, slow healing. Father had heart attack at 54 and diabetes. Smokes a pack a day, poor diet, no exercise.

O: BP 152/96 and 150/94 on repeat, BMI 37, waist 46 inches, neck 18 inches. Acanthosis nigricans. Lumbar flexion 55 degrees, SLR negative, neuro normal. Heart and lungs normal.

A: Metabolic syndrome with stage 2 hypertension, probable type 2 diabetes and probable sleep apnea. Mechanical low back pain.

P: Refer to primary care within days for glucose, A1c, lipids and BP care, and for a sleep study. Smoking cessation and diet advice. Gradual walking plan. Back care with soft tissue work and exercise. Emergency care for chest pain or severe headache.

Common errors

  • Treating the back and ignoring blood pressure and sleep symptoms
  • Taking blood pressure once with a cuff that is too small
  • Not asking about snoring in a truck driver
  • Shaming the patient about weight so he disengages
  • Telling the patient the blood pressure is fine because he feels well

Encounter E18: Colicky flank pain with blood in the urine in a middle aged man

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset, location and quality of the pain
  • Asks whether position or movement changes the pain
  • Asks whether the pain travels to the groin or testicle
  • Asks about blood in the urine and urinary changes
  • Asks about fever, chills, nausea and vomiting
  • Asks about past kidney stones and family history
  • Asks about fluids, diet and medicines

Physical exam

  • Measures blood pressure, pulse and temperature
  • Percusses both costovertebral angles
  • Palpates the abdomen and McBurney point
  • Checks lumbar range of motion and notes pain does not change
  • Performs straight leg raise and Kemp test
  • Performs a lower leg neurologic screen

Diagnosis and case management

  • Recognizes probable kidney stone with renal colic
  • Lists differential: back strain, appendicitis, aortic aneurysm, pyelonephritis, testicular torsion
  • Does not manipulate the back
  • Refers the same day for urinalysis and imaging
  • Sends to the emergency department if pain is uncontrolled, vomiting continues, or fever appears
  • Gives return precautions

Communication

  • Explains in plain words that this looks like a kidney stone
  • Kindly corrects the thrown out back theory
  • Checks the patient has a ride and should not drive
  • Invites questions

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and washes hands
  • Drapes and positions respectfully
  • Stays within scope and states the referral clearly
  • Speaks aloud what is being done for the video

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Right renal colic from a kidney stone

Differential:

  • Mechanical low back strain
  • Appendicitis
  • Acute pyelonephritis
  • Abdominal aortic aneurysm or testicular torsion

Red flags to catch:

  • Pain in waves with no position of relief
  • Blood in the urine
  • Pain traveling from flank to groin
  • Positive kidney punch
  • Fever or vomiting that would signal infection or blockage

Imaging or labs to order: Urinalysis, blood work for kidney function, and a non contrast CT or ultrasound to find the stone

Referral or emergency: Same day medical care for tests and pain control. Go to the emergency department now if pain cannot be controlled, vomiting continues, fever appears, or urine stops. No spinal manipulation.

Model SOAP note

S: 41 year old man, sudden right flank pain 4 hours, spreading to the groin, comes in waves up to 9 of 10, no position relief. Pink urine twice, nausea, vomited once. No fever. Father had stones. Low fluid intake. Moved boxes two days ago without pain afterward. Takes ibuprofen, no allergies. Some right testicular ache without swelling.

O: BP 148/90, HR 96, temp 98.6 F. Restless, sweating. Right costovertebral angle tender and kidney punch positive. No rebound. Lumbar motion full and painless. SLR and Kemp negative. Neuro normal. Testicles normal.

A: Probable right renal colic from a kidney stone. Rule out back strain, appendicitis, pyelonephritis, aneurysm. Findings fit stone pain because the pain is colicky, unrelated to movement, and paired with blood in the urine.

P: No manipulation. Same day referral to the emergency department for urinalysis, imaging and pain control. Told not to drive, to drink fluids, and to go sooner if fever or worse vomiting. Call receiving physician. Resume care after clearance. Advised ibuprofen only as the physician directs.

Common errors

  • Accepting the thrown out back story and adjusting the spine
  • Not asking about blood in the urine
  • Missing the kidney punch test
  • Skipping the abdominal and testicular exam
  • Sending the patient home with heat and rest

Encounter E19: Flank pain with burning urination

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about urinary burning, frequency and urgency
  • Asks about fever, chills and nausea or vomiting
  • Asks about blood in the urine
  • Asks about past urinary infections
  • Asks whether movement or position changes the pain
  • Asks about pregnancy possibility and last period
  • Asks about medications and allergies

Physical exam

  • Checks vital signs including temperature
  • Performs kidney punch percussion at the costovertebral angles
  • Palpates the abdomen and suprapubic area
  • Performs Kemp and straight leg raise
  • Tests lower limb neurologic screen
  • Observes posture and spinal motion
  • Washes hands and drapes appropriately

Diagnosis and case management

  • Names pyelonephritis (kidney infection) as the working diagnosis
  • Lists a kidney stone, muscle strain, appendicitis and ovarian problem as the differential
  • Recognizes fever plus positive kidney punch as a red flag, not a spinal problem
  • Orders or requests urinalysis and urine culture, and a pregnancy test
  • Sends the patient for same day medical care and does not manipulate
  • Gives clear instructions on fluids and when to go to the emergency room

Communication

  • Uses plain words for kidney infection
  • Shows empathy for her discomfort
  • Explains why an adjustment is not safe today
  • Checks that she understands the plan and asks if she has questions

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and confirms her name
  • Respects privacy and modesty during the exam
  • Stays calm and organized
  • Documents the referral and follow up plan

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Acute pyelonephritis, right side

Differential:

  • Kidney stone (ureterolithiasis)
  • Lumbar muscle strain
  • Appendicitis or pelvic inflammatory disease
  • Lower urinary tract infection without kidney involvement

Red flags to catch:

  • Fever and chills
  • Positive costovertebral angle tenderness
  • Vomiting and tachycardia
  • Pain unchanged by movement

Imaging or labs to order: Urinalysis with microscopy, urine culture, CBC and basic metabolic panel by the medical provider, urine pregnancy test. Imaging only if a stone or abscess is suspected.

Referral or emergency: Same day emergency department referral because of fever, tachycardia, vomiting and possible urosepsis; urgent care only if she is well enough to drink and keep medication down. No spinal manipulation.

Model SOAP note

S: 34 year old female with 3 days of right low back and flank pain, 7 of 10, constant, not affected by movement. Burning urination, frequency and cloudy urine for 5 days. Chills, one vomiting episode. Two prior UTIs. Sulfa allergy.

O: Temp 101.2 F, HR 104, BP 112/70. Looks ill. Kidney punch positive right, negative left. Mild suprapubic tenderness. Lumbar motion full, Kemp and SLR negative. Lower limb neuro normal. Abdomen soft, no rebound.

A: Probable acute pyelonephritis, right. Not mechanical low back pain. Differential: kidney stone, appendicitis, pelvic infection. Fever and positive kidney punch make a spinal cause unlikely.

P: No manipulation. Same day ER referral for urinalysis, culture, bloodwork and antibiotics. Push fluids. Go to ER now if vomiting worsens, fever above 103, confusion or faintness. Call office tomorrow. Resume spinal care once the infection is treated. Told her to bring her medication list and keep taking fluids by sipping often.

Common errors

  • Adjusting the back because the pain is in the low back
  • Skipping the kidney punch test
  • Ignoring temperature and heart rate
  • Sending the patient home with only advice to drink water
  • Forgetting to ask about urinary symptoms because the chart says back pain

Encounter E20: Back pain in a man with kidney disease

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about onset, mechanism and activity changes
  • Asks about urination changes, foamy urine and nighttime voiding
  • Asks about swelling, fatigue, itching and appetite
  • Asks about all medications including over the counter pain relievers
  • Asks how long and how much ibuprofen is taken
  • Asks about diabetes control and last kidney tests
  • Screens for fever, weight loss and night pain

Physical exam

  • Takes blood pressure and notes it is high
  • Checks for edema
  • Palpates the spine and paraspinal muscles
  • Performs lumbar range of motion
  • Performs Kemp, SLR and FABER
  • Does lower limb neurologic and monofilament screen
  • Percusses the costovertebral angles

Diagnosis and case management

  • Names mechanical low back strain as the likely pain source
  • Recognizes probable chronic kidney disease with diabetic and NSAID contribution
  • Lists disc problem, spinal stenosis and bone disease from kidney failure as differentials
  • Recommends stopping high dose NSAIDs and discussing alternatives with his doctor
  • Requests blood work: creatinine, eGFR, urine albumin to creatinine ratio, electrolytes
  • Provides gentle care for the back but refers for medical kidney evaluation

Communication

  • Explains kidney risk of ibuprofen without blame
  • Uses plain words for kidney function
  • Gives clear written plan
  • Confirms understanding and asks about concerns

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and addresses him respectfully
  • Stays within scope when discussing medications
  • Sends a note to his primary doctor
  • Documents findings and referral clearly

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Mechanical lumbar strain with probable chronic kidney disease, worsened by NSAID overuse

Differential:

  • Lumbar facet syndrome
  • Lumbar disc degeneration
  • Renal osteodystrophy (bone disease from kidney failure)
  • Diabetic neuropathy

Red flags to catch:

  • Foamy urine and edema
  • Uncontrolled blood pressure
  • Ibuprofen 2400 mg daily with kidney disease
  • Fatigue, itching and metallic taste suggesting uremia

Imaging or labs to order: Creatinine, eGFR, urine albumin to creatinine ratio, potassium, CBC, A1c by the medical doctor. Lumbar radiographs only if pain persists or red flags change.

Referral or emergency: Prompt (within days) referral to the primary care doctor or nephrologist for kidney function and blood pressure. Not an emergency unless confusion, chest pain or very little urine appears. Gentle chiropractic care can continue as shared care.

Model SOAP note

S: 58 year old male with 2 months of dull bilateral low back pain, 5 of 10, worse with bending. Diabetes, high blood pressure, known weak kidneys. Foamy urine, ankle swelling, fatigue, itching. Takes ibuprofen 2400 mg daily for 6 weeks.

O: BP 164/98. 2 plus pitting edema both ankles. Paraspinal tenderness L3 to L5, no midline tenderness, no CVA tenderness. Flexion limited to 50 degrees. Kemp positive right local pain. SLR negative. Strength and reflexes normal. Reduced monofilament at great toes.

A: Mechanical lumbar strain. Probable chronic kidney disease with possible NSAID injury and poorly controlled hypertension. Differential: disc degeneration, renal bone disease.

P: Stop high dose ibuprofen and discuss pain options with his doctor. Referral to primary doctor this week for creatinine, eGFR, urine albumin and blood pressure control. Gentle soft tissue work, no high velocity thrust until labs return. His doctor to review metformin and lisinopril dosing and potassium given kidney function and NSAIDs. Home walking and stretching. Return in one week. ER for little urine, confusion or chest pain.

Common errors

  • Treating the back only and ignoring the swelling and foamy urine
  • Not asking how much ibuprofen he takes
  • Failing to take or comment on the blood pressure
  • Telling him to stop all medicines on your own
  • Performing a strong thrust adjustment without considering possible bone weakness

Encounter E21: Night back pain after prostate cancer

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about cancer history, treatment and follow up
  • Asks about night pain and pain at rest
  • Asks about unexplained weight loss and appetite
  • Asks about leg weakness, numbness and gait changes
  • Asks about bowel and bladder function
  • Asks about fever, cough and other pain sites
  • Asks about trauma and previous spine problems

Physical exam

  • Checks vitals and weight
  • Percusses spinous processes
  • Performs rib spring and Valsalva cautiously
  • Does full lower limb strength and reflex testing
  • Tests for Babinski and clonus
  • Observes gait
  • Avoids thrust techniques during examination

Diagnosis and case management

  • Names metastatic spinal lesion from prostate cancer as the working diagnosis
  • Recognizes upper motor neuron signs as a sign of cord threat
  • Lists compression fracture, myeloma, infection and muscle strain as differentials
  • Does not manipulate the spine
  • Arranges urgent imaging and oncology contact, same day
  • Explains emergency warning signs of cord compression

Communication

  • Asks openly about his fears
  • Explains the concern in plain gentle words
  • Does not promise a diagnosis before tests
  • Confirms he will go for urgent evaluation today

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and respects his dignity
  • Coordinates with his oncologist or primary doctor
  • Documents the red flags and the referral
  • Declines to treat and explains why

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Suspected spinal metastasis at T9 from prostate cancer with early spinal cord involvement

Differential:

  • Osteoporotic compression fracture
  • Multiple myeloma
  • Spinal infection (osteomyelitis)
  • Thoracic muscle strain

Red flags to catch:

  • History of cancer
  • Night pain and rest pain
  • Unexplained weight loss
  • Hyperreflexia and positive Babinski

Imaging or labs to order: Urgent MRI of the whole spine with contrast. PSA, alkaline phosphatase, calcium, CBC. Bone scan or PET as directed by oncology.

Referral or emergency: Same day emergency evaluation or direct oncology contact because upper motor neuron signs suggest impending cord compression. No spinal manipulation.

Model SOAP note

S: 67 year old male, prostate cancer treated 4 years ago, missed PSA follow up. 6 weeks of mid back pain, 6 of 10 and 8 of 10 at night, unrelieved by rest. 17 pound weight loss. Leg heaviness on stairs. No bowel or bladder change.

O: Weight 168 lb. Afebrile. Percussion tenderness T9. Rib spring and Valsalva painful locally. Hip flexion 4 plus out of 5, patellar reflexes 3 plus, left Babinski positive. Gait cautious. Sensation intact.

A: Suspected metastatic lesion at T9 with early cord involvement. Differential: osteoporotic fracture, myeloma, infection.

P: No manipulation. Same day referral to the emergency department for urgent MRI of the spine and oncology consult. Called his oncologist and sent exam notes. Explained warning signs: new leg weakness, numbness, loss of bladder or bowel control, go to ER at once. Patient agrees to leave now with his wife driving.

Common errors

  • Treating a thoracic strain without asking about cancer history
  • Missing the reflex and Babinski findings
  • Performing thoracic manipulation or a drop table thrust
  • Ordering plain radiographs and waiting days for results
  • Not taking the weight loss seriously

Encounter E22: Shoulder and arm pain in a longtime smoker

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about smoking history in pack years
  • Asks about cough, blood in sputum and shortness of breath
  • Asks about weight loss and appetite
  • Asks about night pain and pain unrelated to movement
  • Asks about hand weakness and finger numbness
  • Asks about eye or facial changes and hoarseness
  • Asks about prior chest imaging

Physical exam

  • Checks vitals and respiratory rate
  • Inspects the eyes and pupils
  • Auscultates the lungs
  • Palpates supraclavicular fossae
  • Tests shoulder with rotator cuff tests
  • Tests C5 to T1 myotomes, dermatomes and reflexes
  • Checks hands for muscle wasting and clubbing

Diagnosis and case management

  • Names a Pancoast (lung apex) tumor with Horner syndrome as the working diagnosis
  • Explains why the shoulder tests being negative argues against a shoulder cause
  • Lists rotator cuff, cervical radiculopathy, thoracic outlet syndrome and brachial plexus injury as differentials
  • Orders chest radiograph and urgent CT, as part of referral
  • Refers urgently to a medical doctor or pulmonologist, not chiropractic care
  • Gives clear return and emergency instructions

Communication

  • Explains suspicion without causing panic
  • Uses plain words for lung tumor and Horner syndrome
  • Acknowledges her concern and asks what she understands
  • Confirms she will seek care today

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and confirms her history
  • Stays within scope and does not diagnose cancer as certain
  • Contacts her doctor and documents the plan
  • Respects her decision and follows up

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Suspected Pancoast tumor (lung apex cancer) with Horner syndrome and lower brachial plexus involvement

Differential:

  • Rotator cuff tendinopathy
  • C8 or T1 cervical radiculopathy
  • Thoracic outlet syndrome
  • Ulnar neuropathy

Red flags to catch:

  • Heavy smoking history
  • Weight loss and hemoptysis
  • Horner syndrome: ptosis, small pupil, less sweating
  • Shoulder pain unaffected by movement and worse at night

Imaging or labs to order: Chest radiograph and CT of the chest with contrast, then MRI of the brachial plexus and apex; biopsy by the specialist.

Referral or emergency: Same day referral for chest radiograph and CT with a pulmonologist; emergency room if hemoptysis or breathing worsens. No neck or thoracic manipulation.

Model SOAP note

S: 62 year old female, 40 pack year smoker. 3 months right shoulder and medial arm pain, 7 of 10, worse at night, not affected by movement. Tingling in ring and small fingers, weak grip. Cough 4 months, one episode of blood. 10 pound weight loss. Daughter noted droopy right eyelid.

O: Right ptosis and miosis, decreased facial sweating. Hoarse voice. Supraclavicular fullness. Shoulder range full, rotator cuff tests negative, Spurling negative. Grip 4 out of 5, weak finger abduction, ulnar sensory loss. Reduced breath sounds right apex. Clubbing.

A: Suspected Pancoast tumor with Horner syndrome and C8 T1 plexus involvement. Not a shoulder or disc problem.

P: No manipulation. Urgent referral to medical doctor today for chest radiograph and CT. Contacted her doctor. Stop naproxen only per doctor. Go to ER for trouble breathing, heavy coughing of blood or new weakness. Smoking cessation resources given.

Common errors

  • Treating the shoulder as a rotator cuff problem despite negative tests
  • Missing the droopy eyelid and small pupil
  • Not asking about smoking and cough
  • Adjusting the neck or upper thoracic spine
  • Ordering only a shoulder radiograph

Encounter E23: Leg pain with walking

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks how far he can walk before pain starts
  • Asks what relieves the pain and how fast
  • Asks whether bending forward or sitting is needed for relief
  • Asks about foot color, temperature and sores
  • Asks about smoking, diabetes and cholesterol
  • Asks about back pain and numbness
  • Asks about bowel and bladder function

Physical exam

  • Takes blood pressure in both arms
  • Palpates femoral, popliteal, dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses
  • Inspects skin, hair and nails
  • Checks capillary refill and temperature
  • Performs SLR and lumbar extension tests
  • Performs Buerger test or elevation test
  • Completes a lower limb neurologic screen

Diagnosis and case management

  • Names peripheral arterial disease with intermittent claudication as the working diagnosis
  • Notes the toe ulcer as a sign of critical limb ischemia
  • Distinguishes vascular claudication from neurogenic claudication
  • Orders ankle brachial index and arterial duplex, through referral
  • Refers urgently to a vascular specialist or medical doctor
  • Counsels on stopping smoking and walking program after medical clearance

Communication

  • Explains artery blockage in plain words
  • Handles his smoking without scolding
  • Explains why this is not a pinched nerve
  • Checks that he agrees to see a doctor within 1 to 2 days

Professionalism

  • Introduces self and respects his preferences
  • Does not treat the spine for a vascular cause
  • Sends information to his primary doctor
  • Documents findings and referral

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Peripheral arterial disease of the right leg with claudication and early critical limb ischemia

Differential:

  • Neurogenic claudication from lumbar stenosis
  • Chronic venous insufficiency
  • Diabetic or other neuropathy
  • Popliteal artery entrapment

Red flags to catch:

  • Nonhealing toe ulcer
  • Absent pedal pulses
  • Cold, pale shiny skin
  • Femoral bruit

Imaging or labs to order: Ankle brachial index, arterial duplex ultrasound, possibly CT angiography; lipid panel and A1c by medical doctor.

Referral or emergency: Urgent vascular assessment within 24 to 48 hours because of the ulcer (same day if rest pain, color change or numbness); emergency room right away if the foot turns cold, blue or numb. No manipulation for leg pain.

Model SOAP note

S: 66 year old male, 45 pack year smoker, high cholesterol. 8 months of right calf cramping at about two blocks, relieved by standing 2 to 3 minutes. Cold right foot. Toe sore for 3 weeks. No back pain or numbness.

O: BP 158/92 right, 152/90 left. Right foot cool, shiny, hairless. 8 mm ulcer right great toe. Right DP and PT pulses absent, left weak. Right femoral bruit. Capillary refill 5 seconds. SLR, Kemp negative. Neuro intact. Buerger positive.

A: Peripheral arterial disease with claudication and probable critical limb ischemia. Not neurogenic claudication, since standing relieves it and spine tests are negative.

P: No manipulation. Urgent referral to vascular specialist within 48 hours for ABI and duplex. Sent note to his doctor. Do not walk on the ulcer, keep foot clean and dry. Stop smoking, resources given. ER now if foot turns blue, cold, numb or painful at rest.

Common errors

  • Calling it sciatica or a pinched nerve
  • Not checking pedal pulses
  • Missing the toe ulcer and cold foot
  • Failing to separate vascular from neurogenic claudication
  • Starting a walking program before a medical workup

Encounter E24: Teen with a curve in her back

Checklist by domain

History

  • Asks about pain location, severity and timing
  • Asks about night pain or pain that wakes her
  • Asks about numbness, weakness and bowel or bladder changes
  • Asks about growth spurt and menstrual status
  • Asks about family history of scoliosis
  • Asks about when the asymmetry was first noticed
  • Asks about sports and backpack use

Physical exam

  • Observes posture from behind, front and side
  • Performs Adams forward bend test
  • Uses scoliometer or measures rib hump
  • Checks leg lengths and pelvic level
  • Tests strength, reflexes and sensation
  • Checks abdominal reflexes and skin for spinal markers
  • Draws a plumb line or measures shoulder height

Diagnosis and case management

  • Names adolescent idiopathic scoliosis as the working diagnosis
  • Lists leg length difference, congenital curve, neuromuscular cause and tumor as differentials
  • Explains that painless or mild pain curves are most often idiopathic but pain requires a closer look
  • Orders standing full spine radiographs with Cobb angle
  • Plans shared care and referral to pediatric orthopedics because she is still growing
  • Plans monitoring every 4 to 6 months and exercise advice

Communication

  • Speaks to the teen directly as well as her mother
  • Explains scoliosis in simple reassuring terms
  • Avoids frightening words such as surgery as a first topic
  • Offers a chaperone and checks she is comfortable

Professionalism

  • Uses gown or drape, and a chaperone for a minor
  • Gets parent consent
  • Documents measurements carefully
  • Coordinates with her pediatrician

Diagnosis and management

Working diagnosis: Adolescent idiopathic scoliosis, right thoracic curve, skeletally immature

Differential:

  • Functional scoliosis from leg length difference
  • Congenital scoliosis
  • Neuromuscular scoliosis
  • Osteoid osteoma or spinal tumor causing painful scoliosis

Red flags to catch:

  • Pain at night or constant pain
  • Left thoracic curve
  • Abnormal neurologic or abdominal reflexes
  • Rapid progression, skin markers

Imaging or labs to order: Standing PA full spine radiograph with Cobb angle and Risser sign, with shielding. MRI only if neurologic signs or atypical features appear.

Referral or emergency: Referral to pediatric orthopedics because curve may be above 20 degrees in a premenarchal growing child. Not an emergency. Chiropractic exercise and monitoring only as shared care, no thrust to correct the curve.

Model SOAP note

S: 13 year old female, premenarchal, grew 3 inches this year. Shoulder asymmetry noticed by mother 2 months ago. Mild thoracic ache after sports, 2 of 10, no night pain. No neuro symptoms. Aunt had scoliosis.

O: Right shoulder and scapula high. Adams test: right thoracic rib hump, 8 degrees scoliometer. Leg lengths equal. No skin markers. Neuro and abdominal reflexes normal. SLR negative.

A: Adolescent idiopathic scoliosis, right thoracic, skeletally immature. Differential: leg length cause, congenital curve, tumor. Right thoracic curve with pain this mild fits the usual pattern.

P: Standing full spine radiographs with Cobb angle and Risser. Referral to pediatric orthopedics because she is still growing and the curve may need bracing. Measure again every 4 to 6 months. Core and posture exercises. No high velocity thrust. Return sooner for night pain, numbness or fast change. Parent given written plan. Mother and patient verbalized understanding and a chaperone was present throughout.

Common errors

  • Skipping the Adams forward bend test
  • Ignoring the growth stage and menstrual status
  • Promising a chiropractic adjustment will fix the curve
  • Missing the need to refer a growing child
  • Examining a minor without a chaperone or parent consent

Technique Station Answer Key

T01 (Cervical): Set up a supine rotational adjustment for a right C5 restriction.

Patient position: Supine on the table with the head resting in the doctor's hands, shoulders level.

Doctor position and stance: Seated or standing at the head of the table, facing the patient, feet apart and balanced.

Segment or listing: C5 restricted on the right, listed as right rotation.

Contact point: Segmental contact on the right C5 articular pillar. Doctor contact on the lateral surface of the proximal phalanx of the left index finger.

Line of drive: Short quick thrust with a small rotary component and slight lateral flexion along the plane of the facet, with little extension.

Setup steps
